Slopes of modular forms and geometry of eigencurves Thanks: R. Liu and L. Xiao are partially supported by the National Natural Science Foundation of China under agreement No. NSFC–12321001. In addition, R. Liu is partially supported by the National Natural Science Foundation of China under agreement No. NSFC–11725101, and by the New Cornerstone Foundation. L. Xiao is partially supported by the National Natural Science Foundation of China under agreement NSFC–12071004 and NSFC–12231001, by the New Cornerstone Foundation, and NSF grant DMS–1502147 and DMS–1752703. B. Zhao is partially supported by AMS-Simons Travel Grant.
Ruochuan Liu, Nha Xuan Truong, Liang Xiao, Bin Zhao

Abstract
. Under a strong genericity condition, we prove the local analogue of the ghost conjecture of Bergdall and Pollack. As applications, we deduce in this case (a) a folklore conjecture of Breuil–Buzzard–Emerton on the crystalline slopes of Kisin’s crystabelline deformation spaces, (b) Gouvêa’s ⌊ k − 1 p + 1 ⌋ \lfloor\frac{k-1}{p+1}\rfloor -conjecture on slopes of modular forms, and (c) the finiteness of irreducible components of the eigencurves. In addition, applying combinatorial arguments by Bergdall and Pollack, and by Ren, we deduce as corollaries in the reducible and very generic case, (d) Gouvêa–Mazur conjecture, (e) a variant of Gouvêa’s conjecture on slope distributions, and (f) a refined version of Coleman–Mazur–Buzzard–Kilford spectral halo conjecture.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
11F33 (primary), 11F85
(secondary).

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Questions of slopes of modular forms

Let
p
be an odd prime number and let
N
be a positive integer relatively prime to
p
. The central object of this paper is the
U_{p}
-slopes
, that is, the
p
-adic valuations of the eigenvalues of the
U_{p}
-operator acting on the space of (overconvergent) modular forms of level
\Gamma_{0}(Np)
, or on more general space of overconvergent automorphic forms essentially of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-type. In this paper, the
p
-adic valuation is normalized so that
v_{p}(p)=1
.

The general study of slopes of modular forms dates back to the 1990’s, when Gouvêa and Mazur made several profound and intriguing conjectures on these slopes, based on extensive numerical computations. These conjectures were later extended and refined by Buzzard, Calegari, and many other mathematicians; see
[
Bu05
,
BC04
,
Cl05
,
Lo07
]
;
certain very special cases were also proved based on either the coincidence that a certain modular curve has genus
0
(e.g.
[
BC04
]
), or the still computationally manageable
p
-adic local Langlands correspondence when the slopes are small (e.g.
[
BuGe

13

,
BhGh15
,
GG15
,
BGR18
,
Ar21
]
).
Unfortunately, despite strong numerical evidences, little theoretic progress was made towards these conjectures in the general case.

In recent breakthrough work of Bergdall and Pollack
[
BP19a
,
BP19b
,
BP

22

]
, they unified all historically important conjectures regarding slopes into one conjecture: the
ghost conjecture
, which roughly gives a combinatorially defined “toy model”, called the
ghost series
, of the characteristic power series of the
U_{p}
-action on the space of overconvergent modular forms.
The purpose of this work and its prequel
[
LTXZ

24

]
is to prove this ghost conjecture and place it within the framework of
p
-adic local Langlands conjecture. We now state our main theorem followed by a discussion on all of its corollaries, and then conclude the introduction with a short overview of the proof.

1.2.
Statement of main theorems

We fix an odd prime number
p\geq 5
and an isomorphism
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}\simeq\mathbb{C}
. Let
E
be a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
with ring of integers
\mathcal{O}
and residue field
\mathbb{F}
.
Let

\bar{r}:\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\GL_{2}(\mathbb{F})
be an absolutely irreducible representation.
Let
\mathrm{S}_{k}(\Gamma_{0}(Np);\psi)_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{r}}}\subseteq\mathrm{S}^{\dagger}_{k}(\Gamma_{0}(Np);\psi)_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{r}}}
denote the space of classical and overconvergent modular forms of weight
k
, level
\Gamma_{0}(Np)
, and nebentypus character
\psi
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
, localized at the Hecke maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{r}}
corresponding to
\bar{r}
, respectively. (Our convention is that the cyclotomic character has Hodge–Tate weight
-1
, and the Galois representations associated to weight
k
modular forms is homological, and has Hodge–Tate weights
\{1-k,0\}
. This is the dual to the Galois representation as appeared in
[
Em11
]
; see §
1.28
for more discussion on our choices of convention.)

It is a theorem of Coleman and Kisin that
\mathrm{S}_{k}(\Gamma_{0}(Np);\psi)_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{r}}}
is “almost” the subspace of
\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\dagger}(\Gamma_{0}(Np);\psi)_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{r}}}
spanned by
U_{p}
-eigenforms with slopes
\leq k-1
(the forms of slope
k-1
is a bit tricky and we do not discuss them in this introduction; see Proposition
2.12
(1)). Thus, to understand the slopes of the
U_{p}
-action on
\mathrm{S}_{k}(\Gamma_{0}(Np);\psi)_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{r}}}
, it suffices to understand the slopes of the Newton polygon of the characteristic power series of the
U_{p}
-action on
\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\dagger}(\Gamma_{0}(Np);\psi)_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{r}}}
.

It is a theorem of Coleman that one may interpolate the characteristic power series of the
U_{p}
-actions on spaces of overconvergent modular forms of all weights
k
, as follows. For
\bar{\alpha}\in\mathbb{F}^{\times}
, write
\mathrm{unr}(\bar{\alpha}):\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\to\mathbb{F}^{\times}
for the unramified character sending the geometric Frobenius to
\bar{\alpha}
.
Let

\omega_{1}:\mathrm{I}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\twoheadrightarrow\Gal(\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{p})/\mathbb{Q}_{p})\cong\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
denote the
first fundamental character
of the inertia subgroup
\mathrm{I}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
at
p
; so

\mathrm{det}(\bar{r}|_{\mathrm{I}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}})=\omega_{1}^{c}
for some
c\in\{0,\dots,p-2\}
.
Write
\omega:\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}\to\mathcal{O}^{\times}
for the Teichmüller character, and put
w_{k}:=\exp(p(k-2))-1
for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Then there exists a power series
C_{\bar{r}}(w,t)\in\mathcal{O}\llbracket w,t\rrbracket
such that

C_{\bar{r}}(w_{k},t)=\mathrm{det}\big(\mathrm{I}_{\infty}-U_{p}t;\;\mathrm{S}^{\dagger}_{k}(\Gamma_{0}(Np);\omega^{k-1-c})_{\bar{r}}\big)

holds for all
k\geq 2
.

The ghost conjecture aims, under a condition we specify later, to find a “toy model” power series
G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(w,t)
that has the same Newton polygon as
C_{\bar{r}}(w,t)
for every evaluation of
w
, but only depends on the restriction
\bar{r}|_{\mathrm{I}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
.
Here and later, for a power series
C(t):=1+c_{1}t+c_{2}t^{2}+\cdots\in\mathcal{O}\llbracket t\rrbracket
, the Newton polygon
\NP(C(t))
is the lower convex hull of the points
(n,v_{p}(c_{n}))
for all
n
. In particular, the slopes of
\NP(C_{\bar{r}}(w_{k},-))
are precisely the slopes of
U_{p}
-action on
\mathrm{S}^{\dagger}_{k}(\Gamma_{0}(Np);\omega^{k-1-c})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{r}}}
.

The
key requirement
for the ghost conjecture is that
\bar{r}_{p}:=\bar{r}|_{\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
is
reducible and generic
, namely
c\equiv a+2b+1\bmod(p-1)
for some
a\in\{1,\dots,p-4\}
and
b\in\{0,\dots,p-2\}
, and

•

(reducible split case)
\bar{r}_{p}\simeq\mathrm{unr}(\bar{\alpha})\omega_{1}^{a+b+1}\oplus\mathrm{unr}(\bar{\beta})\omega_{1}^{a}
for some
\bar{\alpha},\bar{\beta}\in\mathbb{F}^{\times}
, or

•

(reducible nonsplit case) either
\bar{r}_{p}\simeq{\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{unr}(\bar{\alpha})\omega_{1}^{a+b+1}&*\neq 0\\
0&\mathrm{unr}(\bar{\beta})\omega_{1}^{b}\end{pmatrix}}
for some
\bar{\alpha},\bar{\beta}\in\mathbb{F}^{\times}
(where the nontrivial extension
*\neq 0
is unique up to isomorphism given the genericity condition on
a
).

We say that
\bar{r}_{p}
is
very generic
if
a\in\{2,\dots,p-5\}
.

We remark that the reducibility and genericity of
\bar{r}_{p}
are necessary for the ghost conjecture to be valid, and refer to
[
BP

22

]
for theoretical explanations and concrete examples.

We need one more technical input to state our theorem (which we give a working definition): there exists an integer
m(\bar{r})
such that

\dim\mathrm{S}_{k}(\Gamma_{0}(Np);\omega^{k-1-c})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{r}}}-\frac{2k}{p-1}m(\bar{r})\textrm{ is bounded as }k\to\infty.

Such
m(\bar{r})
always exists. We give precise dimension formulas in Definition-Proposition
2.13
.

For our reducible and generic
\bar{r}_{p}
above, the (right)
\mathbb{F}
-representation
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\sigma_{a,b}:=\Sym^{a}\mathbb{F}^{\oplus 2}\otimes\mathrm{det}^{b}
of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
is always a Serre weight for
\bar{r}_{p}
(see §
1.28
for our convention on Serre weights). We defined in
[
LTXZ

24

]
a power series
G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(w,t)=\sum\limits_{n\geq 0}g_{n}(w)t^{n}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}[w]\llbracket t\rrbracket
analogous to the ghost series in
[
BP19a
]
. (In
loc. cit.
it was denoted by
G_{\bar{r}_{p}|_{\mathrm{I}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}}(w,t)
but
G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(w,t)
is a more appropriate notation; see Remark
2.3
(1).) We will recall its definition in Definition
1.5
below.

Our main result is the following. It was essentially conjectured by Bergdall and Pollack
[
BP19a
,
BP19b
]
(and is slightly adapted in the prequel
[
LTXZ

24

]
of this series).

Theorem 1.3

(Ghost conjecture)
.

Assume
p\geq 11
and that
\bar{r}:\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\GL_{2}(\mathbb{F})
is an absolutely irreducible representation such that
\bar{r}_{p}
is reducible and very generic (i.e.
2\leq a\leq p-5
). Then for every
w_{\star}\in\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{C}_{p}}
,
the Newton polygon
\NP\big(C_{\bar{r}}(w_{\star},-)\big)
is the same as the Newton polygon
\NP\big(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(w_{\star},-)\big)
, stretched in both
x
- and
y
-directions by
m(\bar{r})
times, except possibly for the their slope zero parts.

Remark 1.4
.

(1)

We have complete results for the slope zero part; see Theorem
8.7
for details. In fact, our Theorem
8.7
is a much more general statement for the space of automorphic forms of general
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-type.

(2)

It is conjectured that Theorem
1.3
holds for
a=1
and
a=p-4
, and for smaller primes
p
. We explain the technical difficulties later in Remarks
2.9
and
5.15
. On the other hand, the irreducibility assumption on
\bar{r}_{p}
is crucial for the ghost conjecture to hold. See Remark
1.8
(2) below.

(3)

In Remark
8.8
, we also explain how one might extend Theorem
1.3
to the case when the global representation
\bar{r}
is reducible. The only difference is some additional dimension computation.

We quickly recall the definition of ghost series
G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(w,t)=1+\sum\limits_{n\geq 1}g_{n}(w)t^{n}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}[w]\llbracket t\rrbracket
; see Definition
2.5
and the following discussion for examples and formulas.

Definition 1.5
.

Take
\bar{r}^{\prime}:\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\GL_{2}(\mathbb{F})
that is absolutely irreducible and that
\bar{r}^{\prime}|_{\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
is reducible nonsplit and generic.
For each
k\equiv a+2b+2\bmod(p-1)
and
k\geq 2
, define

d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}:=\tfrac{1}{m(\bar{r}^{\prime})}{\dim\mathrm{S}_{k}\big(\Gamma_{0}(N)\big)_{\bar{r}^{\prime}}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}:=\tfrac{1}{m(\bar{r}^{\prime})}{\dim\mathrm{S}_{k}\big(\Gamma_{0}(Np)\big)_{\bar{r}^{\prime}}}.

Then we have

g_{n}(w)=\prod_{k\equiv a+2b+2\bmod(p-1)}(w-w_{k})^{m_{n}(k)},

where the exponents
m_{n}(k)
are given by the following recipe

m_{n}(k)=\begin{cases}\min\big\{n-d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}},d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}-d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}-n\big\}&\textrm{ if }d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}<n<d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}-d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}\\
0&\textrm{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Put

G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(w,t):=1+\sum_{n\geq 1}g_{n}(w)t^{n}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}[w]\llbracket t\rrbracket.

We point out that the ghost series
G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(w,t)
depends only on the Serre weight
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
, or equivalently
p
,
a
, and
b
;
it does not depend on
N
and the global representation
\bar{r}^{\prime}
. (See Definition
2.5
for a definition of
G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(w,t)
without reference to the dimensions of modular forms.)

A very primitive form of the ghost conjecture was first asked in
[
BC04
]
, which is only for the case when
p=2
and
N=1
. Later similar types of ghost series for other small primes were conjectured by
[
Cl05
,
Lo07
]
. The general form of the ghost series was first introduced by Bergdall and Pollack
[
BP19a
,
BP19b
]
.
We emphasize that the Bergdall and Pollack’s work is of crucial importance to this paper.

In
[
LTXZ

24

]
, we raised an analogous local ghost conjecture which starts with a completely abstract setting: set
\mathrm{K}_{p}=\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
; consider
a primitive
\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{K}_{p}\rrbracket
-projective augmented module associated to the Serre weight
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\Sym^{a}\mathbb{F}^{\oplus 2}\otimes\mathrm{det}^{b}
, that is, the projective envelope
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
as a right
\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{K}_{p}\rrbracket
-module, on which the
\mathrm{K}_{p}
-action extends to a continuous
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-action, satisfying certain appropriate conditions (that are naturally satisfied in the automorphic setup). From this, one can similarly define analogues of classical and overconvergent forms, and our main result of this paper is the following analogue of Theorem
1.3
in this abstract setup, which we call the
local ghost theorem
.

Theorem 1.6

(Local ghost theorem)
.

Assume that
p\geq 11
. Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\Sym^{a}\mathbb{F}^{\oplus 2}\otimes\mathrm{det}^{b}
be the Serre weight with
a\in\{2,\dots,p-5\}
and
b\in\{0,\dots,p-2\}
.
Let
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
be a primitive
\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{K}_{p}\rrbracket
-projective augmented module of type
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
, and let
\varepsilon
be a character of
(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times})^{2}
such that
\varepsilon(x,x)=x^{a+2b}
for every
x\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
.
Then for the characteristic power series
C^{(\varepsilon)}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}}(w,t)
of the
U_{p}
-action on overconvergent forms associated to
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
, we have,
for every
w_{\star}\in\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{C}_{p}}
,

\NP(G^{(\varepsilon)}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(w_{\star},-))=\NP(C_{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star},-)).

Comparing to Theorem
1.3
, we here allow characters on both
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-factors of the Iwahori group
\mathrm{Iw}_{p}={\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}&\mathbb{Z}_{p}\\
p\mathbb{Z}_{p}&\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}\end{smallmatrix}\big)}
.
We refer to Section
2
for more discussions on undefined notations.

The benefit of extending Theorem
1.3
to the purely local ghost Theorem
1.6
is that the latter works for the “universal”
\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{K}_{p}\rrbracket
-projective augmented module. More precisely, if
\bar{r}_{p}:\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\to\GL_{2}(\mathbb{F})
is a residual reducible nonsplit and generic representation, then Paškūnas in
[
Pa13
]
defined a certain projective envelope
\widetilde{P}
of
\pi(\bar{r}_{p})^{\vee}
in the category of Pontryagin dual of smooth admissible torsion representations of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, so that the endomorphism ring of
\widetilde{P}
is isomorphic to the deformation ring
R_{\bar{r}_{p}}
of
\bar{r}_{p}
.
It is proved by Hu and Paškūnas
[
HP19
]
that there exists an element
x
in the maximal ideal of
R_{\bar{r}_{p}}
such that for every
x_{\star}\in\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
for
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
the maximal ideal in some finite extension
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{O}
,
\widetilde{P}_{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}/(x-x_{\star})\widetilde{P}_{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}
is always a primitive
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\llbracket\mathrm{K}_{p}\rrbracket
-projective augmented module of type
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
. Thus Theorem
1.6
applies and gives the corresponding slopes for overconvergent forms constructed out of
\widetilde{P}_{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}/(x-x_{\star})\widetilde{P}_{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}
(which we call
abstract overconvergent forms
).

The key point here is that the Newton polygon of the characteristic power series of the
U_{p}
-actions on space of abstract overconvergent forms is
independent
of the value
x_{\star}
! Thus, as
x_{\star}
varies, we obtain results for the “universal case”.

Comparing this with the Galois side, we obtain immediately the list of slopes on the trianguline deformation space of
\bar{r}_{p}
à la Breuil–Hellmann–Schraen
[
BHS17
]
. (Moreover, we observe that this also provides the knowledge of the slopes for trianguline deformation space of
\bar{r}_{p}^{\mathrm{ss}}
, for free.) Finally, by a bootstrapping argument, our result implies the ghost conjecture for a general automorphic setup using global triangulation results such as
[
KPX14
,
Li15
]
, in particular Theorem
1.3
.

A discussion of the proof of Theorem
1.6
will be given later in §
1.26
.

Remark 1.7
.

We make several quick comments at the philosophical level on the proof.

(1)

It is essential to work over the entire weight space and harness the integrality of the characteristic power series over the weight ring
\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket
. The pattern of slopes of
G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{k},-)
can be very complicated and subtle; see for example the cited proof of Proposition
2.20
. The involved combinatorics seems to suggest: working over a single weight
k
to treat all slopes is going to be combinatorially extremely difficult.

(2)

The bootstrapping step makes use of essentially the full power of the known
p
-adic local Langlands correspondence for
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
(which might be downgraded to only assuming Breuil–Mézard conjecture for
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
). But the proof of Theorem
1.6
(in the primitive case) does not make use of the
p
-adic local Langlands correspondence.

Remark 1.8
.

We point to several possible extensions of Theorem
1.6
.

(1)

In addition to slopes of
\NP\big(C_{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{k},-)\big)
, we may ask, for each
U_{p}
-eigenvalue
\beta
, what
\beta/p^{v_{p}(\beta)}
modulo
\varpi
is. It seems to be possible that, if we know this for the
U_{p}
-action on the space of “modular forms” with weight
2
and character
\omega^{b}\times\omega^{a+b}
(which only depends on
\bar{r}_{p}
but not on the choice of
x_{\star}
in the discussion following Theorem
1.6
), then we may deduce this answer for all slopes of multiplicity one. Translating this to the Galois side, we conjecture that, when
\bar{r}_{p}
is reducible and generic, “most” irreducible components of every Kisin’s semistabelian deformation space has Breuil–Mézard multiplicity
1
. (We thank Bergdall and Ren for pointing out that it is plausible that some very special component might have higher multiplicities.)
In fact, Breuil–Mézard multiplicity one property can be proved in the crystabelline case with wild inertia type, in the forthcoming work of
[
AXZ

23
+

]
.

(2)

It is very natural to ask whether the method of this paper extends to the case when
\bar{r}_{p}
is irreducible, or even non-generic. Our most optimistic answer is “maybe” but only “partially”, but we have not carefully investigated this case. The key difference is that, when
\bar{r}_{p}
is irreducible and generic, the smallest slope at any classical point seems to depend on the automorphic data. However, some initial computation suggests that although
\NP(C_{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star},-))
can be complicated, if we only consider the convex hull of points whose horizontal coordinates are even integers, then there might be a hope of an analogue of ghost series.

(3)

In
[
Bu05
]
, Buzzard proposed an algorithm which is expected to produce slopes of modular forms inductively, at least under the
Buzzard-regular
condition. We will not include a discussion on this, but only point out that this has been numerically verified extensively in
[
BP19a
, Fact 3.1]
, and proved in a recent work of Eunsu Hur
[
Hu

24
+

]
.

The logical process and relations with various conjectures we address in this paper are summarized in the following diagram:
Local ghost conjecture
Paškūnas functor
Trianguline varieties
Global triangulation
Slopes on trianguline deformation spaces
Automorphic ghost conjecture

(a) Breuil–Buzzard–Emerton conjecture

(b) Gouvêa’s
\big\lfloor\frac{k-1}{p+1}\big\rfloor
-conjecture

(c) Irreducible components of eigencurves

(d) Gouvêa–Mazur conjecture

(e) Slope distribution conjecture

(f) Refined spectral halo conjecture

We now discuss these corollaries.

1.9.
Application A: Breuil–Buzzard–Emerton conjecture

Let
\bar{r}_{p}:\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\to\GL_{2}(\mathbb{F})
be a residual local Galois representation, and let
R_{\bar{r}_{p}}^{\square}
denote the framed deformation ring.
For
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
and a finite-image character
\underline{\psi}=\psi_{1}\times\psi_{2}:(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times})^{2}\to\mathcal{O}^{\times}
, Kisin
[
Kis08
]
defines a quotient of
R_{\bar{r}_{p}}^{\square,1-k,\underline{\psi}}
parameterizing lifts of
\bar{r}_{p}
that are potentially crystalline with Hodge–Tate weights
\{1-k,0\}
and initial type
\psi
.

For each homomorphism
x^{*}:R_{\bar{r}_{p}}^{\square,1-k,\underline{\psi}}\to E^{\prime}
with
E^{\prime}
a finite extension of
E
, let
\mathcal{V}_{x}
denote the deformation of
\bar{r}_{p}
at
x
. Then the
2
-dimensional space
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{pcrys}}(\mathcal{V}_{x})
carries
E^{\prime}
-linear commuting actions of
\Gal(\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{p^{\infty}})/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
and the crystalline Frobenius
\phi
(see Notation
7.1
for the definition of
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{pcrys}}(\mathcal{V}_{x})
).

The following
[
BuGe

16

, Conjecture 4.1.1]
was initially conjectured by Breuil, Buzzard, and Emerton in their personal correspondences around 2005.

Theorem 1.10

(Breuil–Buzzard–Emerton conjecture)
.

Assume that
p\geq 11
and that
\bar{r}_{p}
is reducible and very generic.
Let
k
,
\underline{\psi}
,
R_{\bar{r}_{p}}^{\square,1-k,\underline{\psi}}
, and
x^{*}
be as above.
Let
m
denote the minimal
positive
integer such that
\psi_{1}\psi_{2}^{-1}
is trivial on
(1+p^{m}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}
, and let

\alpha
be an eigenvalue of
\phi
acting on the subspace of
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{pcrys}}(\mathcal{V}_{x})
where
\Gal(\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{p^{\infty}})/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
acts through
\psi_{1}
. Then

v_{p}(\alpha)\in\begin{cases}\big(\frac{a}{2}+\mathbb{Z}\big)\cup\mathbb{Z}&\textrm{ when }m=1,\\
\frac{1}{(p-1)p^{m-1}}\mathbb{Z}&\textrm{ when }m\geq 2.\end{cases}

This is proved in Corollary
7.10
, in fact as a corollary of Theorem
7.6
which identifies all possible slopes on the trianguline deformation spaces with slopes of the Newton polygon of
G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w,t)
. The idea of the proof is essentially explained in the paragraph after Theorem
1.6
, namely, that applying Theorem
1.6
to the universal
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-representation defined by Paškūnas shows that the slopes of the crystalline Frobenius actions are exactly determined by the
U_{p}
-slopes on corresponding overconvergent forms, which is in turn equal to the slopes of
G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w,t)
. Now the integrality statement follows from a (not-at-all-trivial) property of ghost series
[
LTXZ

24

, Corollaries 4.14 and 5.24]
.

Remark 1.11
.

(1)

What is originally conjectured in
[
BuGe

16

, Corollary 4.1.1]
also includes non-generic cases, which our method cannot treat at the moment.

(2)

There have been several attempts
[
Br03
,
BuGe

13

,
BhGh15
,
GG15
,
BGR18
]
on various versions of this theorem, based on mod
p
local Langlands correspondence. In fact, their goals are much more ambitious: classify the reduction of all crystalline or crystabelline representations with slopes less than equal to a particular number, typically less than or equal to
3
.
In their range, their work even addresses non-generic cases that we cannot touch.
Our advantage is to be able to treat all possible slopes.

(3)

Analogous to Theorem
1.10
, Jiawei An
[
An

24
+

]
obtained some partial results towards the
p
-adic valuations of
\mathcal{L}
-invariants of semistable deformations of
\bar{r}_{p}
.

1.12.
Application B: Gouvêa’s
\big\lfloor\frac{k-1}{p+1}\big\rfloor
-conjecture

In 1990s, Gouvêa
[
Go01
, § 4]
numerically computed the
T_{p}
-slopes on
S_{k}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
as
k\to\infty
and found that almost always, the slopes are less than or equal to
\big\lfloor\frac{k-1}{p+1}\big\rfloor
.

Interpreting this using the framework of
p
-adic local Langlands correspondence, we should consider instead the
T_{p}
-slopes on
S_{k}(\Gamma_{0}(N))_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{r}}}
(or better, the lesser
U_{p}
-slopes on old forms in
S_{k}(\Gamma_{0}(pN))_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{r}}}
after
p
-stabilization) when localized at a residual Galois representation
\bar{r}
as in §
1.2
. If we assume further that
\bar{r}|_{\mathrm{I}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
is isomorphic to
\bar{r}_{p}
and
\bar{r}_{p}^{\mathrm{ss}}
as above, it is expected that the slopes are always less than or equal to
\big\lfloor\frac{k-1}{p+1}\big\rfloor
.

This conjecture also has its Galois theoretic counterpart, which seems more intrinsic. Roughly speaking, this folklore conjecture asserts that for any crystalline representation
V
of Hodge–Tate weights
\{0,k-1\}
, if
p
-adic valuation of the trace of the
\phi
-action on
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{crys}}(V)
is strictly larger than
\big\lfloor\frac{k-1}{p+1}\big\rfloor
, then
V
has an irreducible reduction.

Our following result partially answers the contrapositive statement.

Theorem 1.13

(Gouvêa’s
\big\lfloor\frac{k-1}{p+1}\big\rfloor
-conjecture)
.

Assume
p\geq 11
.
Let
\bar{r}_{p}
be a residual local Galois representation that is reducible and very generic (with
a\in\{2,\dots,p-5\}
).
Let

\underline{\psi}:(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times})^{2}\twoheadrightarrow\Delta^{2}\xrightarrow{\omega^{-s_{\varepsilon}}\times\omega^{-s_{\varepsilon}}}\mathcal{O}^{\times}

be a character with
s_{\varepsilon}\in\{0,\dots,p-2\}
, and fix
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
such that
k\equiv a+2s_{\varepsilon}\bmod(p-1)
.

Let
R_{\bar{r}_{p}}^{\square,1-k,\underline{\psi}}
denote the Kisin’s crystabelline deformation ring as in §
1.9
and let
x^{*}:R_{\bar{r}_{p}}^{\square,1-k,\underline{\psi}}\to E^{\prime}
be a continuous homomorphism. Then for the trace
a_{p,x}
of the
\phi
-action on
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{pcrys}}(\mathcal{V}_{x})
, we have

k-1+v_{p}(a_{p,x})\leq\Big\lfloor\frac{k-1-\min\{a+1,p-2-a\}}{p+1}\Big\rfloor.

This is proved in Corollary
7.10
.

Remark 1.14
.

(1)

The Galois-theoretic version of Gouvêa’s conjecture was proved with weaker bounds
\big\lfloor\frac{k-1}{p-1}\big\rfloor
by Berger–Li–Zhu
[
BLZ04
]
and bounds
\big\lfloor\frac{k-1}{p}\big\rfloor
by Bergdall–Levin
[
BL

22

]
. Both results essentially use tools from
p
-adic Hodge theory: the former one uses Wach modules and the latter one uses Kisin modules. Our proof “comes from the automorphic side”.

(2)

The estimate of the slopes of crystalline Frobenius
\phi
comes from the estimate of slopes of the ghost series, which turns out to involve a rather subtle inequality on sum of digits of certain number’s
p
-adic expansions. See
[
LTXZ

24

, Proposition 4.28]
for the non-formal part of the proof.

1.15.
Application C: Finiteness of irreducible components of eigencurves

Near the end of the introduction of the seminal paper
[
CM98
]
of Coleman and Mazur, they listed many far-reaching open questions, among them, one particularly intriguing question is whether the eigencurve has finitely many irreducible components, as somewhat “suggested” by that all non-Hida components have infinite degrees over the weight space
[
HN20
]
.
As far as we understand, almost nothing was known towards this question.
As a corollary of our main theorem, we provide positive theoretic evidence towards this question, namely, the eigencurve associated to
\bar{r}
that is reducible and very generic at
p
, has finitely many irreducible components.

Keep the notation as in Theorem
1.3
. Let
\mathcal{W}:=(\Spf\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket)^{\mathrm{rig}}
denote the rigid analytic weight open unit disk and let
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{rig}}
denote the rigid analytification of
\mathbb{G}_{m,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
. Let
\Spc(\bar{r})
denote the zero locus of
C_{\bar{r}}(w,t)
, as a rigid analytic subspace of
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{rig}}\times\mathcal{W}
; it carries a natural weight map
\mathrm{wt}
to
\mathcal{W}
. By Hida theory, this spectral curve is the disjoint union
\Spc(\bar{r})=\Spc(\bar{r})_{=0}\bigsqcup\Spc(\bar{r})_{>0}
, where
\Spc(\bar{r})_{=0}
(possibly empty) is the component with slope zero, corresponding to the Hida family. It is well known that
\Spc(\bar{r})_{=0}
is finite over
\mathcal{W}
, and hence has finitely many irreducible components.
We prove the following in Corollary
9.7
.

Theorem 1.16
.

Assume
p\geq 11
and that
\bar{r}:\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\GL_{2}(\mathbb{F})
is an absolutely irreducible representation such that
\bar{r}_{p}|_{\mathrm{I}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
is reducible and very generic.
Then
\Spc(\bar{r})_{>0}
has finitely many irreducible components. In fact, every irreducible component
\mathcal{Z}
of
\Spc(\bar{r})_{>0}
is the zero locus of a power series
C_{\mathcal{Z}}(w,t)\in\mathcal{O}\llbracket w,t\rrbracket
such that for every
w_{\star}\in\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{C}_{p}}
, the
\NP\big(C_{\mathcal{Z}}(w_{\star},-)\big)
is the same as
\NP\big(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(w_{\star},-)\big)
with the slope-zero part removed, and stretched in both
x
- and
y
-directions by some constant
m(\mathcal{Z})
.

In fact, what we prove is that, for every power series
C(w,t)
whose positive slopes agree with the ghost series (up to a fixed multiplicity), any irreducible factor of
C(w,t)
has the same property; see Theorem
9.6
and Corollary
9.7
.

1.17.
Application D: Gouvêa–Mazur conjecture

In the pioneer work of
Gouvêa and Mazur
[
GM92
]
, they investigated how slopes of (classical) modular forms vary when the weight
k
changes
p
-adically. Their extensive numerical data suggests that when the weights
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
are
p
-adically close, then the slopes of modular forms of weights
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
agree. More precisely, they made the following conjecture.

Conjecture 1.18

(Gouvêa–Mazur)
.

There is a function
M(n)
linear in
n
such that if
k_{1},k_{2}>2n+2
and

k_{1}\equiv k_{2}\bmod(p-1)p^{M(n)}
, then the sequences of
U_{p}
-slopes (with multiplicities) on
\mathrm{S}_{k_{1}}(\Gamma_{0}(Np))
and
\mathrm{S}_{k_{2}}(\Gamma_{0}(Np))
agree up to slope
n
.

Originally, Gouvêa and Mazur conjectured with
M(n)=n
, but Buzzard and Calegari
[
BC04
]
found explicit counterexamples. The current modified version Conjecture
1.18
is still expected by experts. The only proved result is with
M(n)
quadratic in
n
by Wan
[
Wa98
]
.

It is natural to consider this conjecture for each
\bar{r}
-localized subspaces
\mathrm{S}_{k}(\Gamma_{0}(Np))_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{r}}}
. Under the same hypothesis as above, combining Theorem
1.3
with a combinatorial result of ghost series by Ren
[
Re

24

]
, the following variant of Gouvêa–Mazur conjecture can be deduced (see Theorem
8.10
).

Theorem 1.19
.

Assume
p\geq 11
and that
\bar{r}:\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\GL_{2}(\mathbb{F})
is an absolutely irreducible representation such that
\bar{r}_{p}|_{\mathrm{I}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
is reducible and very generic. Let
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 4}
be an integer. Then for weights
k_{1},k_{2}>m-3
such that
v_{p}(k_{1}-k_{2})\geq m
, the sequence of
U_{p}
-slopes (with multiplicities) on
\mathrm{S}_{k_{1}}(\Gamma_{0}(Np);\omega^{k_{1}-a-2b-2})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{r}}}
and
\mathrm{S}_{k_{2}}(\Gamma_{0}(Np);\omega^{k_{2}-a-2b-2})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{r}}}
agree up to slope
m-4
.

1.20.
Application E: Gouvêa’s slope distribution conjecture

For slopes of modular forms, Gouvêa made extensive numerical computations. In his paper
[
Go01
]
, titled “Where the slopes are”, he made the following intriguing conjecture.

Conjecture 1.21
.

Fix a tame level
N
(relatively prime to
p
). For each
k
, write
\alpha_{1}(k),\dots,\alpha_{d}(k)
for the list of
U_{p}
-slopes on
\mathrm{S}_{k}(\Gamma_{0}(Np))
, and let
\mu_{k}
denote the uniform probability measure of the multiset
\{\frac{\alpha_{1}(k)}{k-1},\dots,\frac{\alpha_{d}(k)}{k-1}\}\subset[0,1]
. Then the measure
\mu_{k}
weakly converges to

(1.21.1)

\frac{1}{p+1}\delta_{[0,\frac{1}{p+1}]}+\frac{1}{p+1}\delta_{[\frac{p}{p+1},1]}+\frac{p-1}{p+1}\delta_{\frac{1}{2}},

where
\delta_{[a,b]}
denotes the uniform probability measure on the interval
[a,b]
, and
\delta_{\frac{1}{2}}
is the Dirac measure at
\frac{1}{2}
.

The symmetry between
\delta_{[0,\frac{1}{p+1}]}
and
\delta_{[0,\frac{1}{p+1}]}
follows from the usual
p
-stabilization process, namely the old form slopes can be paired so that the sum of each pair is
k-1
. The Dirac measure at
\frac{1}{2}
corresponds to the newform slopes, where the
U_{p}
-eigenvalues are
\pm p^{\frac{k-2}{2}}
.

In
[
BP19b
]
, the authors defined abstract ghost series and showed that the slopes of the Newton polygon of abstract ghost series satisfy analogue of Gouvêa’s distribution conjecture. So combining their work and Theorem
1.3
, we obtain the following (see Theorem
8.11
).

Theorem 1.22
.

Assume
p\geq 11
and that
\bar{r}:\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\GL_{2}(\mathbb{F})
is an absolutely irreducible representation such that
\bar{r}_{p}|_{\mathrm{I}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
is reducible and very generic as in Definition
2.2
. For
k\equiv a+2b+2\bmod(p-1)
, let
\alpha_{1}(k),\alpha_{2}(k),\dots
denote the
U_{p}
-slopes of
\mathrm{S}_{k}(\Gamma_{0}(Np))_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{r}}}
in increasing order, and let
\mu_{k}
denote the probability measure for the set
\{\frac{\alpha_{1}(k)}{k-1},\frac{\alpha_{2}(k)}{k-1},\dots\big\}
. Let
m(\bar{r})
be the mod-
p
-multiplicity defined in §
1.2
.
Then

(1)

Put
d_{k,\bar{r}}^{\mathrm{ur}}:=\dim\mathrm{S}_{k}(\Gamma_{0}(N))_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{r}}}
and
d_{k,\bar{r}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}:=\dim\mathrm{S}_{k}(\Gamma_{0}(pN))_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{r}}}
.
We have the following.

\alpha_{i}(k)=\begin{cases}\frac{p-1}{2m(\bar{r})}\cdot i+O(\log k)&\textrm{if }1\leq i\leq d_{k,\bar{r}}^{\mathrm{ur}}\\
\frac{k-2}{2}&\textrm{if }d_{k,\bar{r}}^{\mathrm{ur}}<i\leq d_{k,\bar{r}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}-d_{k,\bar{r}}^{\mathrm{ur}}\\
\frac{p-1}{2m(\bar{r})}\cdot i+O(\log k)&\textrm{if }d_{k,\bar{r}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}-d_{k,\bar{r}}^{\mathrm{ur}}<i\leq d_{k,\bar{r}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}.\end{cases}

(2)

As
k\to\infty
while keeping
k\equiv a+2b+2\bmod(p-1)
, the measure
\mu_{k}
weakly converges to the probability measure (
1.21.1
).

1.23.
Application F: refined Coleman–Mazur–Buzzard–Kilford spectral halo conjecture

In Coleman and Mazur’s foundational paper
[
CM98
]
on eigencurves, they raised an important conjecture on the behavior of the eigencurve near the boundary of weight disks: the eigencurve is an infinite disjoint union of annuli such that each irreducible component is finite and flat over the weight annulus; this was largely inspired by Emerton’s thesis
[
Em98
]
. The first proved result in this direction was by Buzzard and Kilford
[
BK05
]
, which is in the case
N=1
and
p=2
. Some additional examples when
p
is small were subsequently provided
[
Ja04
,
Kil08
,
KM12
,
Ro14
]
. The first result for more general situations was obtained by Wan, the first and the third authors in
[
LWX17
]
, which roughly is the following.

Theorem 1.24
.

Let
C_{D}(w,t)
denote the characteristic power series analogously defined as in §
1.2
but for automorphic forms on a definite quaternion algebra
D
over
\mathbb{Q}
that is split at
p
. Let
\Spc(D)
denote the zero locus of
C_{D}(w,t)
in
\mathcal{W}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{rig}}
, and

\mathcal{W}_{(0,1)}=\big\{w_{\star}\in\mathcal{W}\;\big|\;v_{p}(w_{\star})\in(0,1)\big\}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\Spc_{(0,1)}(D)=\Spc(D)\cap\mathrm{wt}^{-1}(\mathcal{W}_{(0,1)}).

Then
\Spc_{(0,1)}(D)
is an infinite disjoint union
X_{0}\bigsqcup X_{(0,1)}\bigsqcup X_{1}\bigsqcup X_{(1,2)}\bigsqcup\cdots
such that

(1)

for each point
(w_{\star},a_{p})\in X_{I}
with
I=n=[n,n]
or
(n,n+1)
, we have

v_{p}(a_{p})\in(p-1)\cdot v_{p}(w_{\star})\cdot I,

(2)

the weight map
\mathrm{wt}:X_{I}\to\mathcal{W}_{(0,1)}
is finite and flat.

This theorem was later generalized to the Hilbert case when
p
splits, by Johansson–Newton
[
JN19
]
, and Ren and the fourth author
[
RZ22
]
. The case corresponding to the modular forms, namely the “original Coleman–Mazur–Buzzard–Kilford” conjecture was established by Diao and Yao in
[
DiYa

23
+

]
.
Unfortunately, Theorem
1.24
and all these generalizations do not give further information on the slope ratios
v_{p}(a_{p})/v_{p}(w_{\star})
inside the open intervals
(p-1)\cdot(n,n+1)
. When
\bar{r}
satisfies the conditions of our ghost theorem, the slopes of ghost series automatically give the following refined version of the above theorem (see Theorem
8.12
).

Theorem 1.25
.

Assume
p\geq 11
and that
\bar{r}:\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\GL_{2}(\mathbb{F})
is an absolutely irreducible representation such that
\bar{r}_{p}|_{\mathrm{I}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
is reducible and very generic. Let
\Spc(\bar{r})
denote the zero locus of
C_{\bar{r}}(w,t)
inside
\mathcal{W}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{rig}}
, and put

\Spc(\bar{r})_{(0,1)}=\Spc(\bar{r})\cap\mathrm{wt}^{-1}(\mathcal{W}_{(0,1)})
. Then
\Spc(\bar{r})_{(0,1)}
is a disjoint union
Y_{1}\bigsqcup Y_{2}\bigsqcup\cdots
such that

(1)

for each point
(w_{\star},a_{p})\in Y_{n}
,
v_{p}(a_{p})=(\deg g_{n}-\deg g_{n-1})\cdot v_{p}(w_{\star})
, and

(2)

the weight map
\mathrm{wt}:Y_{n}\to\mathcal{W}_{(0,1)}
is finite and flat of degree
m(\bar{r})
.

A similar result can be stated when
\bar{r}
is split, we refer to Theorem
8.12
for the details.

1.26.
Overview of the proof of Theorem
1.6

We now explain the two main inputs in proving Theorem
1.6
.
Recall that
\mathrm{K}_{p}=\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
; we may reduce to the case when
b=0
. Theorem
1.6
involves the following local data: let
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
be the projective envelope of
\Sym^{a}\mathbb{F}^{\oplus 2}
as a right
\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{K}_{p}\rrbracket
-module, and we extend the
\mathrm{K}_{p}
-action to a continuous (right) action by
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
so that
{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p&0\\
0&p\end{smallmatrix}\big)}
acts trivially.
Then for each character
\psi
of
(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times})^{2}
and a character
\varepsilon_{1}
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
, we may define spaces of abstract classical and overconvergent forms

\displaystyle\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\psi)=\mathrm{S}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}},k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\psi)

\displaystyle\ :=\Hom_{\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{Iw}_{p}\rrbracket}\big(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}},\,\Sym^{k-2}\mathcal{O}^{\oplus 2}\otimes\psi\big),

\displaystyle\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})=\mathrm{S}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}},k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})

\displaystyle\ :=\Hom_{\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{K}_{p}\rrbracket}\big(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}},\,\Sym^{k-2}\mathcal{O}^{\oplus 2}\otimes\varepsilon_{1}\circ\mathrm{det}\big),

(1.26.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\dagger}(\psi)=\mathrm{S}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}},k}^{\dagger}(\psi)

\displaystyle\ :=\Hom_{\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{Iw}_{p}\rrbracket}\big(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}},\,\mathcal{O}\langle z\rangle\otimes\psi\big).

These abstract and overconvergent forms behave exactly as their automorphic counterparts, equipped with the corresponding
U_{p}
-operators,
T_{p}
-operators, Atkin–Lehner involutions, and theta maps. (See §
2.4
and Proposition
2.12
.)

Main input I:
p
-stabilization process
; see §
3.3
and Proposition
3.5
.
When
\psi=\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1}=\varepsilon_{1}\times\varepsilon_{1}
, the standard
p
-stabilization process can be summarized by the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{S}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}},k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{S}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}},k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{AL}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{proj}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{proj}_{2}}

Here the space
\mathrm{S}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}},k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})
carries a natural
T_{p}
-action and
\mathrm{S}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}},k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})
carries a
U_{p}
-action and an Atkin–Lehner involution. The maps
\iota_{1},\iota_{2},\mathrm{proj}_{1},\mathrm{proj}_{2}
are the natural ones.
Write
d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1}):=\rank_{\mathcal{O}}\mathrm{S}_{k,\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})
and
d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1}):=\rank_{\mathcal{O}}\mathrm{S}_{k,\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})
.
The key observation is the equality:

(1.26.2)

U_{p}(\varphi)=\iota_{2}(\mathrm{proj}_{1}(\varphi))-\mathrm{AL}(\varphi)\quad\textrm{for all }\varphi\in\mathrm{S}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}},k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1}).

Under the usual power basis, the matrix of
U_{p}
on
\mathrm{S}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}},k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})
is then decomposed as the sum of

•

a matrix with rank
\leq d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})\approx\frac{1}{p+1}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})
, and

•

an antidiagonal matrix for the Atkin–Lehner involution.

Essentially this observation alone already shows that the characteristic power series of the upper-left
n\times n
submatrix of the
U_{p}
-action on abstract overconvergent forms is divisible by the ghost series
g_{n}(w)
(but in a larger ring
\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle
); see Corollary
3.11
. Unfortunately, we need much more work to control the determinant of other minors of the matrix of
U_{p}
.

Main input II: halo estimate
(for center of the weight disk); see Lemma
3.15
(4) and the more refined version in Corollary
3.29
.

As a right
\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{Iw}_{p}\rrbracket
-module, we may write

\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}=e_{1}\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{Iw}_{p}\rrbracket\otimes_{\mathcal{O}[(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times})^{2}],1\otimes\omega^{a}}\mathcal{O}\oplus e_{2}\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{Iw}_{p}\rrbracket\otimes_{\mathcal{O}[(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times})^{2}],\omega^{a}\otimes 1}\mathcal{O}.

Thus, there is a natural power basis of
\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\dagger}(\psi)
of the form

e_{1}^{*}z^{s_{\psi,1}},\,e_{1}^{*}z^{s_{\psi,1}+p-1},\,e_{1}^{*}z^{s_{\psi,1}+2(p-1)},\,\dots,e_{2}^{*}z^{s_{\psi,2}},\,e_{2}^{*}z^{s_{\psi,2}+p-1},\,e_{2}^{*}z^{s_{\psi,2}+2(p-1)},\,\dots,

for some integers
s_{\psi,1},s_{\psi,2}\in\{0,\dots,p-2\}
to match the nebentypus character
\psi
; see §
2.11
for details. It is natural to consider the
U_{p}
-action with respect to this basis and the associated Hodge polygon. Some time between the two papers
[
WXZ17
]
and
[
LWX17
]
, the authors realized that this estimate is not sharp enough. One should use instead the so-called Mahler basis, or rather
the modified Mahler basis
, which means to replace the monomials above by the following polynomials:

f_{1}(z)=\frac{z^{p}-z}{p},\quad f_{\ell+1}(z)=\frac{f_{\ell}(z)^{p}-f_{\ell}(z)}{p}\quad\textrm{for }\ell\geq 1;

\textrm{for }n=n_{0}+pn_{1}+p^{2}n_{2}+\cdots,\quad\textrm{define }\mathbf{m}_{n}(z):=z^{n_{0}}f_{1}(z)^{n_{1}}f_{2}(z)^{n_{2}}\cdots.

Then
\{\mathbf{m}_{n}(z)\,|\,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}
form a basis of
\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathbb{Z}_{p};\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, the space of continuous functions on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. It turns out that the estimate of
U_{p}
-operator using this basis is slightly sharper than the estimate using the power basis. This improvement is the other key to our proof.

We make two remarks here: first, our modified Mahler basis is an approximation of the usual Mahler basis
\binom{z}{n}
; ours have the advantage that each basis element is an eigenform for the action of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
; second, compare to the estimate in
[
LWX17
]
, we also need to treat some “pathological cases”, e.g. coefficients when the degree is close to a large power of
p
. Such “distractions” complicate our proof a lot.

With the two main input I and II discussed, we now sketch the proof of Theorem
1.6
. A more detailed summary can be found at the beginning of Section
4
.

In a rough form, Theorem
1.6
says that
C_{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w,t)=1+\sum\limits_{n\geq 1}c_{n}(w)t^{n}
and
G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w,t)=1+\sum\limits_{n\geq 1}g_{n}(w)t^{n}
are “close” to each other. The leads us to the following.

Step I
:

(Lagrange interpolation) For each
n
, we formally apply Lagrange interpolation to
c_{n}(w)
relative to the zeros
w_{k}
of
g_{n}(w)
(with multiplicity):

(1.26.3)

c_{n}(w)=\sum_{m_{n}(k)\neq 0}A_{k}(w)\cdot\frac{g_{n}(w)}{(w-w_{k})^{m_{n}(k)}}+h(w)g_{n}(w).

We give a sufficient condition on the
p
-adic valuations of the coefficients of
A_{k}(w)
that would imply Theorem
1.6
. This is Proposition
4.4
.

In fact, we will prove a similar
p
-adic valuation condition for the determinants of
all
(principal or not)
n\times n
-submatrices
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})
of the matrix of
U_{p}
with respect to the power basis, where
\underline{\zeta}
and
\underline{\xi}
are row and column index sets of size
n
.

Step II
:

(Cofactor expansion argument)
The key equality (
1.26.2
) writes the matrix
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})
as the sum of a matrix which is simple at
w_{k}
and a matrix which has small rank at
w_{k}
. Taking the cofactor expansion with respect to this decomposition, we reduce the needed estimate to an estimate on the power series expansion of the characteristic power series of smaller minors.
This step involves some rather subtle inductive processes that we defer to Section
5
for the discussion.

Step III
:

(Estimating power series expansion for smaller minors) This is to complete the inductive argument by proving that the known estimate of Lagrange interpolation coefficients of smaller minors implies the needed power series expansion of the characteristic power series. This part is relatively straightforward, but is tangled with some pathological cases, where the refined halo estimate is crucially needed.

Roadmap of the paper

The first five sections are devoted to proving the local ghost conjecture (Theorem
1.6
or Theorem
2.8
). This is divided as: Section
2
collects background results on the local ghost conjecture from
[
LTXZ

24

]
; Section
3
establishes the two main inputs of the proof as explained in §
1.26
; Sections
4
,
5
, and
6
treat precisely Step I, III, and II in §
1.26
, respectively. (We swapped the order for logical coherence.)
In Section
7
, we recall a known-to-experts result: applying Emerton’s locally analytic Jacquet functor to the Paškūnas modules precisely outputs Breuil–Hellmann-Schraen’s trianguline deformation space (Theorem
7.18
). Combining this with the local ghost theorem, we deduce a theorem on the slopes of the trianguline deformation space (Theorem
7.6
). Applications A and B are corollaries of this.
Section
8
is the second part of the bootstrapping argument: using the knowledge of the slopes on trianguline deformation spaces, we determine the
U_{p}
-slopes for any
\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{K}_{p}\rrbracket
-projective arithmetic modules (Theorem
8.7
). In the case of modular forms, this specializes to Theorem
1.3
. Applications D, E, and F follow from this.
Finally, in Section
9
, we prove the finiteness of irreducible components of spectral curves, namely Theorem
1.16
.

1.27.
Notations

For a field
k
, write
\overline{k}
for its algebraic closure.

Throughout the paper, fix a prime number
p\geq 5
.
Let
\Delta\cong(\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z})^{\times}
be the torsion subgroup of

\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, and let
\omega:\Delta\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
be the
Teichmüller character.
For an element
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, we often use
\bar{\alpha}\in\Delta
to denote its reduction modulo
p
.

Let
E
be a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\sqrt{p})
, as the coefficient field. Let
\mathcal{O}
,
\mathbb{F}
, and
\varpi
denote its ring of integers, residue field, and a uniformizer, respectively. We use
\mathbb{C}_{p}
to denote the
p
-adic completion of an algebraic closure of
E
, and
\mathbf{C}_{p}
to denote a completed algebraically closed field containing
\mathbb{C}_{p}
.
The
p
-adic valuation
v_{p}(-)
and
p
-adic norm are normalized so that
v_{p}(p)=1
and
|p|=p^{-1}
.

We will consider the following subgroups of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
:
\mathrm{K}_{p}=\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
,
\mathrm{Iw}_{p}={\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}&\mathbb{Z}_{p}\\
p\mathbb{Z}_{p}&\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}\end{smallmatrix}\big)}
. Write
B
for the upper-triangular subgroup of
\GL_{2}
and
B^{\mathrm{op}}
for the lower-triangular subgroup.

All hom spaces refer to the spaces of continuous homomorphisms.
For
M
a topological
\mathcal{O}
-module, we write
\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathbb{Z}_{p};M)
for the space of continuous functions on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
with values in
M
.

We use
\lceil x\rceil
to denote the ceiling function and
\lfloor x\rfloor
to denote the floor function.

We shall encounter both the
p
-adic logarithmic function
\log(x)=(x-1)-\frac{(x-1)^{2}}{2}+\cdots
for
x
a
p
-adic or a formal element, and the natural logarithmic function
\ln(-)
in real analysis.

For a formal
\mathcal{O}
-scheme
\Spf(R)
formally of finite type, let
\Spf(R)^{\mathrm{rig}}
denote the associated rigid analytic space over
E
.

For
X
a rigid analytic space over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, write
X^{\mathrm{Berk}}
for the associated Berkovich space.
For each analytic function
f
on
X
and
x\in X^{\mathrm{Berk}}
, write
v_{p}(f(x)):=\ln|f|_{x}/\ln|p|_{x}
.

For each
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, we write
\{m\}
for the unique integer
satisfying the conditions

0\leq\{m\}\leq p-2\quad\textrm{and}\quad m\equiv\{m\}\bmod{(p-1)}.

For a square (possibly infinite) matrix
M
with coefficients in a ring
R
, we write
\Char(M;t):=\mathrm{det}(I-Mt)\in R\llbracket t\rrbracket
(if it is well-defined), where
I
is the identity matrix. For
U
an operator acting on an
R
-module given by such a matrix
M
, we write
\Char(U;t)
for
\Char(M;t)
.

For a power series
F(t)=\sum_{n\geq 0}c_{n}t^{n}\in\mathbf{C}_{p}\llbracket t\rrbracket
with
c_{0}=1
, we use
\NP(F)
to denote its
Newton polygon
, i.e. the convex hull of points
(n,v_{p}(c_{n}))
for all
n
; the slopes of the segments of
\NP(F)
are often referred to as
slopes of
F(t)
. For
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, write
\NP(F)_{x=n}
for the
y
-coordinate of
\NP(F)
when
x=n
.

For two Newton polygons
A
and
B
, let
A\#B
denote the Newton polygon (starting at
(0,0)
) whose set of slopes (with multiplicity) is the disjoint union of those of
A
and
B
.

Let
\mathrm{I}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\subset\Gal(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
denote the inertia subgroup, and
\omega_{1}:\mathrm{I}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\twoheadrightarrow\Gal(\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{p})/\mathbb{Q}_{p})\cong\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}

the
1
st fundamental character
. For
R
a
p
-adic ring and
\alpha\in R^{\times}
, let
\mathrm{unr}(\alpha):\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\to R^{\times}
denote the unramified representation that sends the geometric Frobenius to
\alpha
.

1.28.
Normalizations

It is important to clarify the normalization we use in this paper.

The reciprocity map
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\to\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}^{\mathrm{ab}}
is normalized so that
p
is sent to the
geometric
Frobenius element.
The character
\chi_{\mathrm{cycl}}:\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
given by
\chi_{\mathrm{cycl}}(x)=x|x|
extends to the
cyclotomic character
of
\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
.
The Hodge–Tate weight of
\chi_{\mathrm{cycl}}
in our convention is
-1
.
We use Deligne’s convention on Hodge types and on Shimura varieties as explained in
[
De79
]
, except that the Shimura reciprocity map in
[
De79
, § 2.2.3]
should not have the extra inverse (as pointed out by
[
Mi90
]
).

Our convention on associated Galois representation is “
homological
”. Let us be precise.
Taking the case of modular curve as an example, where we use the
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
-conjugacy class of Deligne homomorphisms
h(x+\mathtt{i}y)={\big(\begin{smallmatrix}x&y\\
-y&x\end{smallmatrix}\big)}
(which determines the canonical model of the modular curve).
For a neat open compact subgroup
K^{p}=\prod_{\ell\neq p}K_{\ell}\subseteq\GL_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
, put
K=K^{p}\mathrm{K}_{p}
and there is a canonical étale
right

\mathrm{K}_{p}
-torsor over the modular curve
Y(K)
of level
K
over
\mathbb{Q}
. Thus, every left
\mathrm{K}_{p}
-module
V
defines an étale local system on
Y(K)
. The
\mathrm{K}_{p}
-module
(\Sym_{\mathrm{L}}^{k-2}\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\oplus 2})^{*}
(with subscript
\mathrm{L}
to indicate left action) corresponds to
\mathcal{L}_{k-2}:=\Sym^{k-2}\big(\mathrm{R}^{1}\mathrm{pr}_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{p}\big)
for
\mathrm{pr}:E\to Y(K)
the universal elliptic curve; this additional dual is dictated by Deligne’s convention on Hodge structure, so that the local system normalization is tailored “homologically” as opposed to “cohomologically” (see
[
De79
, Remarque 1.1.6]
).

For an cuspidal automorphic representation
\pi
of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{A})
, algebraic of weight
k
,
we will always work with Galois representation associated via Langlands correspondence
, in the sense that
\pi^{K}\otimes r_{\pi}^{*}
embeds Hecke equivariantly and Galois equivariantly into
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}\big(Y(K)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},\mathcal{L}_{k-2}\big)
.
In particular, this
r_{\pi,p}:=r_{\pi}|_{\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
has Hodge–Tate weights
\{0,k-1\}
.
We require this dual
r_{\pi}^{*}
to be compatible with Harris–Taylor local Langlands correspondence for
\GL_{n}
, after an “appropriate half twist” (see
[
BuGe

14

]
). Note that the Galois representation appearing in the cohomology of Shimura varieties is the composition of the Langlands parameter with a highest weight representation of the Langlands dual group; and in the above setup of modular curve, the highest weight representation is the
dual of the standard representation of
\GL_{2}
.
If
\alpha
and
\beta
are the eigenvalues of crystalline Frobenius (which behaves exactly like geometric Frobenius) acting on
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{crys}}(r_{\pi,p})
, then
the local-global compatibility implies that
\pi_{p}=\Ind_{B(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}^{\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}\big(\mathrm{unr}(\alpha)\otimes\mathrm{unr}(\beta)|\cdot|^{-1}\big)
.
In order to have a compact
U_{p}
-operator acting on overconvergent forms, we have to work with Hecke operators
T_{p}:=\mathrm{K}_{p}{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-1}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)}\mathrm{K}_{p}
and
S_{p}=\mathrm{K}_{p}{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-1}&0\\
0&p^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\big)}
. Then we would characterize the local-global compatibility by that the
inverses

\alpha^{-1}
and
\beta^{-1}
are zeros of the Hecke polynomial
x^{2}-t_{p}x+ps_{p}=0
, where
t_{p}
and
s_{p}
are the eigenvalues of the
T_{p}
and
S_{p}
acting on
\pi_{p}^{\mathrm{K}_{p}}
.

Working out another crystabelline example when
\mathrm{WD}(r_{\pi,p})=\mathrm{unr}(\alpha)\omega_{1}^{c}\oplus\mathrm{unr}(\beta)\omega_{2}^{d}
with
c\neq d
and
\pi_{p}=\Ind_{B(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}^{\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}\big(\mathrm{unr}(\alpha)\omega^{c}\otimes\mathrm{unr}(\beta)\omega^{d}|\cdot|^{-1}\big)
, there are two associated
\mathrm{Iw}_{p}
-eigenvectors:
\pi_{p}^{\mathrm{Iw}_{p}=\omega^{c}\times\omega^{d}}=\mathbb{Q}_{p}\cdot f_{1}
and
\pi_{p}^{\mathrm{Iw}_{p}=\omega^{d}\times\omega^{c}}=\mathbb{Q}_{p}\cdot f_{w}
(related by Atkin–Lehner involution). The Hecke operator
U_{p}=\mathrm{Iw}_{p}{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-1}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)}\mathrm{Iw}_{p}
acts on them by
U_{p}(f_{1})=\alpha^{-1}f_{1}
and
U_{p}(f_{w})=\beta^{-1}f_{w}
. In classical language, the form
f_{1}
appears in
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}\big(Y(K^{p}\mathrm{Iw}_{p})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},\mathcal{L}_{k-2}\otimes(\omega^{-c}\times\omega^{-d})\big)
; we remind the readers that the twist
\omega^{-c}\times\omega^{-d}
is build from the monodromy of relative Tate modules, as opposed to relative first cohomology.

We however uses a slightly different setup to balance the compatibilities with various references.
A key example of
\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{K}_{p}\rrbracket
-augmented modules are completed
homology
groups:

\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{r}}}:=\varprojlim_{m\to\infty}\mathrm{H}_{1}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}\big(Y\big(K^{p}(1+p^{m}\mathrm{M}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}))\big)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},\mathbb{Z}_{p}\big)_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{r}}},

where
\bar{r}:\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\GL_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
is an absolutely irreducible residual representation. It carries a
right

\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-action. If we consider the left
\mathrm{K}_{p}
-module
\Sym_{\mathrm{L}}^{k-2}\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\oplus 2}
and its dual
\Sym_{\mathrm{R}}^{k-2}\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\oplus 2}
as a right
\mathrm{K}_{p}
-module, then

\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}\big(Y(K^{p}\mathrm{K}_{p})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},\mathcal{L}_{k-2}\big)_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{r}}}\cong\big(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{r}}}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}[\mathrm{K}_{p}]}\Sym_{\mathrm{L}}^{k-2}\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\oplus 2}\big)^{*}\cong\Hom_{\mathcal{O}[\mathrm{K}_{p}]}\big(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{r}}},\,\Sym_{\mathrm{R}}^{k-2}\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\oplus 2}\big).

We will exclusively work with spaces similar to the last term.
In some sense,
\Sym_{\mathrm{R}}^{k-2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\oplus 2})
appears to be using the monodromy group of
\mathrm{R}^{1}\mathrm{pr}_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, as opposed to the relative Tate modules. Everything above transports in parallel to this setting. Similarly, in the crystabelline setup, if
r_{\pi}
appears as
\varphi\in\Hom_{\mathcal{O}[\mathrm{Iw}_{p}]}\big(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{r}}},\,\Sym^{k-2}_{\mathrm{R}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\oplus 2}\otimes(\omega^{c}\times\omega^{d})_{\mathrm{R}}\big)
with
c\neq d
, then
r_{\pi,p}
is crystabelline with Hodge–Tate weights
\{1-k,0\}
and
\mathrm{WD}(r_{\pi,p})=\mathrm{unr}(\alpha)\omega_{1}^{c}\oplus\mathrm{unr}(\beta)\omega_{1}^{d}
with
\alpha^{-1}
being the
U_{p}
-eigenvalue of
\varphi
.
In this case, the triangulation of
r_{\pi,p}
given by
\varphi
is

0\to\mathcal{R}(\mathrm{unr}(\beta)\omega^{b}x^{k-1})\to\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{rig}}^{\dagger}(\rho_{\pi,p})\to\mathcal{R}(\mathrm{unr}(\alpha)\omega^{a})\to 0.

(This can be seen by considering the ordinary case.)
In particular, if we rewrite the two characters of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}
as
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
, then
\delta_{2}(p)^{-1}
is equal to the
U_{p}
-eigenvalue and
\delta_{1}(\exp(p))=1
.

Our convention on Serre weights uses right
\mathrm{K}_{p}
-modules and is thus
cohomological
. More precisely, a
right

\mathrm{K}_{p}
-module
\sigma
is called a
(right) Serre weight
for
\bar{r}_{p}:=\bar{r}|_{\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
if

\Hom_{\mathcal{O}[\mathrm{K}_{p}]}\big(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{r}}},\,\sigma\big)\cong\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}\big(Y(K^{p}\mathrm{K}_{p}),\sigma_{\mathrm{L}})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{r}}}\neq 0,

where
\sigma_{\mathrm{L}}
is to turn
\sigma
into a left
\mathrm{K}_{p}
-module by considering inverse action.
For example, if
\bar{r}_{p}\cong{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\mathrm{unr}(\bar{\alpha})\omega_{1}^{a+b+1}&*\neq 0\\
0&\mathrm{unr}(\bar{\beta})\omega_{1}^{b}\end{smallmatrix}\big)}
, the associated right Serre weight is
\Sym^{a}\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\oplus 2}\otimes\mathrm{det}^{b}
. This is compatible with most references in mod-
p
-local-Langlands correspondences if we turn the right Serre weights into a left Serre weights
via transpose
.

2.
Recollection of the local ghost conjecture

In
[
BP

16

,
BP19a
,
BP19b
]
, Bergdall–Pollack proposed a conjectural combinatorial recipe to compute the slopes of modular forms. This was reformulated by the authors
[
LTXZ

24

]
in a setup that can be adapted to the context of
p
-adic local Langlands correspondence of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. In this section, we first recall this construction as well as the statement of the local ghost conjecture; notations mostly follow from
[
LTXZ

24

]
and we refer to
loc. cit.
for details. After this, we quickly recall the power basis of abstract classical and overconvergent forms as well as the dimension formulas for spaces of abstract classical forms.

Notation 2.1
.

Recall the following subgroups of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
.

\mathrm{K}_{p}:=\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\supset\mathrm{Iw}_{p}:=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}&\mathbb{Z}_{p}\\
p\mathbb{Z}_{p}&\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}\end{pmatrix}\supset\mathrm{Iw}_{p,1}:=\begin{pmatrix}1+p\mathbb{Z}_{p}&\mathbb{Z}_{p}\\
p\mathbb{Z}_{p}&1+p\mathbb{Z}_{p}\end{pmatrix}.

Fix a finite extension
E
of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
containing a square root
\sqrt{p}
of
p
. Let
\mathcal{O}
,
\mathbb{F}
, and
\varpi
denote its ring of integers, residue field, and a uniformizer, respectively.

For a pair of non-negative integers
(a,b)
, we use
\sigma_{a,b}
to denote the
right

\mathbb{F}
-representation
\Sym^{a}\mathbb{F}^{\oplus 2}\otimes\mathrm{det}^{b}
of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
.
When
a\in\{0,\dots,p-1\}
and
b\in\{0,\dots,p-2\}
,
\sigma_{a,b}
is irreducible; these exhaust all irreducible right
\mathbb{F}
-representations of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
. We call them the
Serre weights
.
Write
\mathrm{Proj}(\sigma_{a,b})
for the projective envelope of
\sigma_{a,b}
as a (right)
\mathbb{F}[\GL_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{p})]
-module.

Definition 2.2
.

(essentially
[
LTXZ

24

, Definition 2.22]
)
We say a residual local representation
\bar{r}_{p}:\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\to\GL_{2}(\mathbb{F})
is
reducible nonsplit and generic
if

(2.2.1)

\bar{r}_{p}\simeq{\begin{pmatrix}\omega_{1}^{a+b+1}\mathrm{unr}(\bar{\alpha})&*\neq 0\\
0&\omega_{1}^{b}\mathrm{unr}(\bar{\beta})\end{pmatrix}}

for some
\bar{\alpha},\bar{\beta}\in\mathbb{F}^{\times}
,
a\in\{1,\dots,p-4\}
, and
b\in\{0,\dots,p-2\}
. Here the nontrivial extension
*\neq 0
is unique up to isomorphism because
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},\mathrm{unr}(\bar{\alpha}_{2}^{-1}\bar{\alpha}_{1})\omega^{a+1})
is one-dimensional given the genericity condition on
a
.

We say that
\bar{r}_{p}
is
very generic
if
a\in\{2,\dots,p-5\}
.

Fix such a reducible nonsplit and generic
\bar{r}_{p}
; its associated Serre weight is
\boldsymbol{\sigma}:=\sigma_{a,b}
.

An
\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{K}_{p}\rrbracket
-projective augmented module
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
is a finitely generated
right
projective
\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{K}_{p}\rrbracket
-module equipped with an
right

\mathcal{O}[\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})]
-module structure such that the two induced
\mathcal{O}[\mathrm{K}_{p}]
-structures on
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
coincide. We say that
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
is
of type
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
with multiplicity
m(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}})
if

(1)

(Serre weight)

\overline{\mathrm{H}}:=\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}/(\varpi,\mathrm{I}_{1+p\mathrm{M}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})})
is isomorphic to a direct sum of
m(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}})
copies of
\mathrm{Proj}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})
as a right
\mathbb{F}[\GL_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{p})]
-module.

The topology on such
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
is the one inherited from the
\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{K}_{p}\rrbracket
-module structure.

We say
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
is
primitive
if
m(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}})=1
and
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
satisfies the following additional conditions:

(2)

(Central character I)
the action of
{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p&0\\
0&p\end{smallmatrix}\big)}
on
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
is the multiplication by an invertible element
\xi\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
, and

(3)

(Central character II) there exists an isomorphism
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}\cong\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}_{0}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{O}\llbracket(1+p\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}\rrbracket
of
\mathcal{O}[\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})]
-modules, where
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}_{0}
carries an action of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
which is trivial on elements of the form
{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&0\\
0&\alpha\end{smallmatrix}\big)}
for
\alpha\in(1+p\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}
, and the latter factor
\mathcal{O}\llbracket(1+p\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}\rrbracket
carries the natural action of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
via the map
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{det}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\xrightarrow{p^{r}\delta\mapsto\delta/\omega(\bar{\delta})}(1+p\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}
.

Remark 2.3
.

(1)

In
[
LTXZ

24

]
, we call such
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
of type
\bar{r}_{p}|_{\mathrm{I}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
. This was slightly inappropriate as the extension class
*
in (
2.2.1
) plays no role in the definition. So in this paper, we changed this notion to be “type
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
”.

(2)

We quickly remind the readers here that, for the local theory of ghost conjecture, we only treat the case when
\bar{r}_{p}
is reducible and
nonsplit
, or equivalently, when there is only one Serre weight
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
. It is the later bootstrapping argument in Sections
7
and
8
that allows us to deduce the general reducible case from the reducible nonsplit case.

2.4.
Space of abstract forms

Let
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
denote an
\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{K}_{p}\rrbracket
-projective augmented module.

(1)
Set
\Delta:=\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
and write
\omega:\Delta\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
for the Teichmüller character. For each
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, write
\bar{\alpha}
for its reduction modulo
p
.

Recall that there is a canonical identification
\Lambda:=\mathcal{O}\llbracket(1+p\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}\rrbracket\cong\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket
by sending
[\alpha]
for
\alpha\in(1+p\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}
to
(1+w)^{\log(\alpha)/p}
, where
\log(-)
is the formal
p
-adic logarithm. In particular, for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we set

w_{k}:=\exp(p(k-2))-1.

For a character
\varepsilon:\Delta^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, write
\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket^{(\varepsilon)}
for
\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket
, but equipped with the universal character

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi^{(\varepsilon)}_{\mathrm{univ}}:\Delta\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket^{(\varepsilon),\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{\alpha},\,\delta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon(\bar{\alpha},\bar{\delta})\cdot(1+w)^{\log(\delta/\omega(\bar{\delta}))/p},}

where
\bar{\delta}
is the reduction of
\delta
modulo
p
and
\omega(\bar{\delta})
is the Teichmümller lift of
\bar{\delta}
. The
weight disk

\mathcal{W}^{(\varepsilon)}:=\big(\Spf\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket^{(\varepsilon)}\big)^{\mathrm{rig}}
for
\varepsilon
is the associated rigid analytic space over
E
.
The universal character extends to a character of
B^{\mathrm{op}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})={\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}&0\\
p\mathbb{Z}_{p}&\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}\end{smallmatrix}\big)}
, still denoted by
\chi_{\mathrm{univ}}^{(\varepsilon)}
,
given by

(2.4.1)

\chi_{\mathrm{univ}}^{(\varepsilon)}\big({\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&0\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\big)})=\chi_{\mathrm{univ}}^{(\varepsilon)}(\bar{\alpha},\delta).

For a character
\varepsilon:\Delta^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, consider the induced representation (for the
right action convention
)

(2.4.2)

\displaystyle\Ind_{B^{\mathrm{op}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})}^{\mathrm{Iw}_{p}}(\chi_{\mathrm{univ}}^{(\varepsilon)}):=

\displaystyle\ \big\{\textrm{continuous functions }f:\mathrm{Iw}_{p}\to\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket^{(\varepsilon)};

\displaystyle\quad f(gb)=\chi_{\mathrm{univ}}^{(\varepsilon)}(b)\cdot f(g)\textrm{ for }b\in B^{\mathrm{op}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\textrm{ and }g\in\mathrm{Iw}_{p}\big\}

(2.4.3)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\ \mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathbb{Z}_{p};\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket^{(\varepsilon)}),

where
\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathbb{Z}_{p};-)
denotes the space of continuous functions on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
with values in
-
, the isomorphism is given by
f\mapsto h(z)=f\big({\big(\begin{smallmatrix}1&z\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)}\big)
.
Our choice of convention is so that the left action on its dual, i.e. the distributions
\mathcal{D}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}_{p};\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket^{(\varepsilon)})
is the natural one, and this will be compatible with later Emerton’s lower triangular matrix analytic Jacquet functor
[
Em06
]
; see §
7.20
for the discussion.

This space (
2.4.2
) carries a
right
action of the monoid

\mathbf{M}_{1}=\big\{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\big)}\in\mathrm{M}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p});\ p|\gamma,\,p\nmid\delta,\,\alpha\delta-\beta\gamma\neq 0\big\},

given by the explicit formula (setting determinant
\alpha\delta-\beta\gamma=p^{r}d
with
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
)

(2.4.4)

h\big|_{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}(z)=\varepsilon(\bar{d}/\bar{\delta},\bar{\delta})\cdot(1+w)^{\log\left((\gamma z+\delta)/\omega(\bar{\delta})\right)/p}\cdot h\Big(\frac{\alpha z+\beta}{\gamma z+\delta}\Big).

(2) Fix a character
\varepsilon:\Delta^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
. Write
\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle^{(\varepsilon)}
for the same ring
\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle
equipped the associated universal character (
2.4.1
). For an
\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{K}_{p}\rrbracket
-projective augmented module
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
, define the space of
abstract
p
-adic forms
and the space of
family of abstract overconvergent forms
to be

\displaystyle\mathrm{S}^{(\varepsilon)}_{p\textrm{-adic}}=\mathrm{S}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}},p\textrm{-adic}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathcal{O}[\mathrm{Iw}_{p}]}\big(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}},\,\Ind_{B^{\mathrm{op}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})}^{\mathrm{Iw}_{p}}(\chi_{\mathrm{univ}}^{(\varepsilon)})\big)\cong\Hom_{\mathcal{O}[\mathrm{Iw}_{p}]}\big(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}},\,\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathbb{Z}_{p};\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket^{(\varepsilon)})\big),

\displaystyle\mathrm{S}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}=\mathrm{S}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathcal{O}[\mathrm{Iw}_{p}]}\big(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}},\,\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle^{(\varepsilon)}\langle z\rangle\big),

respectively. Viewing power series in
z
as continuous functions on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
induces a natural inclusion

\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle^{(\varepsilon)}\langle z\rangle\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathbb{Z}_{p};\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket^{(\varepsilon)})\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket}\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle,

such that the
\mathbf{M}_{1}
-action on the latter space given by (
2.4.4
), which stabilizes the subspace. This induces a natural inclusion

(2.4.5)

\mathrm{S}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{S}^{(\varepsilon)}_{p\textrm{-adic}}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket}\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle.

The space
\mathrm{S}^{(\varepsilon)}_{p\textrm{-adic}}
(resp.
\mathrm{S}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}
) carries
an
\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket
-linear (resp.
\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle
-linear)
U_{p}
-action: fixing a decomposition of the double coset
\mathrm{Iw}_{p}{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-1}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)}\mathrm{Iw}_{p}=\coprod_{j=0}^{p-1}v_{j}\mathrm{Iw}_{p}
(e.g.
v_{j}={\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-1}&0\\
j&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)}
and
v_{j}^{-1}={\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p&0\\
-jp&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)}
), the
U_{p}
-operator sends
\varphi\in\mathrm{S}_{p\textrm{-adic}}^{(\varepsilon)}
(resp.
\varphi\in\mathrm{S}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}
) to

(2.4.6)

U_{p}(\varphi)(x)=\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}\varphi(xv_{j})|_{v_{j}^{-1}}\quad\textrm{for all }x\in\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}.

The
U_{p}
-operator does not depend on the choice of coset representatives.
As explained in
[
LTXZ

24

, § 2.10 and Lemma 2.14]
, the characteristic power series of the
U_{p}
-action on
\mathrm{S}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}
and
\mathrm{S}^{(\varepsilon)}_{p\textrm{-adic}}
are well-defined and are equal; we denote it by

C^{(\varepsilon)}(w,t)=C^{(\varepsilon)}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}}(w,t)=\sum_{n\geq 0}c_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)t^{n}\in\Lambda\llbracket t\rrbracket=\mathcal{O}\llbracket w,t\rrbracket.

The main subject of local ghost conjecture is to provide an “approximation” of
C^{(\varepsilon)}(w,t)
.

For each integer
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, evaluating at
w=w_{k}:=\exp((k-2)p)-1
, we arrive at the space of
abstract overconvergent forms of weight
k
and character

\psi=\varepsilon\cdot(1\times\omega^{2-k})
:

\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\dagger}(\psi)=\mathrm{S}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}},k}^{\dagger}(\psi):=\mathrm{S}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle,w\mapsto w_{k}}\mathcal{O},

carrying compatible
U_{p}
-actions. Moreover, the characteristic power series for the
U_{p}
-action is precisely
C^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{k},t)
.

(3)
For each integer
k\geq 2
, write
\mathcal{O}[z]^{\leq k-2}
for the space of polynomials of degree
\leq k-2
. Setting
\psi=\varepsilon\cdot(1\times\omega^{2-k})
, we have a canonical inclusion

\mathcal{O}[z]^{\leq k-2}\otimes\psi\ \subset\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle^{(\varepsilon)}\langle z\rangle\otimes_{\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle,w\mapsto w_{k}}\mathcal{O},

such that
the
\mathbf{M}_{1}
-action on the latter given by (
2.4.4
) stabilizes the submodule.
So we may define the space of
abstract classical forms of weight
k
and character
\psi
to be the
U_{p}
-stable submodule

\mathrm{S}^{\mathrm{Iw}}_{k}(\psi)=\mathrm{S}^{\mathrm{Iw}}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}},k}(\psi):=\Hom_{\mathcal{O}[\mathrm{Iw}_{p}]}\big(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}},\,\mathcal{O}[z]^{\leq k-2}\otimes\psi\big)\ \subset\ \mathrm{S}^{\dagger}_{k}(\psi),

In particular, the characteristic power series of the
U_{p}
-action on
\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\psi)
divides
C^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{k},t)
.

(4) For a character
\varepsilon_{1}:\Delta\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, write
\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1}:=\varepsilon_{1}\times\varepsilon_{1}:\Delta^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
for the corresponding character.
The space
\mathcal{O}[z]^{\leq k-2}\otimes(\varepsilon_{1}\circ\mathrm{det})
carries a natural action of the monoid
\mathrm{M}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\mathrm{det}\neq 0}
as follows: for
{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\big)}\in\mathrm{M}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
(setting determinant
\alpha\delta-\beta\gamma=p^{r}d
with
d
in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
),

h|_{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}(z)=\varepsilon_{1}(\bar{d})\cdot(\gamma z+\delta)^{k-2}h\Big(\frac{\alpha z+\beta}{\gamma z+\delta}\Big).

Define the space of
abstract classical forms with
\mathrm{K}_{p}
-level of weight
k
and central character
\varepsilon_{1}
to be

\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})=\mathrm{S}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}},k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1}):=\Hom_{\mathcal{O}[\mathrm{K}_{p}]}\big(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}},\,\mathcal{O}[z]^{\leq k-2}\otimes(\varepsilon_{1}\circ\mathrm{det})\big).

This space carries an action of the
T_{p}
-operator: taking a coset decomposition
\mathrm{K}_{p}{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-1}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)}\mathrm{K}_{p}=\coprod_{j=0}^{p}u_{j}\mathrm{K}_{p}
(e.g.
u_{j}={\big(\begin{smallmatrix}1&jp^{-1}\\
0&p^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\big)}
with
u_{j}^{-1}={\big(\begin{smallmatrix}1&-j\\
0&p\end{smallmatrix}\big)}
for
j=0,\dots,p-1
, and
u_{p}={\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-1}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)}
with
u_{p}^{-1}={\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)}
), the
T_{p}
-operator sends
\varphi\in\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})
to

(2.4.7)

T_{p}(\varphi)(x)=\sum_{j=0}^{p}\varphi(xu_{j})|_{u_{j}^{-1}}\quad\textrm{for all }x\in\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}.

(5)
Let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\sigma_{a,b}
denote a Serre weight.
A character
\varepsilon
of
\Delta^{2}
is called
relevant
to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\sigma_{a,b}
if it is of the form

\varepsilon=\omega^{-s_{\varepsilon}+b}\times\omega^{a+s_{\varepsilon}+b}

for some
s_{\varepsilon}\in\{0,\dots,p-2\}
, or equivalently,
\varepsilon(x,x)=x^{a+2b}
for any
x\in\Delta
. For the rest of this paper, we will always use
\varepsilon
to denote a character of
\Delta^{2}
relevant to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
.

For each
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, we write
\{m\}
for the residue class of
m
modulo
p-1
, represented by an element in
\{0,\dots,p-2\}
.
For the relevant
\varepsilon
above, put

k_{\varepsilon}:=2+\{a+2s_{\varepsilon}\}\in\{2,\dots,p\}.

If a character
\psi:\Delta^{2}\to\mathcal{O}^{\times}
is of the form
\varepsilon\cdot(1\times\omega^{2-k})=\varepsilon^{-s_{\varepsilon}+b}\times\varepsilon^{a+s_{\varepsilon}+b+2-k}
for an integer
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
as in (3) and is at the same time of the form
\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1}=\varepsilon_{1}\times\varepsilon_{1}
as in (4), then we must have

k\equiv k_{\varepsilon}\bmod(p-1).

In this case, we have natural inclusion

\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})\subseteq\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1}).

(6)
Let
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
be a primitive
\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{K}_{p}\rrbracket
-projective augmented module of type
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\sigma_{a,b}
and let
\varepsilon
be a character of
\Delta^{2}
relevant to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
.
For a character
\psi=\varepsilon\cdot(1\times\omega^{2-k})
, put

d^{\mathrm{Iw}}_{k}(\psi):=\rank_{\mathcal{O}}\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\psi).

For
\varepsilon_{1}:=\omega^{-s_{\varepsilon}+b}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
such that
k\equiv k_{\varepsilon}\bmod(p-1)
, set

d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1}):=\rank_{\mathcal{O}}\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})\quad\textrm{and}\quad d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}(\varepsilon_{1}):=d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})-2d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1}).

The ranks
d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\psi)
,
d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})
, and
d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}(\varepsilon_{1})
defined above depend only on
a
,
b
,
s_{\varepsilon}
,
\psi
, and
k
. For their precise formulas, see Definition-Proposition
2.13
later.

(7) Since the definition of
\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\psi)
and
\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})
only uses the
\mathrm{K}_{p}
-modules structure of
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
, it follows that, for a
\mathrm{K}_{p}
-projective augmented module
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
of type
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
with multiplicity
m(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}})
,

(2.4.8)

\rank_{\mathcal{O}}\mathrm{S}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}},k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\psi)=m(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}})\cdot d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\psi)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\rank_{\mathcal{O}}\mathrm{S}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}},k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})=m(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}})\cdot d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1}).

Definition 2.5
.

Following
[
BP

16

]
, we define the
ghost series of type
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
over
\mathcal{W}^{(\varepsilon)}
to be the formal power series

G^{(\varepsilon)}(w,t)=G^{(\varepsilon)}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(w,t)=1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)t^{n}\in\mathcal{O}[w]\llbracket t\rrbracket,

where each coefficient
g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)
is a product

(2.5.1)

g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq 2\\
k\equiv k_{\varepsilon}\bmod{(p-1)}\end{subarray}}(w-w_{k})^{m_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(k)}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}[w]

with exponents
m_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(k)
given by the following recipe

m_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(k)=\begin{cases}\min\big\{n-d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1}),d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})-d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})-n\big\}&\textrm{ if }d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})<n<d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})-d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})\\
0&\textrm{ otherwise.}\end{cases}
