(When all
m_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}=0
in the product, we set
g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}=1
.)
For a fixed
k
, the sequence
(m_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(k))_{n\geq 1}
is given by the following palindromic pattern

(2.5.2)

\underbrace{0,\dots,0}_{d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})},1,2,3,\dots,\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\textrm{new}}(\varepsilon_{1})-1,\tfrac{1}{2}{d_{k}^{\textrm{new}}(\varepsilon_{1})},\tfrac{1}{2}{d_{k}^{\textrm{new}}(\varepsilon_{1})}-1,\dots,3,2,1,0,0,\dots,

where the maximum
\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}(\varepsilon_{1})
appears at the
\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})
th place.

When
m_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(k)\neq 0
, we often refer
w_{k}
as a
ghost zero
of
g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)
.

Notation 2.6
.

As indicated in the definition above, for a ghost zero
w_{k}
of
g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)
, we can always write
k=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{\bullet}
for some
k_{\bullet}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

We will later often write
k=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{\bullet}
, to mean that
by convention,
k_{\bullet}
is a nonnegative integer
, without explicit stating that. (In particular
k\equiv k_{\varepsilon}\bmod(p-1)
and
k\geq 2
.)

Conjecture 2.7

(Local ghost conjecture)
.

Let
\bar{r}_{p}\simeq{\begin{pmatrix}\omega_{1}^{a+b+1}\mathrm{unr}(\bar{\alpha})&*\neq 0\\
0&\omega_{1}^{b}\mathrm{unr}(\bar{\beta})\end{pmatrix}}:\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\to\GL_{2}(\mathbb{F})
be a reducible nonsplit and generic residual representation with
a\in\{1,\dots,p-4\}
and
b\in\{0,\dots,p-2\}
, as in (
2.2.1
).
Let
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
be a primitive
\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{K}_{p}\rrbracket
-projective augmented module of type
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\sigma_{a,b}
, and let
\varepsilon
be a character of
\Delta^{2}
relevant to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
. We define the characteristic power series
C^{(\varepsilon)}(w,t)
of
U_{p}
-action for
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
and the ghost series
G^{(\varepsilon)}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(w,t)
of type
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
as in this section. Then
for every
w_{\star}\in\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{C}_{p}}
, we have

\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star},-))=\NP(C^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star},-))
.

The main local result of this paper is the following.

Theorem 2.8
.

The Conjecture
2.7
holds when
p\geq 11
and
2\leq a\leq p-5
.

Remark 2.9
.

The only place that we essentially need
a\not\in\{1,p-4\}
and
p\geq 11
is in the proof of Proposition
5.5
(1); see also Remark
5.15
. We do not know whether one can make more delicate discussions on boundary cases to retrieve the theorem when
a\in\{1,p-4\}
or
p=11
. The condition
p\geq 7
is required at more places, e.g.
[
LTXZ

24

, Corollary 5.10]
.

As pointed out in
[
LTXZ

24

, Remark 2.30]
, after twisting, we may and will assume that
b=0
and that
{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p&0\\
0&p\end{smallmatrix}\big)}
acts trivially on
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
.

Hypothesis 2.10
.

From now on till the end of Section
6
(with the exception of Proposition
2.15
and the following remarks), we assume that
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
is a primitive
\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{K}_{p}\rrbracket
-projective augmented module of type
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
, with
b=0
and
\xi=1
. In particular,
\overline{\mathrm{H}}=\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}/(\varpi,\mathrm{I}_{1+p\mathrm{M}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})})\simeq\mathrm{Proj}(\sigma_{a,0})
, and
{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p&0\\
0&p\end{smallmatrix}\big)}
acts trivially on
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
.

The letter
\varepsilon
is reserved to denote a character of
\Delta^{2}
relevant to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
.

For the rest of this section, we recall important definitions and results regarding abstract forms and ghost series that we have proved in the prequel
[
LTXZ

24

]
; we refer to
loc. cit.
for details and proofs.

2.11.
Power basis

In
[
LTXZ

24

, § 3]
, we constructed a power basis of the space of abstract overconvergent forms.
Let
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
be as above. As explained in
[
LTXZ

24

, § 3.2]
, we may write
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
as a right
\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{Iw}_{p}\rrbracket
-module

(2.11.1)

\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}\simeq e_{1}\big(\mathcal{O}\otimes_{\chi_{1},\mathcal{O}[\bar{\mathrm{T}}]}\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{Iw}_{p}\rrbracket\big)\oplus e_{2}\big(\mathcal{O}\otimes_{\chi_{2},\mathcal{O}[\bar{\mathrm{T}}]}\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{Iw}_{p}\rrbracket\big)

for the two characters
\chi_{1}=1\times\omega^{a}
and
\chi_{2}=\omega^{a}\times 1
of
\bar{\mathrm{T}}=\Delta^{2}
(embedded diagonally in
\mathrm{Iw}_{p}
). Moreover, by
[
LTXZ

24

, Lemma 3.3]
we may require that
e_{i}{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
p&0\end{smallmatrix}\big)}=e_{3-i}
for
i=1,2
.
We fix such an isomorphism (
2.11.1
).

For the relevant character
\varepsilon=\omega^{-s_{\varepsilon}}\times\omega^{a+s_{\varepsilon}}
of
\Delta^{2}
, we have

\mathrm{S}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}=\Hom_{\mathcal{O}[\mathrm{Iw}_{p}]}\big(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}},\,\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle^{(\varepsilon)}\langle z\rangle\big)\cong e_{1}^{*}\cdot\big(\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle^{(\varepsilon)}\langle z\rangle\big)^{\bar{\mathrm{T}}=1\times\omega^{a}}\oplus e_{2}^{*}\cdot\big(\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle^{(\varepsilon)}\langle z\rangle\big)^{\bar{\mathrm{T}}=\omega^{a}\times 1}.

The power basis
\{z^{n}|n\geq 0\}
of
\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle^{(\varepsilon)}\langle z\rangle
consists of eigenvectors under the action of
\bar{\mathrm{T}}
such that
\bar{\mathrm{T}}
acts (from the right) on
z^{n}
via the character
(\omega^{n}\times\omega^{-n})\cdot\varepsilon
for all
n\geq 0
.
Thus the following list is a basis of
\mathrm{S}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}
and also a basis of
\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\dagger}\big(\varepsilon\cdot(1\times\omega^{2-k})\big)
for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
:

(2.11.2)

\mathbf{B}^{(\varepsilon)}:=\big\{e_{1}^{*}z^{s_{\varepsilon}},e_{1}^{*}z^{p-1+s_{\varepsilon}},e_{1}^{*}z^{2(p-1)+s_{\varepsilon}},\dots;e_{2}^{*}z^{\{a+s_{\varepsilon}\}},e_{2}^{*}z^{p-1+\{a+s_{\varepsilon}\}},e_{2}^{*}z^{2(p-1)+\{a+s_{\varepsilon}\}},\dots\big\}.

When
k\geq 2
, the subsequence consisting of terms whose power in
z
is less than or equal to
k-2
forms a basis of
\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}\big(\varepsilon\cdot(1\times\omega^{2-k})\big)
; we denote this by
\mathbf{B}^{(\varepsilon)}_{k}
.

The
degree
of each basis element
\mathbf{e}=e_{i}^{*}z^{j}\in\mathbf{B}^{(\varepsilon)}
is its exponent on
z
, namely,
\deg(e_{i}^{*}z^{j})=j
.
We order the elements in
\mathbf{B}^{(\varepsilon)}
as
\mathbf{e}_{1}^{(\varepsilon)},\mathbf{e}_{2}^{(\varepsilon)},\dots
with increasing degrees. (Under our generic assumption
1\leq a\leq p-2
, the degrees of elements of
\mathbf{B}^{(\varepsilon)}
are pairwise distinct.)

Write
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}\in\mathrm{M}_{\infty}(\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle)
for the matrix of the
\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle
-linear
U_{p}
-action on
\mathrm{S}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}
with respect to the power basis
\mathbf{B}^{(\varepsilon)}
; for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
, the evaluation of
\mathrm{S}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}
at
w=w_{k}
is the matrix
\mathrm{U}_{k}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}
of the
U_{p}
-action on
\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\dagger}\big(\varepsilon\cdot(1\times\omega^{2-k})\big)
(with respect to
\mathbf{B}^{(\varepsilon)}
). In particular,

\Char(\mathrm{U}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)};t)=C^{(\varepsilon)}(w,t)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\Char(\mathrm{U}_{k}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)};t)=C^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{k},t).

Here and later, despite
\mathbf{M}_{1}
acts on both
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
and
\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle^{(\varepsilon)}\langle z\rangle
from the right, we view
U_{p}
as a left-action-operator. In particular, the entry of
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}
labeled by
(\mathbf{e},\mathbf{e}^{\prime})
is the coefficient of
\mathbf{e}
in the expansion of
U_{p}(\mathbf{e}^{\prime})
as a linear combination of basis elements in
\mathbf{B}^{(\varepsilon)}
.

The following are standard facts regarding theta maps and the Atkin–Lehner involutions.

Proposition 2.12
.

Keep the notations as above and let
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
.

(1)

(Theta maps) Put

\psi=\varepsilon\cdot(1\times\omega^{2-k})
,
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\varepsilon\cdot(\omega^{k-1}\times\omega^{1-k})
with
s_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}=\{s_{\varepsilon}+1-k\}
, and
\psi^{\prime}=\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot(1\times\omega^{k})=\psi\cdot\tilde{\omega}^{k-1}
.
There is a short exact sequence

(2.12.1)

0\to\mathrm{S}^{\mathrm{Iw}}_{k}(\psi)\longrightarrow\mathrm{S}^{\dagger}_{k}(\psi)\xrightarrow{(\frac{d}{dz})^{k-1}\circ}\mathrm{S}_{2-k}^{\dagger}(\psi^{\prime}),

which is equivariant for the usual
U_{p}
-action on the first two terms and the
p^{k-1}U_{p}
-action on the third term. Here the map
\big(\frac{d}{dz}\big)^{k-1}\circ
is given by post-composition with the element
\varphi\in\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\dagger}(\psi)
when viewing the latter as a map from
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
to
\mathcal{O}\langle z\rangle
. The sequence (
2.12.1
) is right exact (i.e. the map
\big(\frac{d}{dz}\big)^{k-1}\circ
is surjective) when restricted to the subspace where
U_{p}
-slopes are finite.

More accurately, the matrix
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}_{k}
is a block-upper-triangular matrix of the form

(2.12.2)

\mathrm{U}_{k}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{U}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw},(\varepsilon)}&*\\
0&p^{k-1}D^{-1}\mathrm{U}_{2-k}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon^{\prime})}D\end{pmatrix},

where the
d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}\big(\varepsilon\cdot(1\times\omega^{2-k})\big)\times d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}\big(\varepsilon\cdot(1\times\omega^{2-k})\big)
upper-left block
\mathrm{U}^{\mathrm{Iw},(\varepsilon)}_{k}
is the matrix for the
U_{p}
-action on
\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}\big(\varepsilon\cdot(1\times\omega^{2-k})\big)
with respect to
\mathbf{B}_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}
,
D
is the diagonal matrix whose diagonal entries are indexed by
\mathbf{e}=e_{i}^{*}z^{j}\in\mathbf{B}^{(\varepsilon)}
with
j\geq k-1
, and are given by
j(j-1)\cdots(j-k+2)
.

In particular, finite
U_{p}
-slopes of
\mathrm{S}^{\dagger}_{k}(\psi)
that are strictly less than
k-1
are the same as the finite
U_{p}
-slopes of
\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\psi)
that are strictly less than
k-1
(counted with multiplicity). The multiplicity of
k-1
as
U_{p}
-slopes of
\mathrm{S}^{\dagger}_{k}(\psi)
is the sum of the multiplicity of
k-1
as
U_{p}
-slopes of
\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\psi)
and the multiplicity of
0
as
U_{p}
-slopes of
\mathrm{S}^{\dagger}_{2-k}(\psi^{\prime})
.

(2)

(Atkin–Lehner involutions)
Write
\psi=\varepsilon\cdot(1\times\omega^{2-k})=\psi_{1}\times\psi_{2}
as character of
\Delta^{2}
(where we allow
\psi_{1}=\psi_{2}
). Put
\psi^{s}=\psi_{2}\times\psi_{1}
and
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}=\varepsilon\cdot\psi^{s}\cdot\psi^{-1}
so that
s_{\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}}=\{k-2-a-s_{\varepsilon}\}
.
Then we have a well-defined natural
Atkin–Lehner involution
:

(2.12.3)

\textstyle{\mathrm{AL}_{(k,\psi)}:\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\psi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\psi^{s})}
\textstyle{\varphi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\ \mathrm{AL}_{(k,\psi)}(\varphi):x\mapsto\varphi\big(x{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0&p^{-1}\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\big)}\big)\big|_{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
p&0\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}\ ).}

Here the last
|_{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
p&0\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}
is the usual action on
\mathcal{O}[z]^{\leq k-2}
and is the
trivial
action on the factor
\psi^{s}
.

Explicitly, for
i=1,2
and any
j
, or for any
\ell=1,\dots,d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\psi^{s})
,

(2.12.4)

\mathrm{AL}_{(k,\psi)}(e_{i}^{*}z^{j})=p^{k-2-j}\cdot e_{3-i}^{*}z^{k-2-j},\qquad\mathrm{AL}_{(k,\psi)}(\mathbf{e}_{\ell}^{(\varepsilon)})=p^{k-2-\deg\mathbf{e}_{\ell}}\mathbf{e}^{(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})}_{d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\psi^{s})+1-\ell},

where we added superscripts to the power basis elements to indicate the corresponding characters.
In particular, we have

(2.12.5)

\mathrm{AL}_{(k,\psi^{s})}\circ\mathrm{AL}_{(k,\psi)}=p^{k-2}.

When
\psi_{1}\neq\psi_{2}
(or equivalently
k\not\equiv k_{\varepsilon}\bmod{(p-1)}
), we have an equality

(2.12.6)

U_{p}\circ\mathrm{AL}_{(k,\psi)}\circ U_{p}=p^{k-1}\cdot\mathrm{AL}_{(k,\psi)}

as maps from
\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\psi)
to
\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\psi^{s})
.
Consequently, when
\psi_{1}\neq\psi_{2}
, we can pair the slopes for the
U_{p}
-action on
\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\psi)
and the slopes for the
U_{p}
-action on
\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\psi^{s})
so that each pair adds up to
k-1
. In particular all slopes on
\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\psi)
belong to
[0,k-1]
.

Proof.

See
[
LTXZ

24

, Propositions 3.10 and 3.12]
.
∎

The following summarizes the dimension formulas for the spaces of abstract classical forms.

Definition-Proposition 2.13
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
be a primitive
\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{K}_{p}\rrbracket
-projective augmented module of type
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
and let
\varepsilon=\omega^{-s_{\varepsilon}}\times\omega^{a+s_{\varepsilon}}
be a relevant character of
\Delta^{2}
. (Recall from Notation
2.6
that whenever writing
k=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{\bullet}
, we implicitly assume that
k_{\bullet}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.)

(1)

We have

d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}\big(\varepsilon\cdot(1\times\omega^{2-k})\big)=\Big\lfloor\frac{k-2-s_{\varepsilon}}{p-1}\Big\rfloor+\Big\lfloor\frac{k-2-\{a+s_{\varepsilon}\}}{p-1}\Big\rfloor+2.

(2)

Set
\delta_{\varepsilon}:=\Big\lfloor\dfrac{s_{\varepsilon}+\{a+s_{\varepsilon}\}}{p-1}\Big\rfloor
. When
k=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{\bullet}
, we have

d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})=2k_{\bullet}+2-2\delta_{\varepsilon}.

(3)

Introduce two integers
t_{1}^{(\varepsilon)},t_{2}^{(\varepsilon)}\in\mathbb{Z}
:

•

when
a+s_{\varepsilon}<p-1
,
t_{1}^{(\varepsilon)}=s_{\varepsilon}+\delta_{\varepsilon}
and
t_{2}^{(\varepsilon)}=a+s_{\varepsilon}+\delta_{\varepsilon}+2
;

•

when
a+s_{\varepsilon}\geq p-1
,
t_{1}^{(\varepsilon)}=\{a+s_{\varepsilon}\}+\delta_{\varepsilon}+1
and
t_{2}^{(\varepsilon)}=s_{\varepsilon}+\delta_{\varepsilon}+1
.

Then for
k=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{\bullet}
, we have

d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})=\Big\lfloor\frac{k_{\bullet}-t_{1}^{(\varepsilon)}}{p+1}\Big\rfloor+\Big\lfloor\frac{k_{\bullet}-t_{2}^{(\varepsilon)}}{p+1}\Big\rfloor+2.

(4)

Recall the power basis
\mathbf{B}^{(\varepsilon)}=\{\mathbf{e}_{1}^{(\varepsilon)},\mathbf{e}_{2}^{(\varepsilon)},\dots\}
. Define the
n
th Hodge slope
to be

\lambda_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}:=\deg\mathbf{e}_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}-\Big\lfloor\frac{\deg\mathbf{e}_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}}{p}\Big\rfloor.

If
a+s_{\varepsilon}<p-1
, we have

(2.13.1)

\deg g_{n+1}^{(\varepsilon)}-\deg g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}-\lambda_{n+1}^{(\varepsilon)}=\begin{cases}1&\textrm{ if }n-2s_{\varepsilon}\equiv 1,3,\dots,2a+1\bmod{2p},\\
-1&\textrm{ if }n-2s_{\varepsilon}\equiv 2,4,\dots,2a+2\bmod{2p},\\
0&\textrm{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

If
a+s_{\varepsilon}\geq p-1
, we have

(2.13.2)

\deg g_{n+1}^{(\varepsilon)}-\deg g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}-\lambda_{n+1}^{(\varepsilon)}=\begin{cases}1&\textrm{ if }n-2s_{\varepsilon}\equiv 2,4,\dots,2a+2\bmod{2p},\\
-1&\textrm{ if }n-2s_{\varepsilon}\equiv 3,5,\dots,2a+3\bmod{2p},\\
0&\textrm{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

In either case, we have

(2.13.3)

\deg g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}-(\lambda_{1}^{(\varepsilon)}+\cdots+\lambda_{n}^{(\varepsilon)})=\begin{cases}0&\textrm{ if }\deg\mathbf{e}_{n+1}-\deg\mathbf{e}_{n}=a,\\
0\textrm{ or }1&\textrm{ if }\deg\mathbf{e}_{n+1}-\deg\mathbf{e}_{n}=p-1-a.\end{cases}

Moreover, the differences
\deg g_{n+1}^{(\varepsilon)}-\deg g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}
are strictly increasing in
n
.

Finally,
\deg g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}=0
for
n\geq 1
only happens when
\varepsilon=1\times\omega^{a}
and
n=1
.

(5)

For two weights
k=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{\bullet}
and
k^{\prime}=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k^{\prime}_{\bullet}
, we have

\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}-\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}=k_{\bullet}-k^{\prime}_{\bullet},\quad\tfrac{2k_{\bullet}}{p+1}\leq d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}\leq\tfrac{2k_{\bullet}}{p+1}+2,\quad\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}\geq\tfrac{p-1}{p+1}k_{\bullet}-1,

\tfrac{2}{p+1}|k_{\bullet}-k^{\prime}_{\bullet}|-2\leq|d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}-d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{ur}}|\leq\tfrac{2}{p+1}|k_{\bullet}-k^{\prime}_{\bullet}|+2,\quad\textrm{and}\quad|\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{new}}|\geq\tfrac{p-1}{p+1}|k_{\bullet}-k^{\prime}_{\bullet}|-2.

Proof.

For (1), see
[
LTXZ

24

, Proposition 4.1]
. For (2), see
[
LTXZ

24

, Corollary 4.4]
. For (3), see
[
LTXZ

24

, Proposition 4.7]
. For (4) except for the last statement, see
[
LTXZ

24

, Proposition 4.11]
. For the last statement, we note that (
2.13.3
) forces if
\deg g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}=0
, then
n=1
and
\lambda_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}=0
. This can only happen when
\varepsilon=1\times\omega^{a}
and
\varepsilon=\omega^{a}\times 1
. In the first case,
\deg g_{n}^{(1\times\omega^{a})}(w)=0
by (
2.13.1
), and in the second case,
\deg g_{n}^{(\omega^{a}\times 1)}=1
by (
2.13.2
). For (5),
\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}-\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}=k_{\bullet}-k^{\prime}_{\bullet}
follows from (2); the inequalities
\tfrac{2k_{\bullet}}{p+1}\leq d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}\leq\tfrac{2k_{\bullet}}{p+1}+2
and
\tfrac{2}{p+1}|k_{\bullet}-k^{\prime}_{\bullet}|-2\leq|d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}-d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{ur}}|\leq\tfrac{2}{p+1}|k_{\bullet}-k^{\prime}_{\bullet}|+2
follows from (3) and the elementary inequality
\alpha-1<\lfloor\alpha\rfloor\leq\alpha
for a rational number
\alpha
. Note that when
\delta_{\varepsilon}=1
, we always have
t_{1}+t_{2}\geq p+1
. The inequality
\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}\geq\tfrac{p-1}{p+1}k_{\bullet}-1
follows from this fact and (2)(3). For the last inequality, we note that
d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}
is non-decreasing with respect to
k
, thus, we may assume that
k>k^{\prime}
. So we deduce the last inequality as follows

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{new}}=\

\displaystyle(\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}-\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Iw}})-(d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}-d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{ur}})

\displaystyle\geq\

\displaystyle(k_{\bullet}-k^{\prime}_{\bullet})-\tfrac{2}{p+1}(k^{\prime}_{\bullet}-k_{\bullet})-2=\tfrac{p-1}{p+1}(k_{\bullet}-k^{\prime}_{\bullet})-2.\qed

Before proceeding, we prove an interesting coincidence of ghost series, for which we temporarily drop the condition
b=0
in Hypothesis
2.10
. This is of crucial importance for our later argument to treat the residually split case.

Proposition 2.15
.

Consider the residual representation
\bar{r}_{p}^{\prime}:\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\to\GL_{2}(\mathbb{F})
given by

\bar{r}_{p}^{\prime}\simeq{\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{unr}(\bar{\beta})&*\neq 0\\
0&\omega_{1}^{a+1}\mathrm{unr}(\bar{\alpha})\end{pmatrix}}=\begin{pmatrix}\omega_{1}^{(p-3-a)+(a+1)+1}\mathrm{unr}(\bar{\beta})&*\neq 0\\
0&\omega_{1}^{a+1}\mathrm{unr}(\bar{\alpha})\end{pmatrix}.

Set
a^{\prime}=p-3-a
and
b^{\prime}=a+1
accordingly and let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}=\sigma_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}
be the Serre weight of
\bar{r}_{p}^{\prime}
. For
s_{\varepsilon}\in\{0,\dots,p-2\}
, write
s^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}=\{a+s_{\varepsilon}+1\}
so that
\varepsilon=\omega^{-s_{\varepsilon}}\times\omega^{a+s_{\varepsilon}}=\omega^{-s^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}+b^{\prime}}\times\omega^{a^{\prime}+s^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}+b^{\prime}}
. In particular, a character
\varepsilon
of
\Delta^{2}
relevant to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
if and only if it is relevant to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}
.

(1)

When
s_{\varepsilon}\notin\{0,p-2-a\}
, we have

G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w,t)=G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w,t).

In the other two cases, we have

(2.15.1)

G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(1\times\omega^{a})}(w,t)=1+tG_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}}^{(1\times\omega^{a})}(w,t)\quad\textrm{and}\quad G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}}^{(\omega^{a+1}\times\omega^{-1})}(w,t)=1+tG_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\omega^{a+1}\times\omega^{-1})}(w,t).

(2)

Fix
w_{\star}\in\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{C}_{p}}
. The Newton polygons
\NP\big(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star},-)\big)
and
\NP\big(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star},-)\big)
agree, except that when
\varepsilon=1\times\omega^{a}
(resp.
\varepsilon=\omega^{a+1}\times\omega^{-1}
),
\NP\big(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star},-)\big)
has one more (resp. one less) slope
0
segment than that of
\NP\big(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star},-)\big)
.

Remark 2.16
.

The representations
\bar{r}_{p}
and
\bar{r}_{p}^{\prime}
have the same semisimplification. On the Galois side, the Galois representations associated to overconvergent modular forms are typically irreducible, in which case one cannot distinguish different reductions
\bar{r}_{p}
and
\bar{r}_{p}^{\prime}
. This is reflected in the statement of Proposition
2.15
: ghost series for
\bar{r}_{p}
is almost the same as the ghost series for
\bar{r}_{p}^{\prime}
over the same weight disk.
The additional subtle relation in (
2.15.1
) accounts for the cases when the associated Galois representations are ordinary (and reducible).

The Galois side of this proposition is discussed later in §
7.11
, and later used in Theorem
7.6
to extend our results from the reducible nonsplit case to the reducible split case.

Proof of Proposition
2.15
.

(1) We add a prime to indicate the corresponding construction for
\bar{r}_{p}^{\prime}
, e.g. write
k^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
,
d^{\mathrm{Iw}^{\prime}}_{k}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})
and etc.
First of all, for the given
s_{\varepsilon}
, we have

k_{\varepsilon}=2+\{a+2s_{\varepsilon}\}=2+\{a^{\prime}+2s^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\}=k^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}.

This means the ghost zeros for
G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w,t)
and for
G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w,t)
are congruent modulo
p-1
.
The main difference comes from Definition-Proposition
2.13
(2):

\delta_{\varepsilon}-\delta^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}=\Big\lfloor\frac{s_{\varepsilon}+\{a+s_{\varepsilon}\}}{p-1}\Big\rfloor-\Big\lfloor\frac{\{a+s_{\varepsilon}+1\}+\{s_{\varepsilon}-1\}}{p-1}\Big\rfloor=\begin{cases}-1&\textrm{if }s_{\varepsilon}=0\\
1&\textrm{if }s_{\varepsilon}=p-2-a\\
0&\textrm{otherwise.}\end{cases}

For
k=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{\bullet}
, Definition-Proposition
2.13
(2) says that

(2.16.1)

d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})=2k_{\bullet}+2-2\delta_{\varepsilon},\quad d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}\prime}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})=2k_{\bullet}+2-2\delta^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}.

For computing
d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})
and
d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}\prime}(\varepsilon_{1})
, we list the values of
t_{1}^{(\varepsilon)}
,
t_{2}^{(\varepsilon)}
,
t_{1}^{(\varepsilon)\prime}
, and
t_{2}^{(\varepsilon)\prime}
in the following table (see the definition in Definition-Proposition
2.13
(3)).

s_{\varepsilon}=0

1\leq s_{\varepsilon}\leq p-3-a

s_{\varepsilon}=p-2-a

s_{\varepsilon}\geq p-1-a

t_{1}^{(\varepsilon)}

\delta_{\varepsilon}

s_{\varepsilon}+\delta_{\varepsilon}

p-2-a+\delta_{\varepsilon}

a+s_{\varepsilon}+\delta_{\varepsilon}-p+2

t_{2}^{(\varepsilon)}

a+\delta_{\varepsilon}+2

a+s_{\varepsilon}+\delta_{\varepsilon}+2

p+\delta_{\varepsilon}

s_{\varepsilon}+\delta_{\varepsilon}+1

t_{1}^{(\varepsilon)^{\prime}}

a+\delta_{\varepsilon}+2

s+\delta_{\varepsilon}

\delta_{\varepsilon}-1

a+s_{\varepsilon}+\delta_{\varepsilon}-p+2

t_{2}^{(\varepsilon)^{\prime}}

p+1+\delta_{\varepsilon}

a+s_{\varepsilon}+\delta_{\varepsilon}+2

p-2-a+\delta_{\varepsilon}

s_{\varepsilon}+\delta_{\varepsilon}+1

This together with Definition-Proposition
2.13
(3) (and (
2.16.1
)) implies the following.

•

When
s_{\varepsilon}\not\in\{0,p-2-a\}
,
t_{i}^{(\varepsilon)}=t_{i}^{(\varepsilon)\prime}
for
i=1,2
. So for every
k=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{\bullet}
as above,
d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})=d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}\prime}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})
and
d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})=d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}\prime}(\varepsilon_{1})
. This implies that
G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w,t)=G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w,t)
.

•

When
s_{\varepsilon}=0
, we have
\varepsilon=1\times\omega^{a}
. In this case,
t_{1}^{(\varepsilon)\prime}=t_{2}^{(\varepsilon)}
, yet
t_{2}^{(\varepsilon)\prime}=t_{1}^{(\varepsilon)}+p+1
, and
\delta^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}=\delta_{\varepsilon}+1
. It follows that for every
k=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{\bullet}
as above,

d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})=d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}\prime}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})+2\quad\textrm{and}\quad d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})=d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}\prime}(\varepsilon_{1})+1.

This implies that
m_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(k)=m_{n+1}^{(\varepsilon)\prime}(k)
. It follows that
G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(1\times\omega^{a})}(w,t)=1+tG_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}}^{(1\times\omega^{a})}(w,t)
.

•

When
s_{\varepsilon}=p-2-a
,
\varepsilon=\omega^{a+1}\times\omega^{-1}
. In this case, the role of
\bar{r}_{p}
and
\bar{r}_{p}^{\prime}
are somewhat swapped, and we deduce that

d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}\prime}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})=d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})+2\quad\textrm{and}\quad d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}\prime}(\varepsilon_{1})=d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})+1.

This implies that
G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}}^{(\omega^{a+1}\times\omega^{-1})}(w,t)=1+tG_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\omega^{a+1}\times\omega^{-1})}(w,t)
.

Part (2) of the Proposition follows from (1) immediately.
∎

The slopes predicted by ghost series also satisfy properties analogous to the theta maps and the Atkin–Lehner involutions, as stated below.

Proposition 2.17
.

Let
\varepsilon
be a character of
\Delta^{2}
relevant to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
.
For
k=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{\bullet}
, write

(2.17.1)

g^{(\varepsilon)}_{n,\hat{k}}(w):=g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)\big/(w-w_{k})^{m_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(k)}.

Fix
k_{0}\geq 2
. Write
d:=d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\varepsilon\cdot(1\times\omega^{2-k_{0}}))
in this proposition.

(1)

(Compatibility with theta maps)
Put
\varepsilon^{\prime}:=\varepsilon\cdot(\omega^{k_{0}-1}\times\omega^{1-k_{0}})
with
s_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}=\{s_{\varepsilon}+1-k_{0}\}
. For every
\ell\geq 1
, the
(d+\ell)
th slope of
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{k_{0}},-))
is
k_{0}-1
plus the
\ell
th slope of
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon^{\prime})}(w_{2-k_{0}},-))
.
In particular, the
(d+\ell)
th slope of
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{k_{0}},-))
is at least
{k_{0}}-1
.

(2)

(Compatibility with Atkin–Lehner involutions)
Assume that
{k_{0}}\not\equiv k_{\varepsilon}\bmod{(p-1)}
.
Put
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}=\omega^{-s_{\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}}}\times\omega^{a+s_{\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}}}
with
s_{\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}}:=\{{k_{0}}-2-a-s_{\varepsilon}\}
. Then for every
\ell\in\{1,\dots,d\}
, the sum of the
\ell
th slope of
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{k_{0}},-))
and the
(d-\ell+1)
th slope of
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})}(w_{k_{0}},-))
is exactly
{k_{0}}-1
.
In particular, the
\ell
th slope of
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{k_{0}},-))
is at most
k_{0}-1
.

In the rest of this proposition, we will fix the character
\varepsilon
of
\Delta^{2}
and suppress it from the notations.

(3)

(Compatibility with
p
-stabilizations)
Assume that
{k_{0}}=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{0\bullet}
. Then for every
\ell\in\{1,\dots,d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})\}
, the sum of the
\ell
th slope of
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(w_{k_{0}},-))
and the
(d-\ell+1)
th slope of
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(w_{k_{0}},-))
is exactly
{k_{0}}-1
.

(4)

(Gouvêa’s inequality) Assume that
{k_{0}}=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{0\bullet}
. Then the first
d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})
slopes of
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(w_{k_{0}},-))
are all less than or equal to

(2.17.2)

\frac{p-1}{2}(d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})-1)-\delta_{\varepsilon}+\beta_{[d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})-1]}\leq\Big\lfloor\frac{k_{0}-1-\min\{a+1,p-2-a\}}{p+1}\Big\rfloor,

where we set
\beta_{[n]}=\begin{cases}t_{1}&\textrm{if }n\textrm{ is even}\\
t_{2}-\tfrac{p+1}{2}&\textrm{if }n\textrm{ is odd}.\end{cases}

(5)

(Ghost duality)
Assume
{k_{0}}=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{0\bullet}
.
Then for each
\ell=0,\dots,\frac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{new}}(\varepsilon_{1})-1
,

(2.17.3)

v_{p}\big(g_{d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})-d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})-\ell,\hat{k}_{0}}(w_{k_{0}})\big)-v_{p}\big(g_{d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})+\ell,\hat{k}_{0}}(w_{k_{0}})\big)=({k_{0}}-2)\cdot(\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{new}}(\varepsilon_{1})-\ell).

In particular, the
(d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})+1)
th to the
(d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})-d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1}))
th slopes of
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(w_{k_{0}},-))
are all equal to
\frac{{k_{0}}-2}{2}
.

Proof.

(1), (2), (3), and (5) are
[
LTXZ

24

, Proposition 4.18(1)(2)(3)(4)]
, respectively. (4) is
[
LTXZ

24

, Proposition 4.28]
.
∎

Definition-Proposition 2.18
.

Let
{k_{0}}=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{0\bullet}
. We set

(2.18.1)

\Delta^{\prime(\varepsilon)}_{k_{0},\ell}:=v_{p}\big(g^{(\varepsilon)}_{\frac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\varepsilon_{1})+\ell,\hat{k}_{0}}(w_{k_{0}})\big)-\tfrac{k_{0}-2}{2}\ell,\quad\textrm{for }\ell=-\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{new}}(\varepsilon_{1}),\dots,\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{new}}(\varepsilon_{1}).

Let
\underline{\Delta}_{k_{0}}^{(\varepsilon)}
denote the convex hull of the points
(\ell,\Delta^{\prime(\varepsilon)}_{k_{0},\ell})
for
\ell=-\frac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{new}}(\varepsilon_{1}),\dots,\frac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{new}}(\varepsilon_{1})
, and let
(\ell,\Delta^{(\varepsilon)}_{k_{0},\ell})
denote the corresponding points on
\underline{\Delta}^{(\varepsilon)}_{k_{0}}
. Then we have

(2.18.2)

\Delta^{\prime(\varepsilon)}_{k_{0},\ell}=\Delta^{\prime(\varepsilon)}_{{k_{0}},-\ell}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\Delta^{(\varepsilon)}_{k_{0},\ell}=\Delta^{(\varepsilon)}_{{k_{0}},-\ell}\quad\textrm{ for all }\ell=-\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{new}}(\varepsilon_{1}),\dots,\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{new}}(\varepsilon_{1}).

Proof.

This is a corollary of (5); see
[
LTXZ

24

, Notation 5.1]
for more discussion.
∎

In
[
LTXZ

24

, § 5]
, we carefully studied the properties of the vertices of the Newton polygon of ghost series. We record the main definitions and results here, with a minor generalization: we allow the point
w_{\star}
to be in an
arbitrary
algebraically closed complete valued field
\mathbf{C}_{p}
containing
\mathbb{C}_{p}
. (See the proof of Corollary
2.27
for the reason of this change.)

Definition 2.19
.

(
[
LTXZ

24

, Definition 5.11]
)

Let
\mathbf{C}_{p}
be an algebraically closed complete valued field containing
E
; write
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{C}_{p}}
for its valuation ring and
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbf{C}_{p}}
the maximal ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{C}_{p}}
.
For
{k}=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{\bullet}
and
w_{\star}\in\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbf{C}_{p}}
, let
L^{(\varepsilon)}_{w_{\star},k}
denote the largest number (if it exists) in
\{1,\dots,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}(\varepsilon_{1})\}
such that

(2.19.1)

v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})\geq\Delta^{(\varepsilon)}_{k,L^{(\varepsilon)}_{w_{\star},k}}-\Delta^{(\varepsilon)}_{k,L^{(\varepsilon)}_{w_{\star},k}-1}.

When such
L^{(\varepsilon)}_{w_{\star},k}
exists, we call the intervals

\mathrm{nS}_{w_{\star},k}^{(\varepsilon)}:=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})-L^{(\varepsilon)}_{w_{\star},k},\,\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})+L^{(\varepsilon)}_{w_{\star},k}\big)\subset\overline{\mathrm{nS}}_{w_{\star},k}^{(\varepsilon)}:=\big[\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})-L^{(\varepsilon)}_{w_{\star},k},\,\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})+L^{(\varepsilon)}_{w_{\star},k}\big]

the
near-Steinberg range
for
(w_{\star},k)
. When no such
L^{(\varepsilon)}_{w_{\star},k}
exists, write
\mathrm{nS}_{w_{\star},k}^{(\varepsilon)}=\overline{\mathrm{nS}}_{w_{\star},k}^{(\varepsilon)}=\emptyset
.

For a positive integer
n
, we say
(\varepsilon,w_{\star},n)
or simply
(w_{\star},n)
is
near-Steinberg
if
n
belongs to the near-Steinberg range
\mathrm{nS}_{w_{\star},k}^{(\varepsilon)}
for some
k
.

Proposition 2.20
.

(1)

For a fixed
w_{\star}\in\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbf{C}_{p}}
and for any
k^{\prime}=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k^{\prime}_{\bullet}\neq k
and
v_{p}(w_{k^{\prime}}-w_{k})\geq\Delta^{(\varepsilon)}_{k,L_{w_{\star},k}}-\Delta^{(\varepsilon)}_{k,L_{w_{\star},k}-1}
, we have the following exclusion

\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}\notin\overline{\mathrm{nS}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{w_{\star},k}\quad\textrm{and}\quad d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{ur}},d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}-d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{ur}}\notin\mathrm{nS}^{(\varepsilon)}_{w_{\star},k}.

(2)

For a fixed
w_{\star}\in\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbf{C}_{p}}
and every
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, the point
\big(n,v_{p}(g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star}))\big)
is a vertex of
\NP(G^{(\varepsilon)}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(w_{\star},-))
if and only if
(\varepsilon,w_{\star},n)
is not near-Steinberg.

(3)

For a fixed
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, the set of elements
w_{\star}\in\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbf{C}_{p}}
for which
\big(n,v_{p}(g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star}))\big)
is a vertex of
\NP\big(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star},-)\big)
form a quasi-Stein subdomain
\mathrm{Vtx}_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}\subseteq\mathcal{W}^{(\varepsilon)}
:

\mathrm{Vtx}_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(\mathbf{C}_{p}):=\mathcal{W}^{(\varepsilon)}(\mathbf{C}_{p})\backslash\bigcup_{k}\Big\{w_{\star}\in\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbf{C}_{p}}\;\Big|\;v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})\geq\Delta^{(\varepsilon)}_{k,|\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})-n|+1}-\Delta^{(\varepsilon)}_{k,|\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})-n|}\Big\},

where the (finite) union is taken over all
k=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{\bullet}
such that
n\in\big(d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1}),d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})-d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})\big)
.

(4)

For a fixed
w_{\star}\in\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbf{C}_{p}}
, the set of near-Steinberg ranges
\mathrm{nS}^{(\varepsilon)}_{w_{\star},k}
for all
k
is nested, i.e. for any two such open near-Steinberg ranges, either they are disjoint or one is contained in another.

A near-Steinberg range
\mathrm{nS}^{(\varepsilon)}_{w_{\star},k}
is called
maximal
if it is not contained in other near-Steinberg ranges.
Over a maximal near-Steinberg range, the slope of
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star},-))
belongs to

(2.20.1)

\tfrac{a}{2}+\mathbb{Z}+\mathbb{Z}\big(\max\{v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k^{\prime}})|w_{k^{\prime}}\text{~is a zero of~}g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)\text{~for some~}n\in\mathrm{nS}^{(\varepsilon)}_{w_{\star},k}\}\big).

(5)

For
{k_{0}}=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{0\bullet}
, the following are equivalent for
\ell\in\{0,\dots,\frac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{new}}(\varepsilon_{1})-1\}
.

(a)

The point
(\ell,\Delta^{\prime(\varepsilon)}_{{k_{0}},\ell})
is not a vertex of
\underline{\Delta}_{k_{0}}^{(\varepsilon)}
,

(b)

\frac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})+\ell\in\mathrm{nS}_{w_{k_{0}},k_{1}}
for some
k_{1}>{k_{0}}
, and

(c)

\frac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})-\ell\in\mathrm{nS}_{w_{k_{0}},k_{2}}
for some
k_{2}<{k_{0}}
.

(6)

For any
{k_{0}}=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{0\bullet}
and any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, the slopes of
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{k},-))
and of
\underline{\Delta}^{(\varepsilon)}_{k_{0}}
with multiplicity one belong to
\mathbb{Z}
; other slopes all have even multiplicity and the slopes belong to
\frac{a}{2}+\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

All of the results essentially follow from
[
LTXZ

24

]
, except that they are proved for
\mathbb{C}_{p}
in places of a general
\mathbf{C}_{p}
. But all the proofs carry over word-by-word the same.

(1) is
[
LTXZ

24

, Proposition 5.16(1)]
. (2) is
[
LTXZ

24

, Theorem 5.19(2)]
. (3) follows from (2) and Definition
2.19
: a point
(\varepsilon,w_{\star},n)
is near-Steinberg if and only if

n\in\mathrm{nS}_{w_{\star},k}^{(\varepsilon)}=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})-L^{(\varepsilon)}_{w_{\star},k},\,\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})+L^{(\varepsilon)}_{w_{\star},k}\big),

or equivalently,
|n-\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})|<L^{(\varepsilon)}_{w_{\star},k}
, for some
k=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{\bullet}
; by (
2.19.1
), this is further equivalent to

v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})\geq\Delta^{(\varepsilon)}_{k,|\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})-n|+1}-\Delta^{(\varepsilon)}_{k,|\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})-n|}.

(4) is a reformulation of
[
LTXZ

24

, Theorem 5.19(1)(3)]
. (5) is
[
LTXZ

24

, Proposition 5.26]
. (6) combines
[
LTXZ

24

, Corollary 5.24 and Proposition 5.26]
.
∎

Remark 2.21
.

By
[
LTXZ

24

, Lemma 5.2]
, asymptotically,

\Delta_{k,\ell+1}^{(\varepsilon)}-\Delta_{k,\ell}^{(\varepsilon)}\sim\frac{p-1}{2}\ell
(when
\ell
is large).
Intuitively and roughly, the set of vertices
\mathrm{Vtx}_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}
in Proposition
2.20
(3) is to remove from the open unit disk
\mathcal{W}^{(\varepsilon)}
, a disk of radius about
p^{-(a+2)}
or
p^{a+1-p}
, centered at
w_{k_{\mathrm{mid}}^{(\varepsilon)}(n)}
, two disks of radius roughly
p^{1-p}
, centered at
w_{k_{\mathrm{mid}}^{(\varepsilon)}(n)\pm(p-1)}
, and two disks of radius roughly
p^{(1-p)\ell/2}
, centered at
w_{k_{\mathrm{mid}}^{(\varepsilon)}(n)\pm\ell(p-1)}
, for each
\ell=3,4,\dots,\frac{p-3}{2(p+1)}n+O(1)
, where
k_{\mathrm{mid}}^{(\varepsilon)}(n)
is the unique positive integer
k\equiv k_{\varepsilon}\bmod(p-1)
such that
\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})=n
.

The following is a technical estimate
[
LTXZ

24

, Corollary 5.10]
on the difference of
\Delta
’s that we will frequently use in this paper.

Proposition 2.22
.

Assume
p\geq 7
. Take integers
\ell,\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}\in\{0,1,\dots,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}(\varepsilon_{1})\}
with
\ell\leq\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell^{\prime\prime}
and
\ell^{\prime\prime}>\ell
. Assume further that
(\ell,\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime})\neq(0,1,1)
.
Let
k^{\prime}=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k^{\prime}_{\bullet}
be a weight such that

(2.22.1)

d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1}),\textrm{ or }d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})-d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})\textrm{ belongs to }\big[\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})-\ell^{\prime},\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})+\ell^{\prime}\big],

then we have

\Delta_{k,\ell^{\prime\prime}}^{(\varepsilon)}-\Delta^{\prime(\varepsilon)}_{k,\ell}-(\ell^{\prime\prime}-\ell^{\prime})\cdot v_{p}(w_{k}-w_{k^{\prime}})\geq(\ell^{\prime}-\ell)\cdot\Big\lfloor\frac{\ln((p+1)\ell^{\prime\prime})}{\ln p}+1\Big\rfloor+\frac{1}{2}\big(\ell^{\prime\prime 2}-\ell^{2}\big).

In particular, for all
\ell^{\prime\prime}>\ell\geq 0
we have

(2.22.2)

\Delta_{k,\ell^{\prime\prime}}^{(\varepsilon)}-\Delta^{\prime(\varepsilon)}_{k,\ell}\geq\frac{1}{2}\big(\ell^{\prime\prime 2}-\ell^{2}\big)+1.

Remark 2.23
.

As pointed out by
[
LTXZ

24

, Corollary 5.10]
, if there exists
k^{\prime}
such that
v_{p}(w_{k^{\prime}}-w_{k})\geq\big\lfloor\frac{\ln((p+1)\ell^{\prime\prime})}{\ln p}+2\big\rfloor
, then there are at most two such
k^{\prime}
satisfying
v_{p}(w_{k^{\prime}}-w_{k})\geq\big\lfloor\frac{\ln((p+1)\ell^{\prime\prime})}{\ln p}+2\big\rfloor
and (
2.22.1
) with
\ell^{\prime}
replaced by
\ell^{\prime\prime}
. In the case of having two such
k^{\prime}
’s, say
k^{\prime}_{1},k^{\prime}_{2}
; up to swapping
k^{\prime}_{1}
and
k^{\prime}_{2}
, we have
d_{k^{\prime}_{1}}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1}),d_{k^{\prime}_{2}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})-d_{k^{\prime}_{2}}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})\in\big(\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})-\ell^{\prime\prime},\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})+\ell^{\prime\prime}\big)
; and between
d_{k^{\prime}_{1}}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})
and
d_{k^{\prime}_{2}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})-d_{k^{\prime}_{2}}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})
, one is
\geq\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})
and the other is
\leq\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})
.

For later argument, we give a criterion to verify the inequality
v_{p}(w_{k^{\prime}}-w_{k})\leq\big\lfloor\frac{\ln((p+1)\ell^{\prime\prime})}{\ln p}+1\big\rfloor
for
\ell^{\prime\prime}=\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}
.

Lemma 2.24
.

Let
k=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{\bullet}
and
k^{\prime}=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k^{\prime}_{\bullet}
be two distinct weights. Assume
d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}>0
and set
\gamma\coloneqq\lfloor\frac{\ln((p+1)(\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}))}{\ln p}+1\rfloor
. Then we have
v_{p}(w_{k}-w_{k^{\prime}})\leq\gamma
when any one of the following conditions holds:

(1)

\frac{1}{2}d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}\in[d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}},d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}-d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}]
;

(2)

k^{\prime}_{\bullet}<k_{\bullet}
;

(3)

d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{ur}}\in[d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}},\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}})
.

Proof.

In all three cases, it suffices to prove
1+\lfloor\frac{\ln|k_{\bullet}-k^{\prime}_{\bullet}|}{\ln p}\rfloor\leq\gamma
, or equivalently,

(2.24.1)

\Big\lfloor\tfrac{\ln|k_{\bullet}-k^{\prime}_{\bullet}|}{\ln p}\Big\rfloor\leq\Big\lfloor\tfrac{\ln\big((p+1)\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}\big)}{\ln p}\Big\rfloor.

(1) By Definition-Proposition
2.13
(5) and
\frac{1}{2}d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}\in[d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}},d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}-d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}]
, we have
|k_{\bullet}-k^{\prime}_{\bullet}|=|\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}-\frac{1}{2}d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}
. (
2.24.1
) is clear.

(2) (
2.24.1
) holds trivially for
k_{\bullet}<p
so we assume
k_{\bullet}\geq p
. Definition-Proposition
2.13
(5) implies that
(p+1)\cdot\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}\geq(p-1)k_{\bullet}-(p+1)\geq k_{\bullet}
, which further implies (
2.24.1
).

(3) If
d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{ur}}=d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}
, by Definition-Proposition
2.13
(5) we have
|k_{\bullet}-k^{\prime}_{\bullet}|\leq p+1
and hence
\lfloor\frac{\ln|k_{\bullet}-k^{\prime}_{\bullet}|}{\ln p}\rfloor\leq 1
. So (
2.24.1
) holds trivially in this case (as
d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}\neq 0
in this case).

If
d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{ur}}>d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}
, then we have
k^{\prime}_{\bullet}>k_{\bullet}
. Again by Definition-Proposition
2.13
(5) we have
\frac{2}{p+1}k^{\prime}_{\bullet}\leq d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{ur}}\leq\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}-1\leq k_{\bullet}
and hence
k^{\prime}_{\bullet}-k_{\bullet}\leq\frac{p-1}{2}k_{\bullet}
. On the other hand, as observed in (2),
(p+1)\cdot\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}\geq(p-1)k_{\bullet}-(p+1)
. By the assumption
p\geq 11
, we have
\frac{p-1}{2}k_{\bullet}\leq(p-1)k_{\bullet}-(p+1)
when
k_{\bullet}\geq 3
and (
2.24.1
) holds in this case. When
k_{\bullet}\leq 2
, we have
k^{\prime}_{\bullet}-k_{\bullet}\leq p-1
and thus
\lfloor\frac{\ln|k_{\bullet}-k^{\prime}_{\bullet}|}{\ln p}\rfloor=0
, so (
2.24.1
) still holds. This completes the proof of (
2.24.1
).
∎

Before concluding this section, we briefly touch upon some compactness argument using Berkovich spaces. The main result Corollary
2.27
will be useful later in Sections
8
and
9
.

Notation 2.25
.

For a rigid analytic space
Z
over a complete valued field extension
K
of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}

, write
Z^{\mathrm{Berk}}
for the associated Berkovich space.
For an analytic function
f
on
Z
and a point
z\in Z^{\mathrm{Berk}}
, we put

v_{p}(f(z)):=\ln|f|_{z}\big/\ln|p|_{z}\in\mathbb{R};

then
v_{p}(f(-))
is a continuous function on
Z^{\mathrm{Berk}}
.

Let
\mathbb{A}^{1,\mathrm{rig}}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}(\Spm\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle p^{n}t\rangle)
denote the rigid affine line.

For a power series
F(t)=1+f_{1}t+f_{2}t^{2}+\cdots\in\mathcal{O}(Z)\llbracket t\rrbracket
and a point
z\in Z^{\mathrm{Berk}}
, we may define the Newton polygon
\NP\big(F(z,-)\big)
to be the convex hull of
(0,0)
and
\big(n,v_{p}(f_{n}(z))\big)
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. For
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, write
\NP\big(F(z,-)\big)_{x=n}
for the value of the polygon when
x=n
.

We say that
F(t)
is a
Fredholm series
if it converges on
Z\times\mathbb{A}^{1,\mathrm{rig}}
.

Lemma 2.26
.

Let
F(t)=1+f_{1}t+\cdots\in\mathcal{O}(Z)\llbracket t\rrbracket
be a Fredholm series over an affinoid rigid analytic space
Z
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
such that for each closed point
z\in Z(\mathbb{C}_{p})
,
F(t)(z)
is not a polynomial (i.e. for any
n\geq 1
, the functions
f_{n}(z),f_{n+1}(z),\dots
have no common zero on
Z
).
Then for every
n_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, the function
\mathsf{z}\mapsto\NP\big(F(\mathsf{z},-)\big)_{x=n_{0}}
is a continuous function on
Z^{\mathrm{Berk}}
.

Proof.

(1) Put
f_{0}=1
. For each
\mathsf{z}\in Z^{\mathrm{Berk}}
, the value of
\NP(F(\mathsf{z},-))_{x=n_{0}}
is equal to

(2.26.1)

\min\bigg\{f_{n_{0}}(\mathsf{z}),\ \min_{0\leq n_{-}<n_{0}<n_{+}}\frac{(n_{0}-n_{-})v_{p}(f_{n_{+}}(\mathsf{z}))+(n_{+}-n_{0})v_{p}(f_{n_{-}}(\mathsf{z}))}{n_{+}-n_{-}}\bigg\}.

It suffices to prove that the above minimum is essentially a finite minimum.

The condition on
F(t)
implies that the ideal
(f_{n_{0}},f_{n_{1}},\dots)=(1)
. This implies that there exists
n_{1}\geq n_{0}
and functions
h_{n_{0}},h_{n_{0}+1},\dots,h_{n_{1}}\in\mathcal{O}(Z)
such that

f_{n_{0}}g_{n_{0}}+f_{n_{0}+1}h_{n_{0}+1}+\cdots+f_{n_{1}}h_{n_{1}}=1.

Fix a Banach norm
||\cdot||_{Z}
on
\mathcal{O}(Z)
.
There exists
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
such that
||h_{i}||_{Z}\leq p^{M}
for every
i=n_{0},\dots,n_{1}
. It then follows that, for every
z\in Z^{\mathrm{Berk}}
, there exists at least one
n\in\{n_{0},\dots,n_{1}\}
such that
v_{p}(h_{n}(\mathsf{z}))\leq M
.

As
F(t)
converges on
Z\times\mathbb{A}^{1,\mathrm{rig}}
, there exists
N>n_{1}
such that whenever
n^{\prime}\geq N
,
||f_{n^{\prime}}||_{Z}\leq p^{-n^{\prime}M}
. Then for each
\mathsf{z}\in Z^{\mathrm{Berk}}
, take the
n
above so that
v_{p}(h_{n}(\mathsf{z}))\leq M
, then whenever
n_{+}\geq N>n_{0}>n_{-}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{(n_{0}-n_{-})v_{p}(f_{n_{+}}(\mathsf{z}))+(n_{+}-n_{0})v_{p}(f_{n_{-}}(\mathsf{z}))}{n_{+}-n_{-}}

\displaystyle\geq\

\displaystyle\frac{(n_{0}-n_{-})\cdot n_{+}M}{n_{+}-n_{-}}\geq M\geq v_{p}(f_{n}(\mathsf{z}))\geq\frac{n_{0}\cdot v_{p}(f_{n}(\mathsf{z}))}{n}\geq\eqref{E:NP at x=n0}.

Thus, for the minimum in (
2.26.1
), it suffices to take it over all
n_{+}<N
. So (
2.26.1
) is essentially a finite minimum and thus it is continuous.
∎

Now, we come back to ghost series to record the following “compactness argument”.

Corollary 2.27
.

Fix
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. Let
\mathbf{C}_{p}
be an complete algebraically closed valued field.

(1)

For every Berkovich point
\mathsf{w}\in\mathrm{Vtx}_{n,\mathbf{C}_{p}}^{(\varepsilon),\mathrm{Berk}}
,
(n,v_{p}(g_{n}(\mathsf{w})))
is a vertex of
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon)}(\mathsf{w},-))
.

(2)

Write
\mathrm{Vtx}_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}
as a union

\mathrm{Vtx}_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}=\bigcup_{\delta\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0},\,\delta\to 0^{+}}\mathrm{Vtx}_{n}^{(\varepsilon),\delta}\quad\textrm{with}

\mathrm{Vtx}_{n}^{(\varepsilon),\delta}:=\Bigg\{w_{\star}\in\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{C}_{p}}\;\Bigg|\;\begin{array}[]{l}v_{p}(w_{\star})\geq\delta,\textrm{ and for each $k=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{\bullet}$ with $k_{\bullet}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}$}\\
\textrm{such that }n\in\big(d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1}),d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})-d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})\big),\textrm{ we have}\\
v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})\leq\Delta^{(\varepsilon)}_{k,|\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})-n|+1}-\Delta^{(\varepsilon)}_{k,|\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})-n|}-\delta.\end{array}\Bigg\}.

Then for any
\delta>0
, there exists
\epsilon_{\delta}>0
such that for every point
\mathsf{w}\in\mathrm{Vtx}_{,\mathbf{C}_{p}}^{(\varepsilon),\delta,\mathrm{Berk}}
, the difference between the
n
th and the
(n+1)
th slope of
\NP(G^{(\varepsilon)}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\mathsf{w},-))
is at least
\epsilon_{\delta}
.

Proof.

(1)
Let
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p}
be a completed algebraic closure of the residue field at
\mathsf{w}
; then there exists a
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p}
-point
\tilde{\mathsf{w}}
of
\mathrm{Vtx}_{n,\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p}}^{(\varepsilon),\delta}
whose image in
\mathrm{Vtx}_{n,\mathbf{C}_{p}}^{(\varepsilon),\delta,\mathrm{Berk}}
is equal to
\mathsf{w}
. Moreover, we have
v_{p}(g_{i}(\mathsf{w}))=v_{p}(g_{i}(\tilde{\mathsf{w}}))
for every
i
. By Proposition
2.20
(3) applied to the
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p}
-point
\tilde{\mathsf{w}}
, we see that
(n,v_{p}(g_{n}(\tilde{\mathsf{w}})))
is a vertex of
\NP(G^{(\varepsilon)}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\tilde{\mathsf{w}},-))
; so the same is true for
\tilde{\mathsf{w}}
in place of
\mathsf{w}
.

(2) Note that the Berkovich space
\mathrm{Vtx}_{n,\mathbf{C}_{p}}^{(\varepsilon),\delta,\mathrm{Berk}}
is
compact
and by (1) the difference between the
(n+1)
th and the
n
th slope of
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon)}(\mathsf{w},-))
is strictly positive for every Berkovich point
\mathsf{w}\in\mathrm{Vtx}_{n,\mathbf{C}_{p}}^{(\varepsilon),\delta,\mathrm{Berk}}
. Part (2) now follows from the continuity of the Newton polygon as
\mathsf{w}
varies, proved in Lemma
2.26
.
∎

Remark 2.28
.

One can probably establish an effective version of Corollary
2.27
(2) for
\epsilon_{\delta}
if one dives into the proof of Proposition
2.20
(3) in
[
LTXZ

24

, Proposition 5.19(2)]
.

3.
Two key inputs on abstract classical forms

In this section, we give the two key inputs for our proof of local version of ghost conjecture:

(1) The first one is a careful study of the
p
-stabilization of abstract classical forms initiated in §
3.3
. The key feature of
p
-stabilization given in Proposition
3.5
allows to deduce a corank result for principal minors (cf. Corollary
3.11
) and non-principal minors (cf. Definition-Proposition
3.23
) of
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}
. This gives a philosophical explanation of the construction of ghost series;

(2) The second one is to use the modified Mahler basis to give an estimate of
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}
. We introduce the modified Mahler basis in §
3.14
. Then we give an estimate on the change of basis matrix between the modified Mahler basis and power basis in Lemma
3.17
and an estimate of matrix of the
U_{p}
-operator with respect to the modfifeid Mahler basis in Corollary
3.29
. Later in §
5
we will combine these two estimates together to give an estimate of
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}
.

Notation 3.1
.

In this section, we keep Hypothesis
2.10
:
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
is a primitive
\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{K}_{p}\rrbracket
-projective augmented module of type
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\Sym^{a}\mathbb{F}^{\oplus 2}
(with
1\leq a\leq p-4
) on which
{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p&0\\
0&p\end{smallmatrix}\big)}
acts trivially.

We always use
\varepsilon
to denote a character
\omega^{-s_{\varepsilon}}\times\omega^{a+s_{\varepsilon}}
of
\Delta^{2}
relevant to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
. When no confusion arises, we suppress
\varepsilon
from the notation in the proofs (but still keep the full notations in the statements), for example, writing
s
,
d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}
, and
d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}
for
s_{\varepsilon}
,
d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})
, and
d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})
, respectively.

Before proceeding, we give a very weak Hodge bound for the matrix
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}
. A much finer estimate will be given later in this section.

Proposition 3.2
.

We have
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}\in\mathrm{M}_{\infty}(\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle)
. More precisely,

(1)

the row of
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}
indexed by
\mathbf{e}
belongs to
p^{\frac{1}{2}\deg\mathbf{e}}\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle
, and

(2)

for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, the row of
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}|_{w=w_{k}}
indexed by
\mathbf{e}
belongs to
p^{\deg\mathbf{e}}\mathcal{O}
.

Proof.

For a monomial
h=z^{m}
and
{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p\alpha&\beta\\
p\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\big)}\in{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p\mathbb{Z}_{p}&\mathbb{Z}_{p}\\
p\mathbb{Z}_{p}&\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}\end{smallmatrix}\big)}
with determinant
pd
for
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, the action (
2.4.4
) is given by

\displaystyle h\big|_{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p\alpha&\beta\\
p\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon(\bar{d}/\bar{\delta},\bar{\delta})\cdot(1+w)^{\log\left((p\gamma z+\delta)/\omega(\bar{\delta})\right)/p}\cdot h\Big(\frac{p\alpha z+\beta}{p\gamma z+\delta}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon(\bar{d}/\bar{\delta},\bar{\delta})\cdot\sum_{n\geq 0}w^{n}\binom{\log\left((p\gamma z+\delta)/\omega(\bar{\delta})\right)/p}{n}\cdot h\Big(\frac{p\alpha z+\beta}{p\gamma z+\delta}\Big).

Note that
\frac{w^{n}}{n!}=(\frac{w}{p})^{n}\cdot\frac{p^{n/2}}{n!}\cdot p^{n/2}
.
So it is not difficult to see that the above expression belongs to
\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle\langle p^{1/2}z\rangle
. Part (1) of the proposition follows.

When
w=w_{k}
, we can rewrite the above equality as

h\big|_{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p\alpha&\beta\\
p\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}(z)=\varepsilon(\bar{d}/\bar{\delta},\bar{\delta})\Big(\frac{p\gamma z+\delta}{\omega(\bar{\delta})}\Big)^{k-2}\cdot h\Big(\frac{p\alpha z+\beta}{p\gamma z+\delta}\Big)\in\mathcal{O}\llbracket pz\rrbracket.

From this, we see that the row of
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}|_{w=w_{k}}
indexed by
\mathbf{e}
belongs to
p^{\deg\mathbf{e}}\mathcal{O}
.
∎

3.3.
p
-stabilization process

Recall from Proposition
2.12
(2) the natural Atkin–Lehner involution

\mathrm{AL}_{(k,\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})}:\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})\longrightarrow\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1}).

We define the following four maps

\textstyle{\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})=\Hom_{\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{K}_{p}\rrbracket}\big(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}},\,\mathcal{O}[z]^{\leq k-2}\otimes\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1}\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{2}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})=\Hom_{\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{Iw}_{p}\rrbracket}\big(\widetilde{\mathrm{H}},\,\mathcal{O}[z]^{\leq k-2}\otimes\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1}\big)}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{proj}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{proj}_{2}}

given by, for
\psi\in\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})
,
\varphi\in\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})
, and
x\in\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
,

\displaystyle\iota_{1}(\psi)

\displaystyle=\psi.

\displaystyle\iota_{2}(\psi)(x)

\displaystyle=\psi\big(x{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-1}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)}\big)\big|_{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}=\psi\big(x{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0&p^{-1}\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\big)}\big)\big|_{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
p&0\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}=\mathrm{AL}_{(k,\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})}(\iota_{1}(\psi))(x).

\displaystyle\mathrm{proj}_{1}(\varphi)(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0,\dots,p-1,\star}\varphi\big(xu_{j})\big|_{u_{j}^{-1}}.

\displaystyle\mathrm{proj}_{2}(\varphi)(x)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{proj}_{1}(\mathrm{AL}_{(k,\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})}(\varphi))(x)=\sum_{j=0,\dots,p-1,\star}\varphi\big(x{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0&p^{-1}\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\big)}u_{j}\big)\big|_{u_{j}^{-1}{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
p&0\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}.

Here
u_{j}={\big(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
j&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)}
for
j=0,\dots,p-1
and
u_{\star}={\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\big)}
form a set of coset representatives of
\mathrm{Iw}_{p}\backslash\mathrm{K}_{p}
. (In fact, the definitions of
\mathrm{proj}_{1}
and
\mathrm{proj}_{2}
do not depend on this choice of coset representatives.)

Remark 3.4
.

As we will not need it, we leave as an interesting exercise for the readers to check that for
\psi\in\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})
and the
T_{p}
-operator defined in (
2.4.7
), we have

U_{p}(\iota_{1}(\psi))=p\cdot\iota_{2}(\psi)\quad\textrm{and}\quad U_{p}(\iota_{2}(\psi))=\iota_{2}(T_{p}(\psi))-p^{k-2}\iota_{1}(\psi).

It then follows that, if
\psi
is an
T_{p}
-eigenform with eigenvalue
\lambda_{\psi}
, the
U_{p}
-action on the span of
\iota_{2}(\psi)
and
\iota_{1}(\psi)
is given by the matrix

\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{\psi}&p\\
-p^{k-2}&0\end{pmatrix}.

The following is a key (although simple) feature of
p
-stabilization.

Proposition 3.5
.

We have the following equality

(3.5.1)

U_{p}(\varphi)=\iota_{2}(\mathrm{proj}_{1}(\varphi))-\mathrm{AL}_{(k,\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})}(\varphi),\quad\textrm{for all }\varphi\in\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1}).

Proof.

For
\varphi\in\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}
and
x\in\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
, we have

\displaystyle\iota_{2}

\displaystyle(\mathrm{proj}_{1}(\varphi))(x)-\mathrm{AL}_{(k)}(\varphi)(x)=\sum_{j=0,\dots,p-1,\star}\varphi\Big(x{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-1}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)}u_{j}\Big)\Big|_{u_{j}^{-1}{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}-\varphi\Big(x{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0&p^{-1}\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\big)}\Big)\Big|_{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
p&0\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}\varphi\Big(x{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-1}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)}{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
j&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)}\Big)\Big|_{{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
j&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}^{-1}{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}=\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}\varphi\Big(x{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-1}&0\\
j&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)}\Big)\Big|_{{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p^{-1}&0\\
j&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}^{-1}}=U_{p}(\varphi)(x).

Here in the first equality, when we unwind the definition of
\iota_{2}
, we use the matrix
{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)}
as opposed to
{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
p&0\end{smallmatrix}\big)}
(using the
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-equivariance).
The second equality comes from canceling the last term in the first row with the term
j=\star
in the sum.
∎

Proposition 3.6
.

For
{k}=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{\bullet}
, consider the power basis
\mathbf{B}^{(\varepsilon)}_{k}=\{\mathbf{e}_{1}^{(\varepsilon)},\mathbf{e}_{2}^{(\varepsilon)},\dots,\mathbf{e}_{d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})}^{(\varepsilon)}\}
of
\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})
from (
2.11.2
), ordered with increasing degrees. Let
\mathrm{U}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw},(\varepsilon)}
(resp.
\mathrm{L}_{k}^{(\varepsilon),\mathrm{cl}}
) be the matrix of the
U_{p}
-operator (resp. the
\mathrm{AL}_{(k,\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})}
-action) on
\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})
with respect to
\mathbf{B}^{(\varepsilon)}_{k}
, i.e. we have
U_{p}(\mathbf{e}_{1}^{(\varepsilon)},\dots,\mathbf{e}_{d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})}^{(\varepsilon)})=(\mathbf{e}_{1}^{(\varepsilon)},\dots,\mathbf{e}_{d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})}^{(\varepsilon)})\cdot\mathrm{U}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw},(\varepsilon)}
and similarly for
\mathrm{L}_{k}^{(\varepsilon),\mathrm{cl}}
. (The superscript
\mathrm{cl}
indicates that the matrix is for classical forms as opposed to overconvergent ones.)

(1)

The matrix
\mathrm{L}_{k}^{(\varepsilon),\mathrm{cl}}
is the
anti-diagonal
matrix with entries

p^{\deg\mathbf{e}_{1}^{(\varepsilon)}},p^{\deg\mathbf{e}_{2}^{(\varepsilon)}},\dots,p^{\deg\mathbf{e}_{d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})}^{(\varepsilon)}}

from upper right to lower left.

(2)

The matrix
\mathrm{U}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw},(\varepsilon)}
is the sum of

•

the antidiagonal matrix
-\mathrm{L}_{k}^{(\varepsilon),\mathrm{cl}}
above, and

•

a
d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})\times d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})
-matrix with rank
\leq d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})
.

Proof.

(1) is just a special case of Proposition
2.12
(2), when
\psi=\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1}
. (2) follows from (1) and the equality (
3.5.1
), because
\varphi\mapsto\iota_{2}(\mathrm{proj}_{1}(\varphi))
has rank at most
d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}
as it factors through the smaller space
\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}
of rank
d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}
.
∎

Corollary 3.7
.

The multiplicities of
\pm p^{(k-2)/2}
as eigenvalues of the
U_{p}
-action on
\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})
are at least
\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}(\varepsilon_{1})
each.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.6
(1), the matrix
\mathrm{L}_{k}^{\mathrm{cl}}
for the Atkin–Lehner operator is semisimple and has eigenvalues
\pm p^{(k-2)/2}
each with multiplicity
\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}
; so
\mathrm{L}_{k}^{\mathrm{cl}}\pm p^{(k-2)/2}I
has rank exactly
\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}
, where
I
is the
d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}\times d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}
-identity matrix. By Proposition
3.6
(2),
\mathrm{U}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}\pm p^{(k-2)/2}I
has corank at least
\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}-d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}=\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}
. The corollary follows.
∎

Remark 3.8
.

It will follow from our local ghost conjecture Theorem
2.8
together with Proposition
2.12
(4) that the multiplicities of the eigenvalues
\pm p^{(k-2)/2}
are exactly
\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}(\varepsilon_{1})
.

Notation 3.9
.

Here and later, we shall frequently refer to the
corank
of an
n\times n
-matrix
B
; it is
n
minus the rank of
B
.

The following lemma will be used in the proof of weak corank theorem (Corollary
3.11
).

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
\mathrm{U}\in\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}\langle u\rangle)
be a matrix and
u_{0}\in\mathcal{O}
. If the evaluation
\mathrm{U}_{0}:=\mathrm{U}|_{u=u_{0}}\in\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O})
has corank
m
, then
\mathrm{det}(\mathrm{U})
is divisible by
(u-u_{0})^{m}
in
\mathcal{O}\langle u\rangle
.

Proof.

By assumption we can find a matrix
P\in\GL_{n}(\mathcal{O})
such that the entries in the last
m
rows of the matrix
P\mathrm{U}_{0}
are all
0
. By Weierstrass Division Theorem, the entries of the last
m
rows of
P\mathrm{U}
are all divisible by
u-u_{0}
. It follows that
\mathrm{det}(\mathrm{U})
is divisible by
(u-u_{0})^{m}
.
∎

The following statement gives a philosophical explanation of the palindromic pattern of (
2.5.2
) in Definition
2.5
of ghost series.

Corollary 3.11

(Weak corank theorem)
.

If we write
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}(\underline{n})\in\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle)
for the upper left
n\times n
-submatrix of
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}
, then

\mathrm{det}(\mathrm{U}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}(\underline{n}))\in\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle
is divisible by
p^{-\deg g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}}g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)
(inside
\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle
).

Proof.

We need to show that, for each
{k}=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{\bullet}
such that
m_{n}(k)>0
,
\mathrm{det}(\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}(\underline{n}))
is divisible by
(w/p-{w_{k}}/p)^{m_{n}(k)}
. Here we work in the ring
\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle
so we need to divide each ghost factor
w-w_{k}
by
p
.
By Lemma
3.10
(applied to
\mathrm{U}=\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}(\underline{n})
,
u=w/p
, and
u_{0}=w_{k}/p)
, it is enough to show that evaluating
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}(\underline{n})
at
w=w_{k}
, i.e. the matrix
\mathrm{U}_{k}^{\dagger}(\underline{n})
, has corank
\geq m_{n}(k)
. Since
m_{n}(k)>0
, we have
n<d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}
and hence the matrix
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}(\underline{n})=\mathrm{U}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\underline{n})
, where
\mathrm{U}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}
is the matrix defined in Proposition
3.6
, and
\mathrm{U}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\underline{n})
is its upper left
n\times n
-submatrix. We denote

\mathrm{L}^{\mathrm{cl}}_{k}(\underline{n})
in a similar way. By Proposition
3.6
(1)(2),

\rank(\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}_{k}(\underline{n}))\leq d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}+\rank\mathrm{L}^{\mathrm{cl}}_{k}(\underline{n})=\begin{cases}d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}&\textrm{ if }n\leq\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}\\
d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}+2(n-\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}})&\textrm{ if }n\geq\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}.\end{cases}

So the corank of
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}_{k}(\underline{n})
is at least
n-d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}
if
n\leq\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}
, and at least
d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}-d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}-n
if
n\geq\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}
; in other words,
\corank\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}_{k}(\underline{n})\geq m_{n}(k)
. The corollary is proved.
∎

Remark 3.12
.

This corollary seems to have given some theoretical support for the definition of the ghost series, and it already gives us confidence towards proving the local ghost conjecture (Theorem
2.8
). In reality, we still need to combine more sophisticated
p
-adic estimates on the corank argument in the corollary above.

Remark 3.13
.

With some effort using the representation theory of
\mathbb{F}[\GL_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{p})]
and consider the standard Hodge polygon for the power basis, one may show that there exists an
\mathcal{O}
-basis
\mathbf{v}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{v}_{d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}}
of
\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})
such that the following list

p^{-\deg\mathbf{e}_{1}}\iota_{2}(\mathbf{v}_{1}),\,\dots,\,p^{-\deg\mathbf{e}_{d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}}}\iota_{2}(\mathbf{v}_{d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}}),\,\mathbf{e}_{d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}+1},\,\dots,\,\mathbf{e}_{d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}-d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}},\,\iota_{1}(\mathbf{v}_{d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}}),\,\dots,\,\iota_{1}(\mathbf{v}_{1})

forms an
\mathcal{O}
-basis of
\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})
and the
U_{p}
-matrix with respect to this basis belongs to

\hbox to448.9pt{\vbox to145.35pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 235.32538pt\lower-62.18323pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

This refines Remark
3.4
.

3.14.
A modified Mahler basis

We now come to the second key ingredient of the proof of the local ghost conjecture (Theorem
2.8
): an estimate of the
U_{p}
-matrix with respect to the (modified) Mahler basis. This will improve Corollary
3.11
on the exponents of
p
.

The same technique was used in
[
LWX17
]
to prove the spectral halo conjecture of Coleman–Mazur–Buzzard–Kilford (over the boundary annulus of the weight space:
(\Spf\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket w,p/w\rrbracket)^{\mathrm{rig}}
). There are two minor modifications we employ here:

(1) Our estimate will be on
\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle
, so we use
p
as the “anchor uniformizer” as opposed to
w
;

(2) The usual Mahler basis
1,z,\binom{z}{2},\dots
does not behave well under the
\bar{\mathrm{T}}
-action; so we modified the Mahler basis as follows.

Consider the following iteratively defined polynomials

(3.14.1)

f(z)=f_{1}(z):=\frac{z^{p}-z}{p},\quad f_{i+1}(z):=f\big(f_{i}(z)\big)=\frac{f_{i}(z)^{p}-f_{i}(z)}{p}\textrm{ for }i=1,2,\dots.

For example,
f_{2}(z)=\dfrac{\big((z^{p}-z)/p\big)^{p}-(z^{p}-z)/p}{p}
. It is clear that every
f_{i}(z)
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-values continuous function on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, i.e.
f_{i}(z)\in\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathbb{Z}_{p};\mathbb{Z}_{p})
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we write it in its base
p
expansion
n=n_{0}+pn_{1}+p^{2}n_{2}+\cdots
with
n_{i}\in\{0,\dots,p-1\}
and define the
n
th modified Mahler basis
element to be

(3.14.2)

\mathbf{m}_{n}(z):=z^{n_{0}}f_{1}(z)^{n_{1}}f_{2}(z)^{n_{2}}\cdots\in\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathbb{Z}_{p};\mathbb{Z}_{p}).

Roughly speaking, one may think of this basis element
\mathbf{m}_{n}(z)
as taking the “main terms” in the binomial function
\binom{z}{n}
.

Lemma 3.15
.

(1)

For every
n=\sum\limits_{i\geq 0}p^{i}n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
as above, the degree of each nonzero monomial term in
\mathbf{m}_{n}(z)
is congruent to
n
modulo
p-1
and the leading coefficient of
\mathbf{m}_{n}(z)
is

(3.15.1)

p^{-\sum\limits_{i\geq 1}n_{i}(1+p+\cdots+p^{i-1})}\in(n!)^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}.

(2)

Let
B=(B_{m,n})_{m,n\geq 0}
denote the change of basis matrix from the usual Mahler basis
\big\{\binom{z}{n};\;n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\big\}
to the modified Mahler basis
\{\mathbf{m}_{n}(z);\;n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}
so that

\mathbf{m}_{n}(z)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}B_{m,n}\binom{z}{m}.

Then
B
is an upper triangular matrix in
\mathrm{M}_{\infty}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
whose diagonal entries lie in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
.

(3)

The set
\{\mathbf{m}_{n}(z);\;n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}
forms an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathbb{Z}_{p};\mathbb{Z}_{p})
.

(4)

If
P=(P_{m,n})_{m,n\geq 0}
denotes the matrix of the action of
{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\big)}\in\mathbf{M}_{1}
with respect to the modified Mahler basis of
\mathcal{C}^{0}\big(\mathbb{Z}_{p};\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket^{(\varepsilon)}\big)
, then

(3.15.2)

P_{m,n}\in\begin{cases}p^{\max\{0,\,m-n\}}\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle&\textrm{ if }{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\big)}\in\mathbf{M}_{1}\\
p^{\max\{0,\,m-\lfloor n/p\rfloor\}}\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle&\textrm{ if }{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\big)}\in{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p\mathbb{Z}_{p}&\mathbb{Z}_{p}\\
p\mathbb{Z}_{p}&\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}\end{smallmatrix}\big)}^{\mathrm{det}\neq 0}\end{cases}.

Proof.

(1) We need to check that the degree of each nonzero monomial term in each
f_{i}(z)
is congruent to
1
modulo
p-1
and the leading coefficient of
f_{i}(z)
is
p^{-(1+\cdots+p^{i-1})}
. This is true for
f_{1}(z)
, and inductively, we may write
f_{i}(z)=zh_{i}(z^{p-1})
with leading coefficient
p^{-(1+\cdots+p^{i-1})}
and see that
f_{i+1}(z)=\frac{1}{p}\big(z^{p}h_{i}(z^{p-1})^{p}-zh_{i}(z^{p-1})\big)=\frac{1}{p}z\big(z^{p-1}h_{i}(z^{p-1})^{p}-h_{i}(z^{p-1})\big)
with leading coefficient
p^{-(1+\cdots+p^{i})}
. The last statement follows from Lemma
A.5
(1).

(2)
Since the degree of
\mathbf{m}_{n}(z)
is
n
,
B_{m,n}=0
if
m>n
.
By comparing the coefficients of
z^{n}
using (
3.15.1
), we see that
B_{n,n}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
. Moreover, since each
\mathbf{m}_{n}(z)\in\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathbb{Z}_{p};\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, it is a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-linear combination of
1,z,\binom{z}{2},\dots,\binom{z}{n}
; so we have
B_{m,n}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
for
m\leq n
. Part (2) follows.

(3) is a corollary of (2) as
B
is invertible over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and Mahler basis is a basis of
\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathbb{Z}_{p};\mathbb{Z}_{p})
.

(4) Let
P^{\prime}=(P^{\prime}_{m,n})_{m,n\geq 0}
denote the matrix of the action of
{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\big)}
on
\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathbb{Z}_{p};\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket^{(\varepsilon)})
with respect to the Mahler basis
1,z,\dots,\binom{z}{n},\dots
. Then
[
LWX17
, Proposition 3.14 (1)]
implies that

(a) when
{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\big)}\in\mathbf{M}_{1}
,

P^{\prime}_{m,n}\in(p,w)^{\max\{0,\,m-n\}}\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket\subseteq p^{\max\{0,\,m-n\}}\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle
, and

(b) when
{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\big)}\in{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p\mathbb{Z}_{p}&\mathbb{Z}_{p}\\
p\mathbb{Z}_{p}&\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}\end{smallmatrix}\big)}^{\mathrm{det}\neq 0}
,
P^{\prime}_{m,n}\in(p,w)^{\max\{0,\,m-\lfloor n/p\rfloor\}}\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket\subseteq p^{\max\{0,\,m-\lfloor n/p\rfloor\}}\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle
.

Changing basis, we have
P=B^{-1}P^{\prime}B
. Yet
B\in\mathrm{M}_{\infty}(\mathcal{O})
is upper triangular with
p
-adic units on the diagonal; the same holds true for
B^{-1}
. From this, we deduce that
P
satisfies the same bound (
3.15.2
).
∎

Notation 3.16
.

By Lemma
3.15
(1), each
\mathbf{m}_{n}(z)
is an eigenvector for the
\bar{\mathrm{T}}
-action. So we may assign the modified Mahler basis to the weight disks according to the character by which
\bar{\mathrm{T}}
acts on
\mathbf{m}_{n}(z)
and obtain another basis of
\mathrm{S}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}
for every relevant character
\varepsilon
as follows.

For
\varepsilon=\omega^{-s_{\varepsilon}}\times\omega^{a+s_{\varepsilon}}
(and possibly suppressing
\varepsilon
from the notation occasionally), recall the power basis
\mathbf{e}_{1}^{(\varepsilon)},\mathbf{e}_{2}^{(\varepsilon)},\dots
of
\mathrm{S}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}
defined in §
2.11
. For each
\mathbf{e}_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}=e_{i}^{*}z^{\deg\mathbf{e}_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}}
with
i=1,2
, we define the
associated modified Mahler basis

\mathbf{f}_{n}=\mathbf{f}_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}:=e_{i}^{*}\cdot\mathbf{m}_{\deg\mathbf{e}_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}}(z);

then Lemma
3.15
(1) above implies that
\mathbf{f}_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}
is a
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-linear combination of
\mathbf{e}_{1}^{(\varepsilon)},\dots,\mathbf{e}_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}
, and
\deg\mathbf{f}_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}=\deg\mathbf{e}_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}
.
Let
\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{C}^{(\varepsilon)}
denote the collection of
\mathbf{f}_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
; it is the
modified Mahler basis
of
\mathrm{S}_{p\textrm{-adic}}^{(\varepsilon)}
(see §
2.4
(2) for the definition of
\mathrm{S}_{p\textrm{-adic}}^{(\varepsilon)}
).

For the rest of this section, we aim to “translate” the halo bound for the
U_{p}
-action on
\mathrm{S}_{p\textrm{-adic}}^{(\varepsilon)}
with respect to
\mathbf{C}^{(\varepsilon)}
to a bound on the
U_{p}
-action with respect to
\mathbf{B}^{(\varepsilon)}
. (This turns out to be stronger than the naive Hodge bound on the power basis.)

We write
Y=(Y_{m,n})_{m,n\geq 0}
,
\mathrm{Y}^{(\varepsilon)}=(\mathrm{Y}_{\mathbf{e}_{m}^{(\varepsilon)},\mathbf{f}_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}})_{m,n\geq 1}\in\mathrm{M}_{\infty}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
for the change of basis matrix between the modified Mahler basis (
3.14.2
) and the normalized power basis, that is to write

(3.16.1)

\mathbf{m}_{n}(z)=\sum_{m\geq 0}Y_{m,n}z^{m},\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathrm{Y}_{\mathbf{e}_{m}^{(\varepsilon)},\mathbf{f}_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}}=Y_{\deg\mathbf{e}_{m},\deg\mathbf{f}_{n}}.

The following estimate on
Y_{m,n}
is important.

Lemma 3.17
.

The matrix
Y
is an upper triangular matrix in
\mathrm{M}_{\infty}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, with diagonal entries
Y_{n,n}\in(n!)^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
.
Moreover,
Y_{m,n}=0
unless
n-m
is divisible by
p-1
.

Write the inverse of
Y
as
((Y^{-1})_{m,n})_{m,n\geq 0}
. We have the following estimates (when
n\geq m
):

(3.17.1)

\displaystyle\displaystyle v_{p}(Y_{m,n})\geq-v_{p}(m!)+\Big\lfloor\frac{m}{p}\Big\rfloor-\Big\lfloor\frac{n}{p}\Big\rfloor-\Big\lfloor\frac{n-m}{p^{2}-p}\Big\rfloor,

(3.17.2)

\displaystyle\displaystyle v_{p}((Y^{-1})_{m,n})\geq v_{p}(n!)+\Big\lfloor\frac{m}{p}\Big\rfloor-\Big\lfloor\frac{n}{p}\Big\rfloor-\Big\lfloor\frac{n-m}{p^{2}-p}\Big\rfloor.

Proof.

It is clear that
Y
is upper triangular. The vanishing of
Y_{m,n}
when
p-1
does not divide
n-m
and the fact
Y_{n,n}\in(n!)^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
follow from Lemma
3.15
(1).

Let
D
(resp.
E
) denote the diagonal matrix whose
n
th diagonal entry is equal to
p^{\lfloor n/p\rfloor}/n!
(resp.
p^{\lfloor n/p\rfloor}
), and set
Y^{\prime}=D^{-1}YE
. It suffices to prove that

(3.17.3)

v_{p}(Y^{\prime}_{m,n})\geq-\Big\lfloor\dfrac{n-m}{p^{2}-p}\Big\rfloor\quad\textrm{and}\quad v_{p}((Y^{\prime-1})_{m,n})\geq-\Big\lfloor\dfrac{n-m}{p^{2}-p}\Big\rfloor

In fact, the second inequality follows from the first one in (
3.17.3
). This is because
Y^{\prime}
is an upper triangular matrix with diagonal entries
Y^{\prime}_{n,n}=n!\cdot Y_{n,n}
, which belongs to
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
by the discussion above; the condition
v_{p}(Y^{\prime}_{m,n})\geq-\Big\lfloor\dfrac{n-m}{p^{2}-p}\Big\rfloor\geq-\dfrac{n-m}{p^{2}-p}
then clearly implies that
v_{p}((Y^{\prime-1})_{m,n})\geq-\dfrac{n-m}{p^{2}-p}
. But
(Y^{\prime-1})_{m,n}\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}
; so we deduce
v_{p}((Y^{\prime-1})_{m,n})\geq-\Big\lfloor\dfrac{n-m}{p^{2}-p}\Big\rfloor
.

It remains to prove the first estimate (
3.17.3
) on
v_{p}(Y^{\prime}_{m,n})
. Rewrite (
3.16.1
) as

(3.17.4)

p^{\lfloor n/p\rfloor}\mathbf{m}_{n}(z)=\sum_{m=0}^{n}\frac{p^{\lfloor m/p\rfloor}}{m!}Y^{\prime}_{m,n}z^{m}=\sum_{m=0}^{n}Y^{\prime\prime}_{m,n}z^{m},\text{~with~}Y_{m,n}^{\prime\prime}=\frac{p^{\lfloor m/p\rfloor}}{m!}Y_{m,n}^{\prime}.

By Lemma
A.5
(2), we need to show that for
m\equiv n\mod(p-1)
,

(3.17.5)

v_{p}(Y^{\prime\prime}_{m,n})\geq-\Big\lfloor\dfrac{n-m}{p^{2}-p}\Big\rfloor+\Big\lfloor\frac{m}{p}\Big\rfloor-v_{p}(m!)=-\Big\lfloor\dfrac{n-m}{p^{2}-p}\Big\rfloor-v_{p}\Big(\Big\lfloor\frac{m}{p}\Big\rfloor!\Big).

We say that a function
h:\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is
sub-additive
if it satisfies
h(x)+h(y)\geq h(x+y)
for all
x,y
. The functions
f(x)=-\lfloor\frac{x}{p^{2}-p}\rfloor
and
g(x)=-v_{p}(\lfloor x/p\rfloor!)
are clearly both sub-additive. By this property, we have the following fact: if we write
n=n^{\prime}+n^{\prime\prime}
such that there is no carry in this addition under base
p
and the estimate (
3.17.5
) holds for
Y_{m,n^{\prime}}^{\prime\prime}
and
Y^{\prime\prime}_{m,n^{\prime\prime}}
with all
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, then (
3.17.5
) holds for all
Y^{\prime\prime}_{m,n}
’s. In fact, we have
p^{\lfloor n/p\rfloor}\mathbf{m}_{n}(z)=\big(p^{\lfloor n^{\prime}/p\rfloor}\mathbf{m}_{n^{\prime}}(z)\big)\cdot\big(p^{\lfloor n^{\prime\prime}/p\rfloor}\mathbf{m}_{n^{\prime\prime}}(z)\big)
. The estimate (
3.17.5
) for
Y^{\prime\prime}_{m,n}
follows by comparing the coefficients of
z^{m}
on both sides and the aforementioned sub-additive property. Therefore, it suffices to prove (
3.17.5
) for
n=p^{i}
,
i\geq 0
. In this case, (
3.17.4
) becomes
p^{p^{i-1}}f_{i}(z)=\sum\limits_{m\geq 0}Y^{\prime\prime}_{m,p^{i}}z^{m}
.

We prove this by induction on
i
. It can be verified directly for
i=0,1
. Assume that (
3.17.5
) is already proved for
n=p^{i}
(
i\geq 1
). To simplify notations, we write

p^{p^{i-1}}f_{i}(z)=\sum\limits_{m=0}^{p^{i}}a_{m}z^{m},\text{~with~}v_{p}(a_{m})\geq-\Big\lfloor\dfrac{p^{i}-m}{p^{2}-p}\Big\rfloor-v_{p}\Big(\Big\lfloor\dfrac{m}{p}\Big\rfloor!\Big).

Now for
n=p^{i+1}
, we rewrite

p^{p^{i}}f_{i+1}(z)=\frac{1}{p}\big(p^{p^{i-1}}f_{i}(z)\big)^{p}-p^{p^{i-1}(p-1)-1}\cdot\big(p^{p^{i-1}}f_{i}(z)\big).

The estimate (
3.17.5
) for the second summand above is clear by inductive hypothesis. For the first summand, note that a general term in the binomial expansion of
\frac{1}{p}\big(p^{p^{i-1}}f_{i}(z)\big)^{p}
is of the form
\frac{1}{p}\binom{p}{j_{1},\dots,j_{s}}\prod\limits_{k=1}^{s}(a_{m_{k}}z^{m_{k}})^{j_{k}}
, where
j_{1},\dots,j_{s}
are positive integers whose sum is
p
, and
m_{1},\dots,m_{s}
are distinct integers in
\{0,\dots,p^{i}\}
that are congruent to
1
modulo
p-1
. We shall prove the coefficient of such a monomial satisfies (
3.17.5
) for
n=p^{i+1}
and
m:=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{s}j_{k}m_{k}
.

When
s>1
,
p
divides the binomial coefficient
\binom{p}{j_{1},\dots,j_{s}}
and it suffices to show

\sum_{k=1}^{s}j_{k}v_{p}(a_{m_{k}})\geq-\Big\lfloor\frac{p^{i+1}-m}{p^{2}-p}\Big\rfloor-v_{p}\Big(\Big\lfloor\frac{m}{p}\Big\rfloor!\Big).

This follows from the inductive hypothesis on
v_{p}(a_{m_{k}})
’s and the aforementioned sub-additive property of the functions
f(x)
and
g(x)
.

When
s=1
and hence
m=pm_{1}
, it suffices to prove

(3.17.6)
