-p\Big\lfloor\frac{p^{i}-m_{1}}{p^{2}-p}\Big\rfloor-pv_{p}\Big(\Big\lfloor\frac{m_{1}}{p}\Big\rfloor!\Big)-1\geq-\Big\lfloor\frac{p^{i+1}-pm_{1}}{p^{2}-p}\Big\rfloor-v_{p}(m_{1}!).

If
m_{1}\geq p
, this follows from the sub-additive property of
f(x)
and Lemma
A.5
(3). If
m_{1}<p
, the condition
m\equiv n\bmod(p-1)
implies
m_{1}\equiv 1\bmod(p-1)
, and we have
m_{1}=1
. Then (
3.17.6
) is nothing but
-p\big\lfloor\frac{p^{i}-1}{p^{2}-p}\rfloor-1\geq-\big\lfloor\frac{p^{i+1}-p}{p^{2}-p}\big\rfloor
, which can be verified by a direct computation.
∎

Notation 3.18
.

We have the following list of matrices of
U_{p}
with respect to the given bases:

•

\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}=\mathrm{U}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}=\big(\mathrm{U}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}_{\mathbf{e}_{m},\mathbf{e}_{n}}\big)_{m,n\geq 1}
for
U_{p}:\big(\mathrm{S}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)},\mathbf{B}^{(\varepsilon)}\big)\longrightarrow\big(\mathrm{S}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)},\mathbf{B}^{(\varepsilon)}\big)
;

•

\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{C}}=\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{C}}^{(\varepsilon)}=\big(\mathrm{U}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\mathbf{C},\mathbf{f}_{m},\mathbf{f}_{n}}\big)_{m,n\geq 1}
for
U_{p}:\big(\mathrm{S}^{(\varepsilon)}_{p\textrm{-adic}},\mathbf{C}^{(\varepsilon)}\big)\longrightarrow\big(\mathrm{S}^{(\varepsilon)}_{p\textrm{-adic}},\mathbf{C}^{(\varepsilon)}\big)
;

•

\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{B}}=\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{B}}^{(\varepsilon)}=\big(\mathrm{U}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{B},\mathbf{e}_{m},\mathbf{f}_{n}}\big)_{m,n\geq 1}
for
U_{p}:\big(\mathrm{S}^{(\varepsilon)}_{p\textrm{-adic}},\mathbf{C}^{(\varepsilon)}\big)\longrightarrow\big(\mathrm{S}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)},\mathbf{B}^{(\varepsilon)}\big)
.

For the change of basis matrix
\mathrm{Y}^{(\varepsilon)}
defined in (
3.16.1
), we have the following equalities

(3.18.1)

\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{B}}^{(\varepsilon)}=\mathrm{Y}^{(\varepsilon)}\mathrm{U}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\mathbf{C}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathrm{U}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}=\mathrm{U}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{B}}\mathrm{Y}^{(\varepsilon),-1}.

A key input in our later proof of local ghost conjecture is that the halo estimate from
[
LWX17
]
“propagates” to estimates on
\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{C}}^{(\varepsilon)}
and
\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{B}}^{(\varepsilon)}
.

Proposition 3.19
.

The matrix
\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{C}}^{(\varepsilon)}
satisfies the following halo estimate:

(3.19.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{C},\mathbf{f}_{m},\mathbf{f}_{n}}^{(\varepsilon)}\in p^{\deg\mathbf{e}_{m}^{(\varepsilon)}-\lfloor\deg\mathbf{e}_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}/p\rfloor}\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle.

Proof.

The
U_{p}
-action on
\mathrm{S}_{p\textrm{-adic}}
is a uniform limit of finite sums of actions
|_{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}
with matrices
{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\big)}\in{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p\mathbb{Z}_{p}&\mathbb{Z}_{p}\\
p\mathbb{Z}_{p}&\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}\end{smallmatrix}\big)}{}^{\mathrm{det}\in p\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}}
(see for example
[
LTXZ

24

, (2.9.1)]
).
The estimate (
3.19.1
) for
\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{C},\mathbf{f}_{m},\mathbf{f}_{n}}
follows from (
3.15.2
).
∎

Remark 3.20
.

This proposition is our new essential input to the local ghost conjecture. The analogous direct estimate of
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}
is more delicate.

Notation 3.21
.

We will often refer to a finite subset
\underline{\zeta}
of
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
of size
n
, in which case, we always order its elements as
\zeta_{1}<\cdots<\zeta_{n}
.
For an infinite matrix
\mathrm{U}
(indexed by
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
) and two finite sets of positive integers

\underline{\zeta}:=\{\zeta_{1}<\zeta_{2}<\dots<\zeta_{n}\}
and
\underline{\xi}:=\{\xi_{1}<\xi_{2}<\dots<\xi_{n}\}
, we write
\mathrm{U}(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})
for the
n\times n
-submatrix of
\mathrm{U}
with row indices
\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{n}
and column indices
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}
.
When
\underline{\zeta}=\underline{\xi}
, we write
\mathrm{U}(\underline{\zeta})
instead.
For example, we often write
\underline{n}=(1<2<\cdots<n)
and thus
\mathrm{U}(\underline{n})
is the upper left
n\times n
-submatrix we have considered above.

For
\underline{\zeta}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
a subset, define
\deg(\underline{\zeta}):=\sum\limits_{\zeta\in\underline{\zeta}}\deg\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}
.

Corollary 3.22
.

Under Notation
3.21
, write
\mathrm{U}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\mathbf{C}}(\underline{\lambda}\times\underline{\eta})
for the submatrix of
\mathrm{U}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\mathbf{C}}
with row indices in
\underline{\lambda}
and column indices in
\underline{\eta}
. Then

v_{p}\big(\mathrm{det}\big(\mathrm{U}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\mathbf{C}}(\underline{\lambda}\times\underline{\eta})\big)\big)\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big(\deg\mathbf{e}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\lambda_{i}}-\Big\lfloor\frac{\deg\mathbf{e}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\eta_{i}}}{p}\Big\rfloor\Big)

Proof.

The estimate follows directly from Proposition
3.19
and the equality
\mathrm{det}\big(\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{C}}(\underline{\lambda}\times\underline{\eta})\big)=\sum\limits_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)\cdot\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{C},\mathbf{f}_{\lambda_{\sigma(1)}},\mathbf{f}_{\eta_{1}}}\cdots\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{C},\mathbf{f}_{\lambda_{\sigma(n)}},\mathbf{f}_{\eta_{n}}}
.
∎

Definition-Proposition 3.23

(General corank theorem)
.

For every
{k}=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{\bullet}
and every two finite sets of positive integers
\underline{\zeta}
and
\underline{\xi}
of size
n
as above,
we set

\displaystyle r_{\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi}}(k)=r^{(\varepsilon)}_{\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi}}(k)

\displaystyle:=\#\big\{i\in\{1,\dots,d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})\}\;\big|\;i\in\underline{\xi}\textrm{ and }d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})+1-i\in\underline{\zeta}\big\},

\displaystyle s_{\underline{\xi}}(k)=s^{(\varepsilon)}_{\underline{\xi}}(k)

\displaystyle:=\#\big\{i\in\underline{\xi}\;\big|\;i>d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})\big\}.

In other words,
r_{\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi}}(k)
is the number of “classical basis” elements in
\mathbf{B}^{(\varepsilon)}
indexed by
\underline{\xi}
that are sent to
\underline{\zeta}
by
\mathrm{AL}_{(k,\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})}
, and
s_{\underline{\xi}}(k)
is the number of basis elements in
\mathbf{B}^{(\varepsilon)}
indexed by
\underline{\xi}
which are “non-classical”.

Then the corank of
\mathrm{U}_{k}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})
is at least

(3.23.1)

m_{\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi}}(k)=m^{(\varepsilon)}_{\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi}}(k):=n-d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})-r_{\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi}}(k)-s_{\underline{\xi}}(k).

Consequently,
\mathrm{det}\big(\mathrm{U}^{\dagger,(\varepsilon)}(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})\big)\in\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle
is divisible by
((w-w_{k})/p)^{\max\{0,m_{\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi}}(k)\}}
in
\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle
.

When
\underline{\zeta}=\underline{\xi}
, we write
r_{\underline{\zeta}}=r^{(\varepsilon)}_{\underline{\zeta}}(k)
and
m_{\underline{\zeta}}=m^{(\varepsilon)}_{\underline{\zeta}}(k)
for
r_{\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\zeta}}(k)
and
m_{\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\zeta}}(k)
, respectively.

Taking
\underline{\zeta}=\underline{\xi}=\underline{n}
with
d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})<n<d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})-d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})
and noting that
m_{\underline{n}\times\underline{n}}(k)=m_{n}(k)
, we recover Corollary
3.11
.

Proof.

By the property of theta map (
2.12.2
),
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}_{k}
is a upper triangular block matrix. So

\rank\big(\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}_{k}(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})\big)\leq s_{\underline{\xi}}(k)+\rank\big(\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}_{k}\big((\underline{\zeta}\cap\underline{d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}})\times(\underline{\xi}\cap\underline{d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}})\big)\big).

By Proposition
3.6
(2),
\mathrm{U}^{\mathrm{Iw}}_{k}
is the sum of a matrix with rank
\leq d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}
and an anti-diagonal matrix; so

\rank\big(\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}_{k}\big((\underline{\zeta}\cap\underline{d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}})\times(\underline{\xi}\cap\underline{d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}})\big)\big)\leq d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}+r_{\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi}}(k);

The corank formula (
3.23.1
) follows from combining above two inequalities.
The corollary and the last statement are immediate consequences of the above discussion.
∎

3.24.
Refined halo estimates

In our later proof of the local ghost theorem, we inevitably encounter some rather pathological cases, which require slightly refined halo bounds depending on the
p
-adic expansions of the row and column indices (see the proof of Proposition
5.5
(1)). The readers are invited to skip this portion on the first reading, and only come back after understanding the complication as seen in the proof of Proposition
5.5
(1).

For this part of the argument, we fix a matrix
{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}pa&b\\
pc&d\end{smallmatrix}\big)}\in{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p\mathbb{Z}_{p}&\mathbb{Z}_{p}\\
p\mathbb{Z}_{p}&\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}
with determinant
p^{u}\delta\in p^{u}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
.
Let
P=(P_{m,n})_{m,n\geq 0}
and
Q=(Q_{m,n})_{m,n\geq 0}
respectively denote the matrix of

\displaystyle\big|_{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}pa&b\\
pc&d\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}:\big(\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathbb{Z}_{p};\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket^{(\varepsilon)}),(\mathbf{m}_{n}(z))_{n\geq 0}\big)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathbb{Z}_{p};\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket^{(\varepsilon)}),(\mathbf{m}_{n}(z))_{n\geq 0}\big)\quad\textrm{and}

\displaystyle\big|_{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}pa&b\\
pc&d\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}:\big(\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathbb{Z}_{p};\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket^{(\varepsilon)}),(\mathbf{m}_{n}(z))_{n\geq 0}\big)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathbb{Z}_{p};\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket^{(\varepsilon)}),\big(\tbinom{z}{n}\big)_{n\geq 0}\big).

Notation 3.25
.

For two positive integers
m,n
, write
m=m_{0}+pm_{1}+\cdots
and
n=n_{0}+pn_{1}+\cdots
for their
p
-adic expansions (so that each
m_{i}
and
n_{i}
belongs to
\{0,\dots,p-1\}
).
Let
D(m,n)
denote the number of indices
i\geq 0
such that
n_{i+1}>m_{i}
.

We refer to Lemma
A.7
for some elementary facts regarding the numbers
D(m,n)
.

Proposition 3.26
.

We have the following refined estimate:

(3.26.1)

P_{m,n},\,Q_{m,n}\in p^{D(m,n)}\cdot p^{m-\lfloor n/p\rfloor}\mathcal{O}\langle\tfrac{w}{p}\rangle.

Proof.

We first explain that (
3.26.1
) for the matrix
Q
implies that for
P
.
Recall the change of basis matrix
B
from the usual Mahler basis
\{\binom{z}{n}\,|\,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}
to the modified Mahler basis
\{\mathbf{m}_{n}(z)\,|\,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}
as introduced in Lemma
3.15
(2). Then
B
and hence
B^{-1}
are upper triangular matrices with entries in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and diagonal entries in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
.
As
P=B^{-1}Q
, we have

P_{m,n}=\sum\limits_{\ell\geq 0}(B^{-1})_{m,\ell}Q_{\ell,n}.

So it is enough to prove that, when
\ell\geq m

D(\ell,n)+\ell-\lfloor n/p\rfloor\geq D(m,n)+m-\lfloor n/p\rfloor.

But this follows from Lemma
A.7
(1).

Now we focus on proving (
3.26.1
) for
Q_{m,n}
.
Recall from (
2.4.4
) that

(3.26.2)

\displaystyle\mathbf{m}_{n}\big|_{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}pa&b\\
pc&d\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}(z)\

\displaystyle=\varepsilon(\delta/\bar{d},\bar{d})\cdot(1+w)^{\log(\frac{pcz+d}{\omega(\bar{d})})/p}\mathbf{m}_{n}\Big(\frac{paz+b}{pcz+d}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\geq 0}\varepsilon(\delta/\bar{d},\bar{d})\cdot p^{r}\Big(\frac{w}{p}\Big)^{r}\binom{\log(\frac{pcz+d}{\omega(\bar{d})})/p}{r}\cdot\mathbf{m}_{n}\Big(\frac{paz+b}{pcz+d}\Big).

We need to go back to several arguments in
[
LWX17
, § 3]
. As proved in
[
LWX17
, Lemma 3.13]
,
\dbinom{\log(\frac{pcz+d}{\omega(\bar{d})})/p}{r}
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-linear combination of
p^{s-r}\dbinom{z}{s}
for
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. So to prove (
3.26.1
) for
Q_{m,n}
, it suffices to prove that, for every
s\geq 0
, when expanding

p^{s}\binom{z}{s}\cdot\mathbf{m}_{n}\Big(\frac{paz+b}{pcz+d}\Big)

with respect to the Mahler basis
\{\binom{z}{n}\;|\;n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}
, the
m
th coefficient has
p
-adic valuation greater than or equal to
m-\lfloor n/p\rfloor+D(m,n)
. For this, we need to reproduce the argument in
[
LWX17
, Lemma 3.12]
: write

n!\cdot\mathbf{m}_{n}\Big(\frac{paz+b}{pcz+d}\Big)=\sum_{t\geq 0}c_{t}\cdot t!\binom{z}{t}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket pz\rrbracket,

then
[
LWX17
, Lemma 3.11]
implies that
v_{p}(c_{t})\geq t
. Moreover, as
\mathbf{m}_{n}(\frac{paz+b}{pcz+d})\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{Z}_{p},\mathcal{O})
, we know that
v_{p}(c_{t})\geq v_{p}(\frac{n!}{t!})
and hence
v_{p}(c_{t})\geq\max\{t,v_{p}(\frac{n!}{t!})\}
.
Using the combinatorial identity in Lemma
A.7
(3), we deduce that

\displaystyle p^{s}\binom{z}{s}\cdot\mathbf{m}_{n}\Big(\frac{paz+b}{pcz+d}\Big)\

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\geq 0}c_{t}p^{s}\frac{t!}{n!}\binom{z}{s}\binom{z}{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\geq 0}\sum_{j\geq\max\{s,t\}}^{s+t}c_{t}p^{s}\frac{t!}{n!}\binom{j}{j-s,j-t,s+t-j}\binom{z}{j}.

Taking the term with
j=m\geq s
, we need to show that whenever
s+t\geq m\geq t
, we have

v_{p}(c_{t})+v_{p}\Big(p^{s}\frac{t!}{n!}\cdot\binom{m}{m-s,m-t,s+t-m}\Big)\geq m-\Big\lfloor\frac{n}{p}\Big\rfloor+D(m,n).

Since
v_{p}(c_{t})\geq\max\{t,v_{p}(\frac{n!}{t!})\}
, we need to show that

s-m+\Big\lfloor\frac{n}{p}\Big\rfloor+\max\Big\{t+v_{p}\Big(\frac{t!}{n!}\Big),0\Big\}+v_{p}\Big(\binom{m}{m-s,m-t,s+t-m}\Big)\geq D(m,n).

This is proved in Lemma
A.7
(4).
∎

Notation 3.27
.

Let
\underline{\lambda}
and
\underline{\eta}
be two subsets of positive integers of cardinality
n
; for each such integer
\lambda_{i}
, we write
\deg\mathbf{e}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\lambda_{i}}=\lambda_{i,0}+p\lambda_{i,1}+\cdots
in its
p
-adic expansion, and similarly for
\eta_{i}
’s.
To iterate, we are expanding
\deg\mathbf{e}_{\lambda_{i}}^{(\varepsilon)}
(as opposed to
\lambda_{i}
)
, as they correspond to the
m
and
n
in Proposition
3.26
.
For each
j\geq 0
, we define

D_{\leq\alpha}^{(\varepsilon)}(\underline{\lambda},j):=\#\{i\;|\;\lambda_{i,j}\leq\alpha\},

counting the number of
\deg\mathbf{e}_{\lambda_{i}}^{(\varepsilon)}
’s whose
j
th digit is less than or equal to
\alpha
. When
\alpha=0
, we write
D^{(\varepsilon)}_{=0}(\underline{\lambda},j)
for
D_{\leq\alpha}^{(\varepsilon)}(\underline{\lambda},j)
. We define
D_{=0}^{(\varepsilon)}(\underline{\eta},j)
similarly.
We define two tuple versions of
D(m,n)
as follows:

D^{(\varepsilon)}(\underline{\lambda},\underline{\eta})=\sum_{j\geq 0}\Big(\max\big\{D^{(\varepsilon)}_{=0}(\underline{\lambda},j)-D^{(\varepsilon)}_{=0}(\underline{\eta},j+1),\ 0\big\}\Big)

and

\mathbb{D}^{(\varepsilon)}(\underline{\lambda},\underline{\eta})=\sum_{j\geq 0}\Big(\max\limits_{0\leq\alpha\leq p-2}\big\{D^{(\varepsilon)}_{\leq\alpha}(\underline{\lambda},j)-D^{(\varepsilon)}_{\leq\alpha}(\underline{\eta},j+1),\ 0\big\}\Big).

Lemma 3.28
.

Under the above notations, if
\underline{\eta}^{\prime}
is given by
\eta_{i}^{\prime}=\eta_{i}
except for one
i_{0}
where
\eta^{\prime}_{i_{0}}=\eta_{i_{0}}+1
, then we have

(3.28.1)

D^{(\varepsilon)}(\underline{\lambda},\underline{\eta}^{\prime})+v_{p}\bigg(\frac{\big\lfloor\deg\mathbf{e}_{\eta^{\prime(\varepsilon)}_{i_{0}}}/p\big\rfloor!}{\big\lfloor\deg\mathbf{e}_{\eta_{i_{0}}^{(\varepsilon)}}/p\big\rfloor!}\bigg)\geq D^{(\varepsilon)}(\underline{\lambda},\underline{\eta}).

Proof.

We have
\deg\mathbf{e}_{\eta^{\prime}_{i_{0}}}-\deg\mathbf{e}_{\eta_{i_{0}}}\in\{a,p-1-a\}
, so
\delta_{i_{0}}:=\lfloor\deg\mathbf{e}_{\eta^{\prime}_{i_{0}}}/p\rfloor-\lfloor\deg\mathbf{e}_{\eta_{i_{0}}}/p\rfloor\in\{0,1\}
.
Note that
v_{p}\Big(\frac{\lfloor\deg\mathbf{e}_{\eta^{\prime}_{i_{0}}}/p\rfloor!}{\lfloor\deg\mathbf{e}_{\eta_{i_{0}}}/p\rfloor!}\Big)
is equal to the number of carries when computing the sum of
\lfloor\deg\mathbf{e}_{\eta_{i_{0}}}/p\rfloor
and
\delta_{i_{0}}
. Yet this number is exactly the same as the number of
additional
zeros we produce in the
p
-adic expansion of
\lfloor\deg\mathbf{e}_{\eta^{\prime}_{i_{0}}}/p\rfloor
. The lemma follows from this, and the definition of
D(\underline{\lambda},\underline{\eta})
.
∎

Corollary 3.29
.

Keep the notation as above. Write
\mathrm{U}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\mathbf{C}}(\underline{\lambda}\times\underline{\eta})
for the submatrix of
\mathrm{U}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\mathbf{C}}
with row indices in
\underline{\lambda}
and column indices in
\underline{\eta}
. Then

(3.29.1)

\displaystyle v_{p}\big(\mathrm{det}\big(\mathrm{U}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\mathbf{C}}(\underline{\lambda}\times\underline{\eta})\big)\big)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{D}^{(\varepsilon)}(\underline{\lambda},\underline{\eta})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big(\deg\mathbf{e}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\lambda_{i}}-\Big\lfloor\frac{\deg\mathbf{e}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\eta_{i}}}{p}\Big\rfloor\Big)

\displaystyle\geq D^{(\varepsilon)}(\underline{\lambda},\underline{\eta})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big(\deg\mathbf{e}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\lambda_{i}}-\Big\lfloor\frac{\deg\mathbf{e}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\eta_{i}}}{p}\Big\rfloor\Big)

Proof.

Write
\mathrm{det}\big(\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{C}}(\underline{\lambda}\times\underline{\eta})\big)=\sum\limits_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)\cdot\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{C},\mathbf{f}_{\lambda_{\sigma(1)}},\mathbf{f}_{\eta_{1}}}\cdots\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{C},\mathbf{f}_{\lambda_{\sigma(n)}},\mathbf{f}_{\eta_{n}}}
. By Proposition
3.26
, for every permutation
\sigma\in S_{n}
and every
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
,

v_{p}\big(\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{C},\mathbf{f}_{\lambda_{\sigma(i)}},\mathbf{f}_{\eta_{i}}}\big)\geq\deg\mathbf{e}_{\lambda_{\sigma(i)}}-\Big\lfloor\frac{\deg\mathbf{e}_{\eta_{i}}}{p}\Big\rfloor+D\big(\deg\mathbf{e}_{\lambda_{\sigma(i)}},\deg\mathbf{e}_{\eta_{i}}\big).

Then the corollary is reduced to the following combinatorial inequality:

\sum_{i=1}^{n}D\big(\deg\mathbf{e}_{\lambda_{\sigma(i)}},\deg\mathbf{e}_{\eta_{i}}\big)\geq\mathbb{D}(\underline{\lambda},\underline{\eta}).

But this is clear, as the total contribution to all
D\big(\deg\mathbf{e}_{\lambda_{\sigma(i)}},\deg\mathbf{e}_{\eta_{i}}\big)
’s from the
j
th digit is at least
\max\limits_{0\leq\alpha\leq p-2}\big\{D_{\leq\alpha}(\underline{\lambda},j)-D_{\leq\alpha}(\underline{\eta},j+1),\,0\big\}
.
∎

Remark 3.30
.

We remark that
D(\underline{\lambda},\underline{\eta})
is often zero; for example, when
\underline{\lambda}=\underline{\eta}=\underline{n}
, we have

(3.30.1)

D^{(\varepsilon)}(\underline{n},\underline{n})=0.

In fact, this follows from the inequality
D_{=0}(\underline{n},j)\leq D_{=0}(\underline{n},j+1)
for every
j\geq 0
by Lemma
A.8
. As stated earlier, while the weaker bound in (
3.29.1
) seems to work better with most part of our later inductive proof of Proposition
5.5
(1), the sharper bound in Corollary
3.29
is necessary to treat certain pathological cases; see the proof of Proposition
5.5
(1) where the finer estimate is used.

4.
Proof of local ghost conjecture I: Lagrange interpolation

In this and the next two sections, we keep Hypothesis
2.10
: let
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
be a primitive
\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{K}_{p}\rrbracket
-projective augmented module of type
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\Sym^{a}\mathbb{F}^{\oplus 2}
on which
{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}p&0\\
0&p\end{smallmatrix}\big)}
acts trivially. We will always use
\varepsilon
to denote a character of
\Delta^{2}
relevant to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
. For each such
\varepsilon
, we have defined the characteristic power series
C^{(\varepsilon)}(w,t)
and the ghost series
G^{(\varepsilon)}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(w,t)
.
We devote these three sections to the proof of the local ghost conjecture (Theorem
2.8
).

The proof is roughly divided into three steps, which we give a quick overview below. To lighten the notation, we fix
\varepsilon
as above, and suppress it from the notation.

In a rough form, Theorem
2.8
says that
C(w,t)
and
G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(w,t)
are “close” to each other; in particular, this says that, for each
n
, near each zero
w_{k}
of
g_{n}(w)
, the function
c_{n}(w)
is very small. This leads us to the following.

Step I
:

(Lagrange interpolation) For each
n
, we formally apply Lagrange interpolation to
c_{n}(w)
relative to the zeros
w_{k}
of
g_{n}(w)
(with multiplicity), that is, to obtain a formula of the form

(4.0.1)

c_{n}(w)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\equiv k_{\varepsilon}\bmod(p-1)\\
m_{n}(k)\neq 0\end{subarray}}A_{k}(w)\cdot g_{n,\hat{k}}(w)+h(w)g_{n}(w).

We give a sufficient condition on the
p
-adic valuations of the coefficients of
A_{k}(w)
that would imply Theorem
2.8
. This is Proposition
4.4
.

In fact, we shall prove a similar
p
-adic valuation condition for the determinants of
all
(principal or not)
n\times n
-submatrices of the matrix of
U_{p}
with respect to the power basis.
More precisely, given two tuples
\underline{\zeta}
and
\underline{\xi}
of
n
positive integers, we apply the same Lagrange interpolation (
4.0.1
) to
p^{\frac{1}{2}(\deg(\underline{\xi})-\deg(\underline{\zeta}))}\cdot\mathrm{det}(\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi}))
in place of
c_{n}(w)
, where the term
p^{\frac{1}{2}(\deg(\underline{\xi})-\deg(\underline{\zeta}))}
is introduced to “balance” the total degrees of basis elements in
\underline{\zeta}
and
\underline{\xi}
(see Notation
3.21
for the definition of
\deg(\underline{\zeta})
and
\deg(\underline{\xi})
). We shall fix
\underline{\zeta}
and
\underline{\xi}
for the rest of this introduction and still use
A_{k}(w)
and
h(w)
to denote the corresponding power series appearing in (
4.0.1
) (with
c_{n}(w)
replaced by
p^{\frac{1}{2}(\deg(\underline{\xi})-\deg(\underline{\zeta}))}\cdot\mathrm{det}(\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi}))
). Since
c_{n}(w)
is the sum of determinants of all principal
n\times n
minors, the estimate for
c_{n}(w)
follows from that for the
p^{\frac{1}{2}(\deg(\underline{\xi})-\deg(\underline{\zeta}))}\cdot\mathrm{det}(\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi}))
’s. We refer to the paragraph after Theorem
5.2
for the precise argument.

We point out that this is a question for each individual zero
w_{k}
of
g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)
.
We fix such a
w_{k}
and write each
A_{k}(w)
as
A_{k,0}+A_{k,1}(w-w_{k})+A_{k,2}(w-w_{k})^{2}+\cdots
, and we are going to prove that for every
i<m_{n}(k)
,

(4.0.2)

v_{p}(A_{k,i})\geq\Delta_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-i}-\Delta^{\prime}_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)}.

Here, a subtle technical point is that we truly need to use
\Delta-\Delta^{\prime}
in order to implement the induction we perform later; see the comments after the statement of Proposition
4.7
. It turns out that the estimate (
4.0.2
) will give sufficient control on the Newton polygon of the ghost series to conclude the local ghost conjecture. Therefore the proof of Theorem
2.8
is then reduced to prove (
4.0.2
). (See the comments following Theorem
5.2
.)

Step II
:

(Cofactor expansion argument) We reduce the proof of (
4.0.2
) to an estimate on the determinant of the minors of
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})
of smaller size.

For simplicity, assume that
s_{\underline{\xi}}(k)=0
, i.e. all
\xi_{i}\leq d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})
(see Definition-Proposition
3.23
).
Then the corank theorem (Definition-Proposition
3.23
) implies that
A_{k,i}=0
when
i<m_{\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi}}(k)
.
Moreover, we can write
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})=\mathrm{T}_{k}(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})+\mathrm{L}_{k}(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})
, where
\mathrm{L}_{k}(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})
has coefficients in
E
and has exactly
r_{\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi}}(k)
nonzero entries (coming from the matrix for the Atkin–Lehner operator at
w_{k}
), and
\mathrm{T}_{k}(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})
is a matrix in
E\langle w/p\rangle
whose evaluation at
w=w_{k}
has rank at most
d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}
.

We apply a version of cofactor expansion to
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})=\mathrm{L}_{k}(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})+\mathrm{T}_{k}(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})
, to express
\mathrm{det}\big(\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})\big)
as a linear combination of the determinant of smaller minors of
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})
plus a term that is divisible by
(w-w_{k})^{m_{\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi}}(k)}
. This way, we essentially reduce the question of estimating
v_{p}(A_{k,i})
(after appropriate normalizing by
p^{\frac{1}{2}(\deg(\underline{\xi})-\deg(\underline{\zeta}))}
) to the question of estimating the Taylor coefficients for the determinant of smaller minors, when expanded as a power series in
E\llbracket w-w_{k}\rrbracket
(see the Step III below).
There are several subtleties when executing this plan; we leave the discussion to the corresponding points, especially the discussion before Lemma
6.4
and §
6.16
.

Step III
:

(Estimating power series expansion for smaller minors)
What is needed in the Step II from the inductive proof is an estimate of
v_{p}(A^{\prime}_{k,i})
in the expansion of
c_{n^{\prime}}(w)/g_{n^{\prime},\hat{k}}(w)=\sum\limits_{i\geq 0}A^{\prime}_{k,i}(w-w_{k})^{i}
in
E\llbracket w-w_{k}\rrbracket
not for
i<m_{n^{\prime}}(k)
but for
i\geq m_{n^{\prime}}(k)
.

This estimate will be deduced in Proposition
5.5
from the estimate of the Lagrange interpolation coefficients
A^{\prime}_{k^{\prime},i}
of
c_{n^{\prime}}(w)
for
other

k^{\prime}\neq k
and
i\leq m_{n^{\prime}}(k^{\prime})
, as well as the polynomial
h^{\prime}(w)
that appears in the Lagrange interpolation of the determinant of the smaller minor. The latter gives the most trouble; in most cases, it follows immediately from the usual halo estimate, but in some pathological case, we need the refined halo estimate in Proposition
3.26
.

To streamline the logical flow, we will prove Step I in this section, and prove Step III in the next section, and finally complete Step II in Section
6
.

This section is organized as follows. We first discuss the “ordinary” parts of the characteristic power series and the ghost series in Proposition
4.1
. In Definition-Lemma
4.2
and Notation
4.3
, we recall the Lagrange interpolation formula and apply it to the coefficients of characteristic power series. Proposition
4.4
is the key result of this section, which provides a sufficient condition to prove Theorem
2.8
. The rest of the section is devoted to proving Proposition
4.4
.

Proposition 4.1
.

(1)

We have
c_{1}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)\in\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket
is a unit if and only if
\varepsilon=1\times\omega^{a}
.

(2)

For
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
, write
d_{\varepsilon,k}:=d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\varepsilon\cdot(1\times\omega^{2-k}))
. Then
\big(d_{\varepsilon,k},v_{p}(c_{d_{\varepsilon,k}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{k}))\big)
is a vertex of
\NP(C^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{k},-))
, and
\big(d_{\varepsilon,k},v_{p}(g_{d_{\varepsilon,k}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{k}))\big)
is a vertex of
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{k},-))
.

Proof.

(1) When
s_{\varepsilon}=0
(and thus
\varepsilon=1\times\omega^{a}
),
c_{1}^{(1\times\omega^{a})}(w_{2})
is a
p
-adic unit as proved in
[
LTXZ

24

, Proposition A.7]
. So
c_{1}^{(1\times\omega^{a})}(w)\in\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket^{\times}
.

When
s_{\varepsilon}>0
,
c_{1}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)
is not a unit in
\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket
.
Indeed, in this case, Definition-Proposition
2.13
(3) implies that
t_{1}^{(\varepsilon)}\geq\delta_{\varepsilon}+1
; so for
k=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)\delta_{\varepsilon}=2+s_{\varepsilon}+\{a+s_{\varepsilon}\}
, Definition-Proposition
2.13
(3) and (2) imply
d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})=2
and
d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})=0
, respectively. This means that
\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})
consists of only new forms, whose
U_{p}
-slopes are
\frac{k-2}{2}=\frac{s_{\varepsilon}+\{a+s_{\varepsilon}\}}{2}>0
. In particular, this shows that
v_{p}(c_{1}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{k}))>0
and thus
c_{1}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)
is not a unit.

(2) By part (1) and Proposition
2.12
(2), the
d_{\varepsilon,k}
-th slope in
\NP(C(w_{k},-))
is
\leq k-1

and the equality holds precisely when
s_{\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}}:=\{k-2-a-s_{\varepsilon}\}=0
. Similarly, part (1) and Proposition
2.12
(1) imply that the
(d_{\varepsilon,k}+1)
-th slope of
\NP(C(w_{k},-))
is
\geq k-1
and the equality holds if and only if
s_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}:=\{1+s_{\varepsilon}-k\}=0
. Yet, clearly,
s_{\varepsilon}+1
and
2+a+s_{\varepsilon}
are never congruent modulo
p-1
. So the
d_{\varepsilon,k}
-th slope and the
(d_{\varepsilon,k}+1)
-th slope of
\NP(C(w_{k},-))
are never equal, proving that
\big(d_{\varepsilon,k},v_{p}(c_{d_{\varepsilon,k}}(w_{k}))\big)
is a vertex of
\NP(C(w_{k},-))
.

The same argument above with Proposition
2.12
replaced by Proposition
2.17
proves that
\big(d_{\varepsilon,k},v_{p}(g_{d_{\varepsilon,k}}(w_{k}))\big)
is a vertex of
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(w_{k},-))
,
∎

We recall the standard Lagrange interpolation formula, as our main tool to study local ghost conjecture.

Definition-Lemma 4.2
.

Let
f(w)\in E\langle w/p\rangle
be a power series, and let
g(w)=(w-x_{1})^{m_{1}}\cdots(w-x_{s})^{m_{s}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}[w]
be a monic polynomial with zeros
x_{1},\dots,x_{s}\in p\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and multiplicities
m_{1},\dots,m_{s}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. For every
j=1,\dots,s
, let

\frac{f(w)}{g(w)/(w-x_{j})^{m_{j}}}=\sum_{i\geq 0}A_{j,i}(w-x_{j})^{i}

be the formal expansion in
E\llbracket w-x_{j}\rrbracket
and
A_{j}(w):=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{m_{j}-1}A_{j,i}(w-x_{j})^{i}\in E[w]
be its truncation up to the term of degree
m_{j}-1
. Then there exists
h(w)\in E\langle w/p\rangle
such that

(4.2.1)

f(w)=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\Big(A_{i}(w)\frac{g(w)}{(w-x_{i})^{m_{i}}}\Big)+h(w)\cdot g(w).

(1)

If we assume further that
f(w)
belongs to
\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket
, so does
h(w)
.

(2)

If we assume instead that
f(w)\in p^{N}\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle
for some integer
N
, then
h(w)\in p^{N-\deg(g)}\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle
.

We call the expression (
4.2.1
) the
Lagrange interpolation of
f(w)
along
g(w)
.

Proof.

By assumption, the polynomial
g(w)
is
\frac{w}{p}
-distinguished of degree
\deg g
in
E\langle w/p\rangle
. Applying Weierstrass division theorem
[
BGR84
, § 5.2.1, Theorem 2]
to
f(w)
and the polynomial
g(w)
in the Tate algebra
E\langle w/p\rangle
, produces a power series
h(w)\in E\langle w/p\rangle
and a polynomial
r(w)\in E[w]
such that
\deg r<\deg g
and
f(w)=h(w)g(w)+r(w)
. The norm estimate in
[
BGR84
, § 5.2.1, Theorem 2]
gives the estimate (2). When
f(w)\in\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket
, applying instead the division theorem
[
Lang
, IV, Theorem 9.1]
in
\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket
ensures that
h(w)\in\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket
.

From this, we deduce that
\frac{f(w)}{g(w)}=\frac{r(w)}{g(w)}+h(w)
. Applying partial fractions to the rational function
\frac{r(w)}{g(w)}
, we can find polynomials
B_{j}(w)\in E[w]
with
\deg B_{j}(w)<m_{j}
for
j=1,\dots,s
such that
\frac{r(w)}{g(w)}=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{s}\frac{B_{j}(w)}{(w-x_{j})^{m_{j}}}
. Summing up everything, we have

f(w)=\sum_{j=1}^{s}\Big(B_{j}(w)\frac{g(w)}{(w-x_{j})^{m_{j}}}\Big)+h(w)g(w)\text{\quad in~}E\langle w/p\rangle.

We can verify that
A_{j}(w)=B_{j}(w)
for every
j
by first dividing the above equality by
\frac{g(w)}{(w-x_{j})^{m_{j}}}
and considering its formal expansion in
E\llbracket w-x_{j}\rrbracket
.
∎

Notation 4.3
.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, recall the notation
g_{n,\hat{k}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)=g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)/(w-w_{k})^{m_{n}(k)}
from (
2.17.1
).
We write the
n
th coefficient
c_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)
of the characteristic power series
C^{(\varepsilon)}(w,t)
in terms of its Lagrange interpolation along
g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)
as follows. For every ghost zero
w_{k}
of
g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)
consider the formal expansion

\frac{c_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)}{g_{n,\hat{k}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)}=\sum_{i\geq 0}A_{k,i}^{(n,\varepsilon)}(w-w_{k})^{i}\text{~in ~}E\llbracket w-w_{k}\rrbracket

and let
A^{(n,\varepsilon)}_{k}(w)=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{m^{(\varepsilon)}_{n}(k)-1}A^{(n,\varepsilon)}_{k,i}(w-w_{k})^{i}\in E[w]
be its truncation up to the term of degree
m^{(\varepsilon)}_{n}(k)-1
. Then by Definition-Lemma
4.2
, we can write

(4.3.1)

c_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\equiv k_{\varepsilon}\bmod{(p-1)}\\
m_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(k)\neq 0\end{subarray}}\hskip-10.0pt\big(A^{(n,\varepsilon)}_{k}(w)\cdot g_{n,\hat{k}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)\big)+h_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)\cdot g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w),

for some
h_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)\in\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket
as
c_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)\in\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket
.

Proposition 4.4
.

To prove Theorem
2.8
, it suffices to prove that, for every relevant character
\varepsilon
, every
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, and every ghost zero
w_{k}
of
g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)
, we have

(4.4.1)

v_{p}(A_{k,i}^{(n,\varepsilon)})\geq\Delta^{(\varepsilon)}_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}(\varepsilon_{1})-i}-\Delta^{(\varepsilon)\prime}_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}(\varepsilon_{1})-m_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(k)}\quad\textrm{for}\quad i=0,1,\dots,m_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(k)-1.

Proof.

We assume that (
4.4.1
) holds for every
\varepsilon
,
n
,
k
as above. Then Theorem
2.8
clearly follows from the following two claims:

Claim
1

Every point
(n,v_{p}(c_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star})))
lies on or above
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star},-))
.

Claim
2

If
\big(n,v_{p}(g^{(\varepsilon)}_{n}(w_{\star}))\big)
is a vertex of
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star},-))
, then
v_{p}(c_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star}))=v_{p}(g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star}))
.

Through the Lagrange interpolation (
4.3.1
), we will reduce the two Claims to the following.

Statement 4.5
.

For each relevant character
\varepsilon
, each
w_{\star}\in\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{C}_{p}}
and each
k=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{\bullet}
such that
m_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(k)\neq 0
,

(1)

The point
\big(n,v_{p}\big(A_{k}^{(n,\varepsilon)}(w_{\star})g^{(\varepsilon)}_{n,\hat{k}}(w_{\star})\big)\big)
lies on or above
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star},-))
; and

(2)

if
\big(n,v_{p}(g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star}))\big)
is a vertex of
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star},-))
, then
v_{p}\big(A_{k}^{(n,\varepsilon)}(w_{\star})g^{(\varepsilon)}_{n,\hat{k}}(w_{\star})\big)>v_{p}\big(g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star})\big)
.

Indeed, we will prove (a strengthened version of) this later in Proposition
4.7
. We now assume Statement
4.5
to finish the proof of Proposition
4.4
. For this, we fix a relevant character
\varepsilon
and omit it from the notations when no confusion arises.

Proof of
Claim

1
assuming Statement
4.5
(1)
.

Fix
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.
Since
h_{n}(w)\in\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket
, the last term in (
4.3.1
) satisfies that, for every
w_{\star}\in\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{C}_{p}}

v_{p}\big(h_{n}(w_{\star})\cdot g_{n}(w_{\star})\big)\geq v_{p}(g_{n}(w_{\star})).

By Statement
4.5
(1), the evaluations of all other terms in the Lagrange interpolation (
4.3.1
) at
w_{\star}
have
p
-adic valuations greater than or equal to
\NP\big(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star},-)\big)_{x=n}
. Claim 1 follows.

Proof of
Claim

2
assuming Statement
4.5
(2)
.

It is enough to show that, in the Lagrange interpolation (
4.3.1
),
h_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)\in\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket^{\times}
is a unit. Indeed, if this is known, and if
\big(n,v_{p}(g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star}))\big)
is a vertex of
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star},-))
, then Statement
4.5
(2) implies

v_{p}\big(A_{k}(w_{\star})g^{(\varepsilon)}_{n,\hat{k}}(w_{\star})\big)>v_{p}(g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star}))\quad\textrm{yet}\quad v_{p}\big(h_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star})g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star})\big)=v_{p}(g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star})).

From this, we deduce that
v_{p}(c_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star}))=v_{p}(g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star}))
.

Now we prove that
h_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)
is a unit. Since
\{a+s_{\varepsilon}\}-s_{\varepsilon}\equiv a\bmod(p-1)
and
a\not\equiv 0,\pm 1\bmod(p-1)
by our genericity assumption, it follows from Definition-Proposition
2.13
(1) that we can take one
k\not\equiv k_{\varepsilon}\bmod{(p-1)}
such that
d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\varepsilon\cdot(1\times\omega^{2-k}))=n
. Set
s_{\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}}:=\{k-2-a-s_{\varepsilon}\}
.
By Proposition
4.1
(2),
\big(n,v_{p}(c_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{k}))\big)
(resp.
\big(n,v_{p}(c_{n}^{(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})}(w_{k}))\big)
) is a vertex of
\NP(C^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{k},-))
(resp.
\NP(C^{(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})}(w_{k},-))
) and
\big(n,v_{p}(g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{k}))\big)
(resp.
\big(n,v_{p}(g_{n}^{(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})}(w_{k}))\big)
) is a vertex of
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{k},-))
(resp.
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})}(w_{k},-))
).

By a similar argument as in the proof of
Claim
1, we can use (
4.3.1
) to deduce that

v_{p}(c_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{k}))\geq v_{p}(g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{k}))\text{~and~}v_{p}(c_{n}^{(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})}(w_{k}))\geq v_{p}(g_{n}^{(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})}(w_{k})),

and the equalities hold if and only if
v_{p}(h_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{k}))=v_{p}(h_{n}^{(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})}(w_{k}))=0
.

Consider the Atkin–Lehner involution between
\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\varepsilon\cdot(1\times\omega^{2-k}))
and
\mathrm{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}\cdot(1\times\omega^{2-k}))
. By Proposition
2.12
(2) and Proposition
2.17
(2), we deduce that

v_{p}(c_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{k}))+v_{p}(c_{n}^{(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})}(w_{k}))=(k-1)n=v_{p}(g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{k}))+v_{p}(g_{n}^{(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})}(w_{k})).

This implies that
v_{p}(c_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{k}))=v_{p}(g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{k}))
and
v_{p}(c_{n}^{(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})}(w_{k}))=v_{p}(g_{n}^{(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})}(w_{k}))
. From this, we deduce that
h_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{k}),h_{n}^{(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})}(w_{k})\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
; so
h_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)
and
h_{n}^{(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})}(w)
are both units in
\mathcal{O}\llbracket w\rrbracket
. This completes the proof of Proposition
4.4
assuming Statement
4.5
.
∎

Here and later, we say two sets of points
P_{n^{\prime}}=(n^{\prime},A_{n^{\prime}})
and
Q_{n^{\prime}}=(n^{\prime},B_{n^{\prime}})
with integers
n^{\prime}\in[a,b]
are
differed by a linear function
if there exist real numbers
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
such that
B_{n^{\prime}}-A_{n^{\prime}}=\alpha{n^{\prime}}+\beta
for all integers
n^{\prime}\in[a,b]
.

We record here a “toolbox” result
[
LTXZ

24

, Proposition 5.16]
that we shall frequently use in the proof of Statement
4.5
. (Its proof is somewhat straightforward.)

Proposition 4.6
.

Fix
w_{\star}\in\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{C}_{p}}
and a weight
{k_{\alpha}}=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{\alpha\bullet}
. Let
\mathrm{nS}_{w_{\star},k_{\alpha}}^{(\varepsilon)}=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k_{\alpha}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})-L^{(\varepsilon)}_{w_{\star},k_{\alpha}},\,\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k_{\alpha}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})+L_{w_{\star},k_{\alpha}}^{(\varepsilon)}\big)
be a near-Steinberg range. Set
\overline{\mathrm{nS}}=\overline{\mathrm{nS}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{w_{\star},k_{\alpha}}=\big[\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k_{\alpha}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})-L^{(\varepsilon)}_{w_{\star},k_{\alpha}},\,\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k_{\alpha}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})+L_{w_{\star},k_{\alpha}}^{(\varepsilon)}\big]
for simplicity in this proposition.

(1)

For any
k_{\beta}=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{\beta\bullet}\neq k_{\alpha}
such that

v_{p}(w_{k_{\beta}}-w_{k_{\alpha}})\geq\Delta^{(\varepsilon)}_{k_{\alpha},L^{(\varepsilon)}_{w_{\star},k_{\alpha}}}-\Delta^{(\varepsilon)}_{k_{\alpha},L^{(\varepsilon)}_{w_{\star},k_{\alpha}}-1}
, the ghost multiplicity
m_{n^{\prime}}^{(\varepsilon)}(k_{\beta})
is linear in
n^{\prime}
when
n^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathrm{nS}}
.

(2)

Let
\mathbf{k}:=\{k_{\alpha},k_{1},\dots,k_{r}\}
be a set of integers with each
k_{i}=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{i\bullet}
.
Then for any set of constants
(A_{n^{\prime}})_{n^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathrm{nS}}}
,
the two lists of points

P_{n^{\prime}}=\big(n^{\prime},\,A_{n^{\prime}}+v_{p}(g^{(\varepsilon)}_{n^{\prime},\hat{\mathbf{k}}}(w_{\star}))\big),\quad Q_{n^{\prime}}=\big(n^{\prime},\,A_{n^{\prime}}+v_{p}(g^{(\varepsilon)}_{{n^{\prime}},\hat{\mathbf{k}}}(w_{k_{\alpha}}))\big)\quad\textrm{ with }{n^{\prime}}\in{\overline{\mathrm{nS}}},

differ by a linear function, where
g^{(\varepsilon)}_{{n^{\prime}},\hat{\mathbf{k}}}(w_{k_{\alpha}}):=g^{(\varepsilon)}_{{n^{\prime}},\hat{k}_{\alpha}}(w_{k_{\alpha}})\Big/\!\!\!\!\prod\limits_{k^{\prime}\in\mathbf{k},k^{\prime}\neq k_{\alpha}}\!\!(w_{k_{\alpha}}-w_{k^{\prime}})^{m_{n^{\prime}}^{(\varepsilon)}(k^{\prime})}
.

The following strengthens Statement
4.5
.

Proposition 4.7
.

Assume that
p\geq 7
.
Fix
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and a weight
k=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{\bullet}
so that
m_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(k)\neq 0
.
Fix
i\in\{0,\dots,m_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(k)-1\}
. Assume that
A\in\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{C}_{p}}
satisfies

(4.7.1)

v_{p}(A)\geq\Delta^{(\varepsilon)}_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}(\varepsilon_{1})-i}-\Delta^{(\varepsilon)\prime}_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}(\varepsilon_{1})-m_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(k)}.

(1)

For each
w_{\star}\in\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{C}_{p}}
, the point

\big(n,v_{p}\big(A(w_{\star}-w_{k})^{i}g^{(\varepsilon)}_{n,\hat{k}}(w_{\star})\big)\big)

lies on or above the Newton polygon
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star},-))
; and it lies strictly above this Newton polygon if
\big(n,v_{p}(g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{\star}))\big)
is a vertex;

(2)

If
w_{\star}=w_{k_{0}}
for some integer
k_{0}=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{0\bullet}\neq k
such that
m_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(k_{0})\neq 0
, we have an analogous statement: assuming condition (
4.7.1
), the point

\big(n,v_{p}\big(A(w_{k_{0}}-w_{k})^{i}g^{(\varepsilon)}_{n,\hat{k},\hat{k}_{0}}(w_{k_{0}})\big)\big)

lies on or above the lower convex hull of points
\big(n^{\prime},v_{p}(g^{(\varepsilon)}_{n^{\prime},\hat{k}_{0}}(w_{k_{0}}))\big)_{n^{\prime}\in[d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1}),d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})-d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{ur}}(\varepsilon_{1})]}
.

This proposition will be proved in §
4.11
.
Statement
4.5
and hence Proposition
4.4
follow by applying Proposition
4.7

to
A=A_{k,i}^{(n,\varepsilon)}
with each
i=0,\dots,m_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(k)-1
.

Remark 4.8
.

One might wish to replace the term
\Delta_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-i}-\Delta^{\prime}_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)}
in (
4.7.1
) by a more natural-looking expression such as
\Delta^{\prime}_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-i}-\Delta^{\prime}_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)}
or
\Delta_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-i}-\Delta_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)}
.
But it seems that (
4.7.1
) is the only expression for which our inductive proof works, for the following two reasons.

(1) The use of
-\Delta^{\prime}_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)}
is related the cofactor expansion argument in §
6
, reducing
A_{k,i}^{(n)}
to terms like
A_{k,j}^{(n-\ell)}
, where we need to multiply
A_{k,i}^{(n)}
with
g_{n,\hat{k}}(w_{k})
; see Notation
6.7
.

(2) The use of
\Delta_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-i}
is related to the inductive step, where we consider how the estimate of
A_{k,i}^{(n)}
would affect the
A_{k^{\prime},j}^{(n)}
for another
k^{\prime}
and
j\geq m_{n}(k^{\prime})
; such argument is similar to Proposition
4.7
(2) above. So we can only hope to prove for a factor of the form
\Delta_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-i}
; see also Remark
6.10
.

Remark 4.9
.

When
w_{\star}=w_{k_{0}}
is a ghost zero of
g_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(w)
, Proposition
4.7
(1) holds trivially, and Proposition
4.7
(2) can be regarded as a substitute in this case. Also, in view of (
2.18.1
), if we apply the linear map
(x,y)\mapsto\big(x-\frac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1}),y-\frac{k_{0}-2}{2}(x-\frac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1}))\big)
to all the points therein, Proposition
4.7
(2) is equivalent to that, assuming (
4.7.1
), the point

\Big(n-\frac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{Iw}},\ v_{p}(A)+(i-m_{n}(k))v_{p}(w_{k_{0}}-w_{k})+\Delta^{\prime}_{k_{0},n-\frac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}}\Big)

lies on or above the lower convex hull
\underline{\Delta}_{k_{0}}
defined in Definition-Proposition
2.18
. The latter is also equivalent to the equality

(4.9.1)

v_{p}(A)+(i-m_{n}(k))v_{p}(w_{k_{0}}-w_{k})+\Delta^{\prime}_{k_{0},\ell}\geq\Delta_{k_{0},\ell}

if we write
n=\frac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}+\ell
. Note that even though we replace the term
\Delta_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-i}-\Delta^{\prime}_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)}
in (
4.7.1
) by stronger estimate mentioned in Remark
4.8
, we do not know how to upgrade the estimate (
4.9.1
) to
v_{p}(A)+(i-m_{n}(k))v_{p}(w_{k_{0}}-w_{k})\geq 0
.

We first list several results that will be frequently used in the proof of Proposition
4.7
.

Lemma 4.10
.

Under the notations of Proposition
4.7
, we have

(1)

m_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(k)=\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}(\varepsilon_{1})-|n-\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})|
;

(2)

If we write
n=\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})+\ell
, then we have

v_{p}\big(g_{n,\hat{k},\hat{k}_{0}}^{(\varepsilon)}(w_{k})\big)=\Delta_{k,\ell}^{(\varepsilon)\prime}+\tfrac{k-2}{2}\cdot\ell-m_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}(k_{0})v_{p}(w_{k}-w_{k_{0}});

(3)

If
\mathrm{nS}_{w_{\star},k}^{(\varepsilon)}=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})-L,\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1})+L\big)
with
L=L_{w_{\star},k}^{(\varepsilon)}
is a near-Steinberg range, then for any
L^{\prime}\in\{0,\dots,\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}(\varepsilon_{1})\}
, we have

(4.10.1)

\Delta_{k,L^{\prime}}^{(\varepsilon)}+(L-L^{\prime})\cdot v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})\geq\Delta^{(\varepsilon)}_{k,L}.

Proof of Lemma
4.10
.

(1)
and
(2)
follow from a direct computation. For
(3)
, write
L=L_{w_{\star},k}
.
Then (
4.10.1
) is equivalent to

v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})\begin{cases}\geq\dfrac{\Delta_{k,L}-\Delta_{k,L^{\prime}}}{L-L^{\prime}}&\textrm{ if $L>L^{\prime}$}\\
\leq\dfrac{\Delta_{k,L^{\prime}}-\Delta_{k,L}}{L^{\prime}-L}&\textrm{ if $L^{\prime}>L$}.\end{cases}

But this follows from the definition of
L=L_{w_{\star},k}
in Definition
2.19
. Note that this argument also works for
w_{\star}=w_{k}
as in this case we have
L=\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}\geq L^{\prime}
and
v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})=+\infty
.
∎

4.11.
Proof of Proposition
4.7

Throughout this proof, the relevant character
\varepsilon
is fixed and suppressed from the notations.
We will treat the two parts of the proposition simultaneously and refer them as statement
(1)
and
(2)
respectively, using the following conventions.

(1)

For statement (1),
k_{0}
is an empty object (and hence
m_{n}(k_{0})=0
),
w_{\star}
is the given
w_{\star}\in\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{C}_{p}}
, and
we define an interval
\mathrm{I}:=[0,+\infty)
.

(2)

For statement (2),
k_{0}
is the given integer,
w_{\star}
is just
w_{k_{0}}
, and we define an interval
\mathrm{I}:=[d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{ur}},d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}-d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{ur}}]

Under these notations, the two statements can be expressed uniformly as follows:

•

The point
P:=(n,v_{p}(A(w_{\star}-w_{k})^{i}g_{n,\hat{k},\hat{k}_{0}}(w_{\star})))
lies on or above the lower convex hull of the points
(n^{\prime},v_{p}(g_{n^{\prime},\hat{k}_{0}}(w_{\star})))_{n^{\prime}\in\mathrm{I}}
. Moreover in statement (1), the point
P
lies strictly above this lower convex hull if
(n,v_{p}(g_{n}(w_{\star})))
is a vertex of
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(w_{\star},-))
.

Set
\ell:=n-\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}
and
L:=L_{w_{\star},k}
for simplicity. Since the statements involve whether the point
(n,v_{p}(g_{n}(w_{\star})))
is a vertex of
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(w_{\star},-))
, we will divide the discussion into two cases according to whether
n\in\mathrm{nS}_{w_{\star},k}
or not. When
n\notin\mathrm{nS}_{w_{\star},k}
, we further divide the argument into three sub-cases based on whether
n
belongs to some other near-Steinberg range
\mathrm{nS}_{w_{\star},k^{\prime}}
with
k^{\prime}\neq k
and whether
\Delta^{\prime}_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)}=\Delta_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)}
. The last one is a technical condition apapted to the estimate (
4.7.1
).

Case A:
Assume
n\in\mathrm{nS}_{w_{\star},k}
(and we allow

n\in\mathrm{nS}_{w_{\star},k^{\prime}}
for some
k^{\prime}=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k^{\prime}_{\bullet}\neq k
). By Proposition
2.20
(2)(5),
(n,v_{p}(g_{n}(w_{\star})))
is not a vertex of
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(w_{\star},-))
for statement (1) and
\big(n-\frac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{Iw}},\Delta^{\prime}_{k_{0},n-\frac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}}\big)
is not
a vertex of
\underline{\Delta}_{k_{0}}
for statement (2). It suffices to show that the point
P\big(n,v_{p}\big(A(w_{\star}-w_{k})^{i}g_{n,\hat{k},\hat{k}_{0}}(w_{\star})\big)\big)
lies on or above the line segment
\overline{Q_{-}Q_{+}}
with

Q_{-}:=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}-L,\ v_{p}\big(g_{\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}-L,\hat{k}_{0}}(w_{\star})\big)\big)\quad\textrm{and}\quad Q_{+}:=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}+L,\ v_{p}\big(g_{\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}+L,\hat{k}_{0}}(w_{\star})\big)\big).

Here
Q_{-}
and
Q_{+}
lie on or above the lower convex hull of the points
(n^{\prime},v_{p}(g_{n^{\prime},\hat{k}_{0}}(w_{\star})))_{n^{\prime}\in\mathrm{I}}
but are not necessarily vertices.

We rewrite the coordinates of
Q_{-}
and
Q_{+}
as

\displaystyle Q_{\pm}

\displaystyle=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}\pm L,\ m_{\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}\pm L}(k)v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})+v_{p}\big(g_{\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}\pm L,\hat{k},\hat{k}_{0}}(w_{\star})\big)\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}\pm L,\ (\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-L)v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})+v_{p}\big(g_{\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}\pm L,\hat{k},\hat{k}_{0}}(w_{\star})\big)\big).

We apply Proposition
4.6
(2) to the point
w_{\star}\in\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{C}_{p}}
, the weight
k_{\alpha}=k
, the set
\mathbf{k}=\{k,k_{0}\}
and the near-Steinberg range
\mathrm{nS}_{w_{\star},k}=\big(\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}-L,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}+L\big)
. Then the set of points
\{P,Q_{-},Q_{+}\}
and
\{P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}_{-},Q^{\prime}_{+}\}
differ by a linear function, where

\displaystyle P^{\prime}\

\displaystyle=\big(n,\,v_{p}(A)+i\cdot v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})+v_{p}\big(g_{n,\hat{\mathbf{k}}}(w_{k})\big)\big)\textrm{\quad and}

\displaystyle Q^{\prime}_{\pm}\

\displaystyle=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}\pm L,\,(\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-L)v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})+v_{p}\big(g_{\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}\pm L,\hat{\mathbf{k}}}(w_{k})\big)\big),

i.e. we replace the evaluation at
w_{\star}
in the definitions of
P
and
Q_{\pm}
by evaluation at
w_{k}
.

By Lemma
4.10
(2)
, we can write the coordinates of
P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}_{-},Q_{+}^{\prime}
as

\displaystyle P^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}+\ell,\,v_{p}(A)+i\cdot v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})-m_{\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}+\ell}(k_{0})v_{p}(w_{k}-w_{k_{0}})+\Delta_{k,\ell}^{\prime}+\tfrac{k-2}{2}\cdot\ell\big),

\displaystyle Q^{\prime}_{\pm}

\displaystyle=\big(\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}\pm L,\,(\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-L)v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})-m_{\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}\pm L}(k_{0})v_{p}(w_{k}-w_{k_{0}})+\Delta_{k,\pm L}^{\prime}+\tfrac{k-2}{2}\cdot(\pm L)\big).

Note that for statement (2), the condition
v_{p}(w_{k_{0}}-w_{k})=v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})\geq\Delta_{k,L}-\Delta_{k,L-1}
implies that the ghost multiplicity
m_{n^{\prime}}(k_{0})
is linear for
n^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathrm{nS}}_{w_{k_{0}},k}
by Proposition
4.6
(1).
Since
m_{n}(k_{0})\neq 0
, we have
\overline{\mathrm{nS}}_{w_{k_{0}},k}\subseteq[d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{ur}},d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}-d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{ur}}]
.

Now, the function
f(n^{\prime}):=\tfrac{k-2}{2}\big(n^{\prime}-\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}\big)-m_{n^{\prime}}(k_{0})v_{p}(w_{k}-w_{k_{0}})
is linear for
n^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathrm{nS}}_{w_{\star},k}=[\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}-L,\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}+L]
(with the function being a constant function for statement (1)). We apply the linear map
(x,y)\mapsto(x-\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}},y-f(x))
to the points
P^{\prime}
,
Q^{\prime}_{\pm}
, to get points

P^{\prime\prime}=\big(\ell,v_{p}(A)+i\cdot v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})+\Delta^{\prime}_{k,\ell}\big)\text{~and~}Q^{\prime\prime}_{\pm}=\big(\pm L,(\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-L)v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})+\Delta^{\prime}_{k,\pm L}\big).

So it suffices to show that the point
P^{\prime\prime}
lies on or above the line segment
\overline{Q^{\prime\prime}_{-}Q^{\prime\prime}_{+}}
. By ghost duality (
2.18.2
), we have
\Delta^{\prime}_{k,L}=\Delta_{k,-L}^{\prime}
. So it suffices to prove the inequality

v_{p}(A)+i\cdot v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})+\Delta^{\prime}_{k,\ell}\geq\big(\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-L\big)\cdot v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})+\Delta^{\prime}_{k,L}.

By Lemma
4.10
(1)
we have
|\ell|=\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)
. Combining with condition (
4.7.1
), we are reduced to prove

\Delta_{k,\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-i}+(i+L-\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}})\cdot v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})\geq\Delta^{\prime}_{k,L}.

This follows from Lemma
4.10
(3)
and the equality
\Delta^{\prime}_{k,L}=\Delta_{k,L}
. This concludes the proof of the proposition in Case A.

Case B
:
Assume
n\notin\mathrm{nS}_{w_{\star},k}
. Then Lemma
4.10
(1)
implies that
L\leq|n-\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}|=\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)
and hence

(4.11.1)

v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})<\Delta_{k,L+1}-\Delta_{k,L}\leq\Delta_{k,\frac{1}{2}d^{\mathrm{new}}_{k}-m_{n}(k)+1}-\Delta_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)}.

Adapted to the estimate (
4.7.1
), we divide the argument into several sub-cases:

Case B1
: Assume that the point
\big(\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k),\Delta^{\prime}_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)}\big)
is a vertex of
\underline{\Delta}_{k}
so that
\Delta^{\prime}_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)}=\Delta_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)}
.

In this case, we will prove that the point
P=\big(n,v_{p}\big(A(w_{\star}-w_{k})^{i}g_{n,\hat{k},\hat{k}_{0}}(w_{\star})\big)\big)
lies
strictly
above the point
\big(n,v_{p}\big(g_{n,\hat{k}_{0}}(w_{\star})\big)\big)
. Equivalently, we need to prove the strictly inequality

v_{p}(A)>(m_{n}(k)-i)\cdot v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k}).

But this is clear, as we argue as follows.

\displaystyle v_{p}(A)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{E:v(A) bigger than Lagrange bound}}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle\Delta_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-i}-\Delta^{\prime}_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-i}-\Delta_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)}

\displaystyle\hskip-10.0pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{convexity of $\underline{\Delta}_{k}$}}}{{\geq}}\hskip-10.0pt

\displaystyle(m_{n}(k)-i)\cdot\big(\Delta_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)+1}-\Delta_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)}\big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{E:not near-Steinberg}}}{{>}}

\displaystyle(m_{n}(k)-i)\cdot v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k}).

Case B2
: Assume the following two conditions:

(a)

the point
\big(\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k),\Delta^{\prime}_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)}\big)
is not a vertex of
\underline{\Delta}_{k}
, and

(b)

the point
(n,v_{p}(g_{n}(w_{\star})))
is a vertex of
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(w_{\star},-))
for statement (1) or the point
\big(n-\frac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{Iw}},\Delta^{\prime}_{k_{0},n-\frac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}}\big)
is a vertex of
\underline{\Delta}_{k_{0}}
for statement (2).

As in Case B1, we will prove that the point
P=\big(n,v_{p}\big(A(w_{\star}-w_{k})^{i}g_{n,\hat{k},\hat{k}_{0}}(w_{\star})\big)\big)
lies
strictly
above the point
\big(n,v_{p}\big(g_{n,\hat{k}_{0}}(w_{\star})\big)\big)
, or equivalently the strict inequality

(4.11.2)

v_{p}(A)>(m_{n}(k)-i)\cdot v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k}).

We first point out that, by Proposition
2.20
(2)(5), condition (b) implies that

(4.11.3)

n\notin\mathrm{nS}_{w_{\star},k^{\prime}}\text{~for any~}k^{\prime}=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k^{\prime}_{\bullet}\neq k_{0}.

By Proposition
2.20
(5)
, condition (a) implies that there exists
k^{\prime}=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k^{\prime}_{\bullet}
such that
n\in\mathrm{nS}_{w_{k},k^{\prime}}=\big(\frac{1}{2}d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}-L^{\prime},\frac{1}{2}d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}+L^{\prime}\big)
with
L^{\prime}=L_{w_{k},k^{\prime}}
. By Proposition
2.20
(4)
, the set of near-Steinberg ranges
\mathrm{nS}_{w_{k},k^{\prime}}
for all such
k^{\prime}
is nested. So we can choose
k^{\prime}
with the largest
L^{\prime}
. Then by Proposition
2.20
(4)(5)
, the points
\big(\frac{1}{2}d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}\pm L^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}},\Delta_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}\pm L^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}}\big)
are two consecutive vertices of
\underline{\Delta}_{k}
. From the fact
n\in\mathrm{nS}_{w_{k},k^{\prime}}
and Proposition
2.22
(note that here we use the assumption
p\geq 7
), we have

(4.11.4)

v_{p}(w_{k}-w_{k^{\prime}})\geq\Delta_{k^{\prime},L^{\prime}}-\Delta^{\prime}_{k^{\prime},L^{\prime}-1}\geq L^{\prime}+\tfrac{1}{2}.

Since
v_{p}(w_{k}-w_{k^{\prime}})\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have
v_{p}(w_{k}-w_{k^{\prime}})\geq L^{\prime}+1
and
v_{p}(k-k^{\prime})=v_{p}(k_{\bullet}-k^{\prime}_{\bullet})\geq L^{\prime}
.

If
v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})>v_{p}(w_{k}-w_{k^{\prime}})
, we have
v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k^{\prime}})=v_{p}(w_{k}-w_{k^{\prime}})
and hence
\mathrm{nS}_{w_{\star},k^{\prime}}=\mathrm{nS}_{w_{k},k^{\prime}}
contains
n
. But this contradicts ((
4.11.3
)). So we have
v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})\leq v_{p}(w_{k}-w_{k^{\prime}})
.

Set
\gamma:=v_{p}(k-k^{\prime})=v_{p}(k_{\bullet}-k^{\prime}_{\bullet})
. From the above discussion we have
\gamma\geq L^{\prime}\geq 1
. Thus
|k_{\bullet}-k^{\prime}_{\bullet}|\geq p^{\gamma}
. Then by Lemma
4.10
(1)
we have

(4.11.5)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)=

\displaystyle\ |n-\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}|\geq|\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}-\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}|-|n-\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}|

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle\ |k^{\prime}_{\bullet}-k_{\bullet}|-L^{\prime}\geq p^{\gamma}-\gamma>\gamma+1.

By Proposition
2.22
,

\displaystyle v_{p}(A)

\displaystyle\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{E:v(A) bigger than Lagrange bound}}}{{\geq}}\;\Delta_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-i}-\Delta^{\prime}_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)}\;>\;(m_{n}(k)-i)\big(\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)\big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{E:dknew -mnk bigger than gamma}}}{{>}}(m_{n}(k)-i)(\gamma+1)\geq(m_{n}(k)-i)\cdot v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k}).

This proves (
4.11.2
) and completes the proof in Case B2.

Case B3:
(Continue to assume
n\notin\mathrm{nS}_{w_{\star},k}
), we assume the following:

(a)

the point
\big(\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k),\Delta^{\prime}_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)}\big)
is not a vertex of
\underline{\Delta}_{k}
, and

(c)

the point
(n,v_{p}(g_{n}(w_{\star})))
is a not vertex of
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(w_{\star},-))
for statement (1) or the point
\big(n-\frac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{Iw}},\Delta^{\prime}_{k_{0},n-\frac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}}\big)
is not a vertex of
\underline{\Delta}_{k_{0}}
for statement (2).

We start the argument as in Case B2: condition (a) implies that there exists
k^{\prime}=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k^{\prime}_{\bullet}
such that
n\in\mathrm{nS}_{w_{k},k^{\prime}}=\big(\frac{1}{2}d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}-L^{\prime},\frac{1}{2}d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}+L^{\prime}\big)
with
L^{\prime}=L_{w_{k},k^{\prime}}
; take the largest such
k^{\prime}
.

If
v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})\leq v_{p}(w_{k}-w_{k^{\prime}})
, exactly the same argument as in Case B2 shows that
v_{p}(A)>(m_{n}(k)-i)\cdot v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})
; then the point
P=\big(n,v_{p}\big(A(w_{\star}-w_{k})^{i}g_{n,\hat{k},\hat{k}_{0}}(w_{\star})\big)\big)
lies
strictly
above the point
\big(n,v_{p}\big(g_{n,\hat{k}_{0}}(w_{\star})\big)\big)
. We are done in this case.

So in what follows, we assume
v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})>v_{p}(w_{k}-w_{k^{\prime}})
. So we have

(4.11.6)

v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})>\Delta^{\prime}_{k^{\prime},L^{\prime}}-\Delta^{\prime}_{k^{\prime},L^{\prime}-1}\quad\textrm{and}\quad v_{p}(w_{k}-w_{k^{\prime}})=v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k^{\prime}}),

and we have
\mathrm{nS}_{w_{k},k^{\prime}}=\mathrm{nS}_{w_{\star},k^{\prime}}
. Set
n_{\pm}=\frac{1}{2}d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}\pm L^{\prime}
so that
n\in\mathrm{nS}_{w_{k},k^{\prime}}=(n_{-},n_{+})
. It suffices to show the point
P\big(n,\,v_{p}\big(A(w_{\star}-w_{k})^{i}\cdot g_{n,\hat{k},\hat{k}_{0}}(w_{\star})\big)\big)
lies on or above the line segment
\overline{R_{-}R_{+}}
with
R_{\pm}=\big(n_{\pm},\,v_{p}\big(g_{n_{\pm},\hat{k}_{0}}(w_{\star})\big)\big)
.

Set
\mathbf{k}=\{k,k^{\prime},k_{0}\}
. We rewrite the coordinates of
P,R_{-}
and
R_{+}
as

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle=\big(n,\ v_{p}(A)+i\cdot v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})+m_{n}(k^{\prime})v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k^{\prime}})+v_{p}\big(g_{n,\hat{\mathbf{k}}}(w_{\star})\big)\big)

\displaystyle R_{\pm}

\displaystyle=\big(n_{\pm},\ m_{n_{\pm}}(k)\cdot v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})+m_{n_{\pm}}(k^{\prime})v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k^{\prime}})+v_{p}\big(g_{n_{\pm},\hat{\mathbf{k}}}(w_{\star})\big)\big).

Define six points as follows:

\displaystyle P^{\circ}

\displaystyle=\big(n,\ v_{p}(A)+i\cdot v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})+m_{n}(k^{\prime})v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k^{\prime}})+v_{p}\big(g_{n,\hat{\mathbf{k}}}(w_{k^{\prime}})\big)\big),

\displaystyle P^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\big(n,\ v_{p}(A)+i\cdot v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})+m_{n}(k^{\prime})v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k^{\prime}})+v_{p}\big(g_{n,\hat{\mathbf{k}}}(w_{k})\big)\big),

\displaystyle R^{\circ}_{\pm}

\displaystyle=\big(n_{\pm},\ m_{n_{\pm}}(k)\cdot v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})+m_{n_{\pm}}(k^{\prime})v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k^{\prime}})+v_{p}\big(g_{n_{\pm},\,\hat{\mathbf{k}}}(w_{k^{\prime}})\big)\big),

\displaystyle R^{\prime}_{\pm}

\displaystyle=\big(n_{\pm},\ m_{n_{\pm}}(k)\cdot v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})+m_{n_{\pm}}(k^{\prime})v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k^{\prime}})+v_{p}\big(g_{n_{\pm},\hat{\mathbf{k}}}(w_{k})\big)\big).

We apply Proposition
4.6
(2)
to the near-Steinberg range
\mathrm{nS}_{w_{\star},k^{\prime}}
and the set
\mathbf{k}
and see that the sets of points
\{P,R_{-},R_{+}\}
and
\{P^{\circ},R^{\circ}_{-},R^{\circ}_{+}\}
differ by a linear function. Similarly we apply Proposition
4.6
(2)
to
\mathrm{nS}_{w_{k},k^{\prime}}
and the set
\mathbf{k}
, and see that
\{P^{\prime},R_{-}^{\prime},R_{+}^{\prime}\}
and
\{P^{\circ},R_{-}^{\circ},R_{+}^{\circ}\}
differ by a linear function. Therefore it suffices to show that the point
P^{\prime}
lies on or above the line segment
\overline{R^{\prime}_{-}R^{\prime}_{+}}
. From (
4.11.6
), we can apply Proposition
4.6
(1)
to the near-Steinberg range
\mathrm{nS}_{w_{k},k^{\prime}}=\mathrm{nS}_{w_{\star},k^{\prime}}
and find that the ghost multiplicities
n^{\prime}\mapsto m_{n^{\prime}}(k)
and
n^{\prime}\mapsto m_{n^{\prime}}(k_{0})
are linear in
n^{\prime}
for
n^{\prime}\in[n_{-},n_{+}]
. So the function
f(s)=\frac{k-2}{2}(s-\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}})+m_{s}(k)\cdot v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})-m_{s}(k_{0})\cdot v_{p}(w_{k}-w_{k_{0}})
is a linear function in
s\in[n_{-},n_{+}]
. By Lemma
4.10
(2)
, if we apply the linear map
(x,y)\mapsto(x-\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}},y-f(x))
to the set of points
\{P^{\prime},R^{\prime}_{-},R^{\prime}_{+}\}
, we get
\{P^{\prime\prime},R^{\prime\prime}_{-},R^{\prime\prime}_{+}\}
with
P^{\prime\prime}=\big(\ell,v_{p}(A)+(i-m_{n}(k))\cdot v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})+\Delta_{k,\ell}^{\prime}\big)
and
R^{\prime\prime}_{\pm}=\big(n_{\pm}-\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}},\Delta^{\prime}_{k,n_{\pm}-\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}}\big)
.

By our choice of
k^{\prime}
,
R^{\prime\prime}_{\pm}
are two vertices of
\underline{\Delta}_{k}
. So it suffices to prove that

v_{p}(A)+(i-m_{n}(k))\cdot v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})+\Delta^{\prime}_{k,\ell}\geq\Delta_{k,\ell}.

By ghost duality (
2.18.2
) and Lemma
4.10
(1)
, we have
\Delta^{\prime}_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)}=\Delta^{\prime}_{k,|\ell|}=\Delta^{\prime}_{k,\ell}
and
\Delta_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)}=\Delta_{k,|\ell|}=\Delta_{k,\ell}
. In view of the estimate (
4.7.1
), it suffices to prove that

\Delta_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-i}+(i-m_{n}(k))\cdot v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})\geq\Delta_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)}.

This follows from
v_{p}(w_{\star}-w_{k})<\Delta_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)+1}-\Delta_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)}
as observed in (
4.11.1
) and the convexity of
\underline{\Delta}_{k}
. The proposition is proved in this case.

\Box

To sum up,
Proposition
4.7
completes the proof of Proposition
4.4
. In this section, we reduced the proof of Theorem
2.8
to proving the condition (
4.4.1
).

5.
Proof of local ghost conjecture II: halo bound estimates

In this section, we implement Step III of the proof of Theorem
2.8
as laid out at the beginning of §
4
; the Step II will be discussed in the next section. More precisely, we will initiate the proof of the key estimate (
4.4.1
) of the coefficients of Lagrange interpolation of terms in the characteristic power series. This is done by proving a similar result about the Lagrange interpolation of the determinant of every (not necessarily principal) minor in Theorem
5.2
below. We refer to Remark
5.3
the organization of its proof.

As in the previous section, we fix a primitive
\mathcal{O}\llbracket\mathrm{K}_{p}\rrbracket
-projective augmented module
\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}
of type
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
satisfying Hypothesis
2.10
, and we fix a character
\varepsilon=\omega^{-s_{\varepsilon}}\times\omega^{a+s_{\varepsilon}}
relevant to
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
; we suppress both
\varepsilon
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
entirely from the notation. For this and the next section, we assume that
p\geq 11
and
2\leq a\leq p-5
; this is used in the proof of Proposition
5.5
(1).

Notation 5.1
.

Let
\underline{\zeta}=\{\zeta_{1}<\dots<\zeta_{n}\}
and
\underline{\xi}=\{\xi_{1}<\dots<\xi_{n}\}
be two subsets of
n
positive integers, and let
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})
be the
(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})
-minor of the matrix of
U_{p}
-action with respect to the power basis (cf. §
2.11
and Notation
3.21
). Recall that in Notation
3.21
, for a finite subset
\underline{\zeta}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, we defined
\deg(\underline{\zeta}):=\sum\limits_{\zeta\in\underline{\zeta}}\deg\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}
.

We apply the Lagrange interpolation (Definition-Lemma
4.2
) to
p^{\frac{1}{2}(\deg(\underline{\xi})-\deg(\underline{\zeta}))}\cdot\mathrm{det}(\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi}))\in E\langle w/p\rangle
along
g_{n}(w)
. For every ghost zero
w_{k}
of
g_{n}(w)
, consider the formal expansion

(5.1.1)

p^{\frac{1}{2}(\deg(\underline{\xi})-\deg(\underline{\zeta}))}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{det}(\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi}))}{g_{n,\hat{k}}(w)}=\sum_{i\geq 0}A_{k,i}^{(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})}(w-w_{k})^{i}\text{~in~}E\llbracket w-w_{k}\rrbracket.

Let
A_{k}^{(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})}(w)=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{m_{n}(k)-1}A_{k,i}^{(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})}(w-w_{k})^{i}\in E[w]
be its truncation up to the term of degree
m_{n}(k)-1
. Then there exists
h_{\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi}}(w)\in E\langle w/p\rangle
such that

(5.1.2)

p^{\frac{1}{2}(\deg(\underline{\xi})-\deg(\underline{\zeta}))}\cdot\mathrm{det}\big(\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})\big)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\equiv k_{\varepsilon}\bmod{(p-1)}\\
m_{n}(k)\neq 0\end{subarray}}\hskip-10.0pt\big(A^{(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})}_{k}(w)\cdot g_{n,\hat{k}}(w)\big)+h_{\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi}}(w)\cdot g_{n}(w).

Note that by Definition-Proposition
3.23
, we have
A_{k,i}^{(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})}=0
for
i\leq m_{\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi}}(k)
.

Theorem 5.2
.

Assume that
2\leq a\leq p-5
.
For every finite subsets
\underline{\zeta}
and
\underline{\xi}
of size
n
, and every ghost zero
w_{k}
of
g_{n}(w)
, we have the following inequality for every
i=0,1,\dots,m_{n}(k)-1
,

(5.2.1)

v_{p}(A_{k,i}^{(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})})\geq\Delta_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-i}-\Delta^{\prime}_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k)}.

By the weak Hodge bound on
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}
in Proposition
3.2
, a standard argument (cf.
[
LTXZ

24

, § 2.10]
) shows that the sum
(-1)^{n}\sum\limits_{\underline{\xi}}\mathrm{det}\big(\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}(\underline{\xi}\times\underline{\xi})\big)
over all principal minors of size
n
converges in
\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle
and is equal to
c_{n}(w)
. So for each
n
and each ghost zero
w_{k}
of
g_{n}(w)
,

A_{k,i}^{(n)}=(-1)^{n}\sum_{\underline{\xi}}A_{k,i}^{(\underline{\xi}\times\underline{\xi})},

where
A_{k,i}^{(n)}
is the number defined in Notation
4.3
. So condition (
4.4.1
) (and hence Theorem
2.8
) follows from Theorem
5.2
above.

Remark 5.3
.

We will prove Theorem
5.2
by induction on
n
. In this section, after establishing the base case
n=1
in §
5.4
, we give a technical result (Proposition
5.5
below) that will play a crucial role in later inductive arguments; this is the main result for Step III of the proof of Theorem
2.8
and its proof will occupy the rest of this section. The proof of Theorem
5.2
will be concluded in §
6.11
(and §
6.19
).

5.4.
Proof of Theorem
5.2
when
n=1

Fix a ghost zero
w_{k}
of
g_{1}(w)
. The condition
m_{1}(k)>0
is equivalent to that
d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}=0
and
d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}\geq 2
. In particular we have
m_{1}(k)=1
and it suffices to prove (
5.2.1
) for
i=0
. From the construction of the convex hull
\underline{\Delta}_{k}
and the ghost duality (
2.18.2
), we have

\Delta_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}}=\Delta^{\prime}_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}}=\Delta^{\prime}_{k,-\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}}=v_{p}(g_{d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}},\hat{k}}(w_{k}))+\frac{k-2}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}{\mathrel{\to}^{d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}=0}}\frac{k-2}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}

and

\Delta^{\prime}_{k,\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-1}=\Delta^{\prime}_{k,1-\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}}{\mathrel{\to}^{d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}=0}}v_{p}(g_{1,\hat{k}}(w_{k}))+\frac{k-2}{2}\cdot\Big(\frac{1}{2}d_{k}^{\mathrm{new}}-1\Big).

As
n=1
, the set
\underline{\zeta}
(resp.
\underline{\xi}
) consists of a single integer
\zeta
(resp.
\xi
). Evaluating (
5.1.2
) at
w=w_{k}
, we get
A_{k,0}^{(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})}\cdot g_{1,\hat{k}}(w_{k})=p^{\frac{1}{2}(\deg\mathbf{e}_{\xi}-\deg\mathbf{e}_{\zeta})}\cdot\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{e}_{\zeta},\mathbf{e}_{\xi}}|_{w=w_{k}}
.
Therefore it suffices to prove that

(5.4.1)

v_{p}(\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{e}_{\zeta},\mathbf{e}_{\xi}}|_{w=w_{k}})\geq\tfrac{k-2}{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}(\deg\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}-\deg\mathbf{e}_{\xi}).

We divide our discussion into three cases:

(1)

Assume
\xi>d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}
. By the remark below (
2.11.2
), we have
\deg\mathbf{e}_{\xi}>k-2
. Combining with the inequality
v_{p}(\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{e}_{\zeta},\mathbf{e}_{\xi}}|_{w=w_{k}})\geq\deg(\mathbf{e}_{\zeta})
from Proposition
3.2
(2), we get (
5.4.1
);

(2)

Assume
\zeta>d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}
and
\xi\leq d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}
. By Proposition
2.12
(1), we have
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{e}_{\zeta},\mathbf{e}_{\xi}}|_{w=w_{k}}=0
and (
5.4.1
) follows;

(3)

Assume
\zeta,\xi\in\{1,\dots,d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}\}
. Note that the matrix
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}(\underline{d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}})|_{w=w_{k}}
coincides with the matrix
\mathrm{U}_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}
defined in Proposition
3.6
. Since
d_{k}^{\mathrm{ur}}=0
, the matrix
\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}(\underline{d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}})|_{w=w_{k}}=-\mathrm{L}_{k}^{\mathrm{cl}}
is anti-diagonal by Proposition
3.6
(1), whose
(\zeta,\zeta^{\mathrm{op}})
-entry is precisely
-p^{\deg\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}}
, where
\zeta^{\mathrm{op}}=d_{k}^{\mathrm{Iw}}+1-\zeta
. It suffices to verify (
5.4.1
) for these entries. By Proposition
2.12
(2), we have
\deg\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}+\deg\mathbf{e}_{\zeta^{\mathrm{op}}}=k-2
. So

v_{p}(\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{e}_{\zeta},\mathbf{e}_{\xi}}|_{w=w_{k}})=\deg\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}=\tfrac{k-2}{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}(\deg\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}-\deg\mathbf{e}_{\zeta^{\mathrm{op}}}).

This completes the proof of Theorem
5.2
when
n=1
.

\Box

The following is the main result for Step III in the proof of Theorem
2.8
.

Proposition 5.5
.

Assume that
p\geq 11
and that
2\leq a\leq p-5
.
Fix two subsets
\underline{\zeta}
and
\underline{\xi}
of positive integers of cardinality
n
. Assume that for every ghost zero
w_{k}
of
g_{n}(w)
, the inequality (
5.2.1
) holds.

(1)

We have
h_{\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi}}(w)\in\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle
.

(2)

For every ghost zero
w_{k_{0}}
of
g_{n}(w)
, we have the following estimate:

(5.5.1)

v_{p}\big(A_{k_{0},i}^{(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})}\big)\geq\begin{cases}\Delta_{k_{0},\frac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k_{0})}-\Delta^{\prime}_{k_{0},\frac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k_{0})},&\text{if~}i=m_{n}(k_{0}),\\
\tfrac{1}{2}\big((\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{new}}-i)^{2}-(\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k_{0}))^{2}\big),&\text{if~}m_{n}(k_{0})<i\leq\frac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{new}}-1.\end{cases}

(3)

For every integer
k_{0}=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{0\bullet}
such that
d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{ur}}\geq n
(in particular
w_{k_{0}}
is not a ghost zero of
g_{n}(w)
), if we consider the formal expansion in
E\llbracket w-w_{k_{0}}\rrbracket
:

(5.5.2)

p^{\frac{1}{2}(\deg(\underline{\xi})-\deg(\underline{\zeta}))}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{det}(\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi}))}{g_{n}(w)}=\sum_{i\geq 0}A_{k_{0},i}^{(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})}(w-w_{k_{0}})^{i},

then we have the estimate

(5.5.3)

v_{p}\big(A_{k_{0},i}^{(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})}\big)\geq\begin{cases}\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(w_{k_{0}},-))_{x=n}-v_{p}\big(g_{n}(w_{k_{0}})\big),&\text{if~}i=0,\\
\tfrac{1}{2}\big((\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{new}}-i)^{2}-(\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{new}})^{2}\big),&\text{if~}i=1,\dots,\frac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{new}}-1.\end{cases}

Here
\NP(G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(w_{k_{0}},-))_{x=n}
denotes the
y
-coordinate of the Newton polygon at
x=n
.

Remark 5.6
.

(1)

This proposition involves the coefficients of the Taylor expansion of some determinant of the minor with exponent
greater than or equal to
the corresponding ghost multiplicity; in contrast, condition (
5.2.1
) concerns the coefficients in the Taylor expansions of
p^{\frac{1}{2}(\deg(\underline{\xi})-\deg(\underline{\zeta}))}\cdot\mathrm{det}\big(\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})\big)/g_{n,\hat{k}}(w)
with exponents
strictly less
than the corresponding ghost multiplicity.

(2)

In (
5.5.1
), we do not hope to prove
v_{p}(A_{k_{0},i}^{(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})})\geq 0
when
i=m_{n}(k_{0})
. This is because we need to take into account of the effect of terms of the form
A_{k,j}^{(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})}(w-w_{k})^{j}
with
k\neq k_{0}
. For such terms, the probably best estimate comes from an argument similar to Proposition
4.7
(2) which is essentially about
\Delta_{k_{0},\frac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k_{0})}
.

Notation 5.7
.

For every integer
k=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{\bullet}
, if
w_{k}
is not a ghost zero of
g_{n}(w)
, we set
g_{n,\hat{k}}(w)=g_{n}(w)
. Under this notation, the formal expansion (
5.1.1
) in Notation
5.1
makes sense for all such
k
and coincides with the formal expansion (
5.5.2
) in Proposition
5.5
(3)
when
w_{k}=w_{k_{0}}
is not a zero of
g_{n}(w)
.

Lemma 5.8
.

To prove Proposition
5.5
, it suffices to show that (under the hypothesis therein)

(5.8.1)

\mathrm{det}\big(\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})\big)\in p^{\frac{1}{2}(\deg(\underline{\zeta})-\deg(\underline{\xi}))+\deg g_{n}}\cdot\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle.

Proof.

We first point out that, under Notation
5.7
, we always have

\displaystyle p^{\frac{1}{2}(\deg(\underline{\xi})-\deg(\underline{\zeta}))}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{det}(\mathrm{U}^{\dagger}(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi}))}{g_{n,\hat{k}_{0}}(w)}

(5.8.2)

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\equiv k_{\varepsilon}\bmod{(p-1)}\\
m_{n}(k)\neq 0\end{subarray}}\hskip-10.0pt\Big(\sum_{j=0}^{m_{n}(k)-1}\frac{A_{k,j}^{(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})}(w-w_{k})^{j}g_{n,\hat{k}}(w)}{g_{n,\hat{k}_{0}}(w)}\Big)+h_{\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi}}(w)(w-w_{k_{0}})^{m_{n}(k_{0})}.

By Definition-Lemma
4.2
(2), if (
5.8.1
) holds, then
h_{\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi}}(w)\in\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle
. This proves Proposition
5.5
(1).
To prove Proposition
5.5
(2) and (3), it suffices to prove that, for each summand in (
5.8.2
), the coefficients of its expansion in
E\llbracket w-w_{k}\rrbracket
satisfy the same estimate in (
5.5.1
) and (
5.5.3
).
Now, we fix the
k_{0}=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k_{0\bullet}
as in Proposition
5.5
(2)(3); we treat both cases simultaneously.

First, we treat the term
h_{\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi}}(w)(w-w_{k_{0}})^{m_{n}(k_{0})}
. We can formally write

h_{\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi}}(w)(w-w_{k_{0}})^{m_{n}(k_{0})}=\sum_{i\geq m_{n}(k_{0})}h_{k_{0},i-m_{n}(k_{0})}(w-w_{k_{0}})^{i},

where the assumption
h_{\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi}}(w)\in\mathcal{O}\langle w/p\rangle
(and the fact
v_{p}(w_{k_{0}})\geq 1
) imply that

(5.8.3)

v_{p}(h_{k_{0},i-m_{n}(k_{0})})\geq m_{n}(k_{0})-i\text{\quad for all~}i\geq m_{n}(k_{0}).

In this case, we will prove the following estimate:

(5.8.4)

v_{p}(h_{k_{0},i-m_{n}(k)})\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\big((\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{new}}-i)^{2}-(\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k_{0}))^{2}\big)

for
i=m_{n}(k_{0}),\dots,\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{new}}-1
, which is slightly stronger than Proposition
5.5
(2)(3) when
i=m_{n}(k_{0})
.
Given the estimate (
5.8.3
), this follows immediately from the following inequality:

i-m_{n}(k_{0})\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\big((\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k_{0}))^{2}-(\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{new}}-i)^{2}\big)=\tfrac{1}{2}(i-m_{n}(k_{0}))\cdot(\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{new}}-m_{n}(k_{0})+\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{new}}-i),

which holds under the assumption
m_{n}(k_{0})\leq i\leq\tfrac{1}{2}d_{k_{0}}^{\mathrm{new}}-1
.

Now we consider the term
\frac{A_{k,j}^{(\underline{\zeta}\times\underline{\xi})}(w-w_{k})^{j}g_{n,\hat{k}}(w)}{g_{n,\hat{k}_{0}}(w)}
for a ghost zero
w_{k}
