; this contradicts with
[
LTXZ

24

, Lemmas 5.6 and 5.8]
(which says that the “first”
p-1
points on
\underline{\Delta}_{k}
are vertices). This completes the proof of the Claim in Step II.

Step III
: Write
m(H):=m(H,1)
and
m(H^{\prime}):=m(H^{\prime},1)
. We will prove inductively that
m(H,n)=n\cdot m(H)
and
m(H^{\prime},n)=n\cdot m(H^{\prime})
.
The inductive base is clear. Suppose that
m(H,i)=i\cdot m(H)
and
m(H^{\prime},i)=i\cdot m(H^{\prime})
holds for
i=1,\dots,n
(with
n\geq 1
). We will prove this for
i=n+1
. For this
n
, take the weight
k
as in Step II.

By Step II(1),
\eta_{w_{k},\Delta_{k,1}-\Delta_{k,0}-\epsilon}
belongs to both
\overline{\mathrm{Vtx}}_{n,\geq\lambda}^{\mathrm{Berk}}
and
\overline{\mathrm{Vtx}}_{n+1,\geq\lambda}^{\mathrm{Berk}}
for all
\epsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
. By Step I and the inductive hypothesis, we have

|h_{m(H,n+1)}(\eta_{w_{k},\Delta_{k,1}-\Delta_{k,0}-\epsilon})|=\Big|g_{n}^{m(H)}(\eta_{w_{k},\Delta_{k,1}-\Delta_{k,0}-\epsilon})\cdot\Big(\frac{g_{n+1}}{g_{n}}\Big)^{m(H,n+1)-m(H,n)}(\eta_{w_{k},\Delta_{k,1}-\Delta_{k,0}-\epsilon})\Big|.

By continuity, the above equality holds for
\epsilon=0
as well.
So in particular, for the slope derivatives at
\eta_{w_{k},\Delta_{k,1}-\Delta_{k,0}}
defined in (
9.10.1
), we have

(9.11.2)

V_{w_{k},\Delta_{k,1}-\Delta_{k,0}}^{+}(h_{m(H,n+1)})=V_{w_{k},\Delta_{k,1}-\Delta_{k,0}}^{+}\Big(g_{n}^{m(H)}\cdot\Big(\frac{g_{n+1}}{g_{n}}\Big)^{m(H,n+1)-m(H,n)}\Big).

On the other hand, by Step II(2)(3), for every
\alpha\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}_{p}}
and any
\epsilon\in[0,\frac{1}{2})
, the point
\eta_{w_{k}+\alpha p^{\Delta_{k,1}-\Delta_{k,0}},\Delta_{k,1}-\Delta_{k,0}+\epsilon}
is contained in
\overline{\mathrm{Vtx}}_{n,\geq\lambda}^{\mathrm{Berk}}
and
\overline{\mathrm{Vtx}}_{n-2,\geq\lambda}^{\mathrm{Berk}}
but not in
\overline{\mathrm{Vtx}}_{n-1,\geq\lambda}^{\mathrm{Berk}}
. It follows that the Newton polygon of
G_{\boldsymbol{\sigma},\mathrm{nord}}(\mathsf{w},-)
at each of those points is a straight line of width
2
from
n-1
to
n+1
. We therefore deduce that for
\bar{\alpha}\in\overline{\mathbb{F}}
,

(9.11.3)

V_{w_{k},\Delta_{k,1}-\Delta_{k,0}}^{\bar{\alpha}}(h_{m(H,n+1)})=V_{w_{k},\Delta_{k,1}-\Delta_{k,0}}^{\bar{\alpha}}\Big(g_{n-1}^{m(H)}\cdot\Big(\frac{g_{n+1}}{g_{n-1}}\Big)^{(m(H,n+1)-m(H,n-1))/2}\Big).

Taking the sum of (
9.11.2
) and (
9.11.3
) for all
\bar{\alpha}\in\overline{\mathbb{F}}
and using the harmonicity equality (
9.10.2
) (for
h_{m(H,n+1)}
in the first equality and for
g_{n+1}
and
g_{n-1}
in the third equality), we deduce that

\displaystyle 0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{E:harmonicity}}}{{=}}\

\displaystyle V^{+}_{w_{k},\Delta_{k,1}-\Delta_{k,0}}(h_{m(H,n+1)})+\sum_{\bar{\alpha}\in\overline{\mathbb{F}}}V^{\bar{\alpha}}_{w_{k},\Delta_{k,1}-\Delta_{k,0}}(h_{m(H,n+1)})

\displaystyle=\ \

\displaystyle V_{w_{k},\Delta_{k,1}-\Delta_{k,0}}^{+}\Big(g_{n}^{m(H)}\cdot\Big(\frac{g_{n+1}}{g_{n}}\Big)^{m(H,n+1)-m(H,n)}\Big)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\bar{\alpha}\in\overline{\mathbb{F}}}V_{w_{k},\Delta_{k,1}-\Delta_{k,0}}^{\bar{\alpha}}\Big(g_{n-1}^{m(H)}\cdot\Big(\frac{g_{n+1}}{g_{n-1}}\Big)^{(m(H,n+1)-m(H,n-1))/2}\Big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{E:harmonicity}}}{{=}}\

\displaystyle V_{w_{k},\Delta_{k,1}-\Delta_{k,0}}^{+}\Big(\Big(\frac{g_{n+1}g_{n-1}}{g_{n}^{2}}\Big)^{(m(H,n+1)-m(H,n)-m(H))/2}\Big).

(The third equality also makes use of
m(H,n)-m(H,n-1)=m(H)
on the exponents of
g_{n+1}
and
g_{n-1}
.)

To show that
m(H,n+1)=(n+1)\cdot m(H)
, or equivalently
m(H,n+1)-m(H,n)=m(H)
, it then suffices to show that

(9.11.4)

2V_{w_{k},\Delta_{k,1}-\Delta_{k,0}}^{+}(g_{n})\neq V_{w_{k},\Delta_{k,1}-\Delta_{k,0}}^{+}(g_{n+1})+V_{w_{k},\Delta_{k,1}-\Delta_{k,0}}^{+}(g_{n-1}).

By definition, for
i\in\{n-1,n,n+1\}
, we have

(9.11.5)

V_{w_{k},\Delta_{k,1}-\Delta_{k,0}}^{+}(g_{i})=\sum_{v_{p}(w_{k^{\prime}}-w_{k})\geq\Delta_{k,1}-\Delta_{k,0}}m_{i}(k^{\prime})

is the sum of ghost zero multiplicities for those weights
k^{\prime}=k_{\varepsilon}+(p-1)k^{\prime}_{\bullet}
such that
v_{p}(w_{k^{\prime}}-w_{k})\geq\Delta_{k,1}-\Delta_{k,0}
.
Note that the function
i\mapsto m_{i}(k^{\prime})
is linear over
i\in\{n-1,n,n+1\}
except when
i
is equal to
\frac{1}{2}d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}
,
d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}-d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{ur}}
, and
d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{ur}}
.
We claim that this exactly happens when
k^{\prime}=k
, and therefore (as
2m_{n}(k)-m_{n+1}(k)-m_{n-1}(k)=2
,) we deduce that

2V_{w_{k},\Delta_{k,1}-\Delta_{k,0}}^{+}(g_{n})-V_{w_{k},\Delta_{k,1}-\Delta_{k,0}}^{+}(g_{n+1})-V_{w_{k},\Delta_{k,1}-\Delta_{k,0}}^{+}(g_{n-1})=2.

To see the claim, we note that, by the definition of near-Steinberg range in Definition
2.19
, the condition
v_{p}(w_{k^{\prime}}-w_{k})\geq\Delta_{k,1}-\Delta_{k,0}
implies that
n-1
belongs to the near-Steinberg range for
(w_{k^{\prime}},k)
. Yet Proposition
2.20
(1) (for
L_{w_{k^{\prime}},k}\geq 1
) implies that the condition
v_{p}(k^{\prime}_{\bullet}-k_{\bullet})\geq\Delta_{k,1}-\Delta_{k,0}
excludes the case that
i=d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Iw}}-d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{ur}}
or
i=d_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{ur}}
. So the only
k^{\prime}
that appears in the sum of (
9.11.5
) and that
i\mapsto m_{i}(k^{\prime})
is not linear is when
k^{\prime}=k
.
This proves the claim and thus
(
9.11.4
), which concludes the inductive proof of Step III.

Appendix A

Some linear algebra and
p
-adic analysis

Notation A.1
.

Let
n
be a positive integer.
Write
\underline{n}=\{1,\dots,n\}
.
For a subset
I
of
\underline{n}
, write
I^{\mathsf{c}}:=\underline{n}-I
, and write
\mathrm{sgn}(I,\underline{n})
for the sign of the permutation from
\underline{n}
(in increasing order) to the ordered disjoint union
I\sqcup I^{\mathsf{c}}
, where both
I
and
I^{\mathsf{c}}
are ordered increasingly.

Write
\underline{\infty}=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.
Let
R
be a ring. For
m
and
n
positive integers or infinity, write
\mathrm{M}_{m\times n}(R)
for the space of matrices of size
m\times n
, with entries in
R
. Let
A\in\mathrm{M}_{m\times n}(R)
. For
i\in\underline{m}
and
j\in\underline{n}
, write
A_{i,j}
for the
(i,j)
-entry of
A
; for two subsets
I\subseteq\underline{m}
and
J\subseteq\underline{n}
, write
A(I\times J)
for the submatrix of
A
whose rows are from
I
and whose columns are from
J
, where indices are in increasing order.

Lemma A.2
.

(1)

If
I
is a subset of
\underline{n}
and
I^{\prime}
is a subset of
I^{\mathsf{c}}
, put
I^{\prime\prime}:=I\sqcup I^{\prime}
, then

\mathrm{sgn}(I,\underline{n})\mathrm{sgn}(I^{\prime\prime}-I,I^{\mathsf{c}})=\mathrm{sgn}(I^{\prime\prime},\underline{n})\mathrm{sgn}(I,I^{\prime\prime}).

(2)

Let
m\leq n
and let
A\in\mathrm{M}_{n\times n}(R)
be a matrix. Then we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq\underline{n}\\
\#I=m\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\subseteq\underline{n}\\
\#J=m\end{subarray}}\mathrm{sgn}(I,\underline{n})\mathrm{sgn}(J,\underline{n})\cdot\mathrm{det}(A(I\times J))\cdot\mathrm{det}(A(I^{\mathsf{c}}\times J^{\mathsf{c}}))=\binom{n}{m}\mathrm{det}(A).

(3)

Let
A,B\in\mathrm{M}_{n\times n}(R)
be two matrices. We have

\mathrm{det}(A+B)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I,J\subseteq\underline{n}\\
\#I=\#J\end{subarray}}\mathrm{sgn}(I,\underline{n})\mathrm{sgn}(J,\underline{n})\cdot\mathrm{det}(A(I\times J))\cdot\mathrm{det}(B(I^{\mathsf{c}}\times J^{\mathsf{c}})).

Proof.

(1) Consider the permutation
\sigma
that first sends
\underline{n}
to the ordered disjoint union
I\sqcup I^{\mathsf{c}}
, and then sends
I^{\mathsf{c}}
to the disjoint union
I^{\prime}\sqcup I^{\prime\prime\mathsf{c}}
. This permutation has sign
\mathrm{sgn}(I,\underline{n})\mathrm{sgn}(I^{\prime},I^{\mathsf{c}})
and sends
\underline{n}
to the ordered disjoint union
I\sqcup I^{\prime}\sqcup I^{\prime\prime\mathsf{c}}
. On the other hand,
\sigma
can be rewritten as first sending
\underline{n}
to
I^{\prime\prime}\sqcup I^{\prime\prime\mathsf{c}}
and then sending
I^{\prime\prime}
to
I\sqcup I^{\prime}
. Thus, this permutation also has sign
\mathrm{sgn}(I^{\prime\prime},\underline{n})\mathrm{sgn}(I,I^{\prime\prime})
. (1) is proved.

(2) For a fixed
I
, the sum on the left is equal to
\mathrm{det}(A)
by standard cofactor expansion of the determinant. The number of choices of such
I
is
\binom{n}{m}
. (2) follows.

(3) This elementary formula can be found for example in
[
Ma90
, Equation (1)]
.
∎

Lemma A.3
.

Let
R
be a topological ring, and let
n
be positive integers and
m
a positive integer greater than or equal to
n
, or infinity.

(1)

Let
A\in\mathrm{M}_{n\times m}(R)
and
B\in\mathrm{M}_{m\times n}(R)
be matrices such that the product
AB
converges. Then we have

\mathrm{det}(AB)=\sum_{\underline{\lambda}\subseteq\underline{m},\,\#\underline{\lambda}=n}\mathrm{det}\big(A(\underline{n}\times\underline{\lambda})\big)\cdot\mathrm{det}\big(B(\underline{\lambda}\times\underline{n})\big),

where the sum is over all subsets
\underline{\lambda}
of
\underline{m}
of cardinality
n
.

(2)

Let
A\in\mathrm{M}_{n\times m}(R)
,
B\in\mathrm{M}_{m\times m}(R)
, and
C\in\mathrm{M}_{m\times n}(R)
be matrices such that the product
ABC
converges. Then we have

\mathrm{det}(ABC)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\underline{\lambda},\underline{\eta}\subseteq\underline{m}\\
\#\underline{\lambda}=\#\underline{\eta}=n\end{subarray}}\mathrm{det}\big(A(\underline{n}\times\underline{\lambda})\big)\cdot\mathrm{det}\big(B(\underline{\lambda}\times\underline{\eta})\big)\cdot\mathrm{det}\big(C(\underline{\eta}\times\underline{n})\big).

Proof.

(1)
By a direct computation we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{det}(AB)=\

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n}(AB)_{\sigma(i),i}=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Big(\sum_{\lambda_{i}\in\underline{m}}A_{\sigma(i),\lambda_{i}}B_{\lambda_{i},i}\Big)

(A.3.1)

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}\in\underline{m}}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n}A_{i,\lambda_{\sigma^{-1}(i)}}B_{\lambda_{i},i}.

Consider the multiset
\underline{\lambda}
constructed from
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}
. We may first sum over all such possible multiset
\underline{\lambda}
of size
n
and then sum over all numberings of elements of
\underline{\lambda}
into
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}
. This way, if some
\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{j}
for
i\neq j
, then in the sum (
A.3.1
) above, the term associated to
\sigma
and the term associated to
\sigma(ij)
are the same and hence got canceled because of the different sign.
It follows that,
(
A.3.1
)
is equal to

\mathrm{det}(AB)=\sum_{\underline{\lambda}\subseteq\underline{m}}\sum_{\tau\in S_{n}}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)\prod_{i=1}^{n}A_{i,\lambda_{\tau(\sigma^{-1}(i))}}B_{\lambda_{\tau(i)},i},

where the sum is over all subsets
\underline{\lambda}
(as opposed to multisets) of
\underline{m}
of size
n
and the elements in
\underline{\lambda}
is ordered so that
\lambda_{1}<\cdots<\lambda_{n}
. Reorganizing, this is further equal to

\displaystyle\mathrm{det}(AB)=\

\displaystyle\sum_{\underline{\lambda}\subseteq\underline{m}}\sum_{\tau\in S_{n}}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\Big(\mathrm{sgn}(\tau\sigma^{-1})\prod_{i=1}^{n}A_{i,\lambda_{\tau\sigma^{-1}(i)}}\Big)\cdot\Big(\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)\prod_{i=1}^{n}B_{\lambda_{\tau(i)},i}\Big)

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\sum_{\underline{\lambda}\subseteq\underline{m}}\mathrm{det}\big(A(\underline{n}\times\underline{\lambda})\big)\cdot\mathrm{det}\big(B(\underline{\lambda}\times\underline{n})\big).

(2) Applying (1) to the product
A\cdot(BC)
gives

\mathrm{det}(ABC)=\sum_{\underline{\lambda}\subseteq\underline{m},\,|\#\underline{\lambda}=n}\mathrm{det}(A(\underline{n}\times\underline{\lambda}))\cdot\mathrm{det}\big((BC)(\underline{\lambda}\times\underline{n})\big).

Then apply (1) to each of
(BC)(\underline{\lambda}\times\underline{n})
gives

\mathrm{det}(ABC)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\underline{\lambda},\underline{\eta}\subset\underline{m}\\
\#\underline{\lambda}=\#\underline{\eta}=n\end{subarray}}\mathrm{det}\big(A(\underline{n}\times\underline{\lambda})\big)\cdot\mathrm{det}\big(B(\underline{\lambda}\times\underline{\eta})\big)\cdot\mathrm{det}\big(C(\underline{\eta}\times\underline{n})\big).\qed

Notation A.4
.

For
n=\sum\limits_{i\geq 0}n_{i}p^{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
with
n_{i}\in\{0,\dots,p-1\}
, set
\mathrm{Dig}(n)=\sum\limits_{i\geq 0}n_{i}
.

Lemma A.5
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we have

(1)

v_{p}(n!)=\frac{n-\mathrm{Dig}(n)}{p-1}
;

(2)

v_{p}(n!)=\lfloor n/p\rfloor+v_{p}(\lfloor n/p\rfloor!)
; and

(3)

when
n\geq p
,
v_{p}(n!)\geq pv_{p}(\lfloor n/p\rfloor!)+1
.

Proof.

(1) is well known. For (2) and (3), write
n=mp+b
with
m=\lfloor n/p\rfloor
and
b\in\{0,\dots,p-1\}
. Then
\mathrm{Dig}(n)=\mathrm{Dig}(m)+b
. Then (1) implies that

\displaystyle v_{p}(n!)\

\displaystyle=\tfrac{(pm+b)-(\mathrm{Dig}(m)+b)}{p-1}=m+\tfrac{m-\mathrm{Dig}(m)}{p-1}=\lfloor n/p\rfloor+v_{p}(\lfloor n/p\rfloor!),\quad\textrm{and}

\displaystyle v_{p}(n!)\

\displaystyle=\tfrac{(pm+b)-(\mathrm{Dig}(m)+b)}{p-1}\geq p\tfrac{m-\mathrm{Dig}(m)}{p-1}+\mathrm{Dig}(m)\geq pv_{p}(\lfloor n/p\rfloor!)+1\quad\textrm{if }m\geq 1.\qed

Lemma A.6
.

Let
m,n
be two positive integers such that
m-n\geq 2
. Then we have

v_{p}\Big(\frac{m!}{n!}\Big)\leq\gamma+\Big\lfloor\frac{m-n-2}{p-1}\Big\rfloor,\text{~with~}\gamma=\max\{v_{p}(i)\,|\,i=n+1,\dots,m\}.

Proof.

By Lemma
A.5
(1),

v_{p}\Big(\frac{m!}{n!}\Big)=\frac{m-n+\mathrm{Dig}(n)-\mathrm{Dig}(m)}{p-1}.

It suffices to show that
(p-1)\gamma\geq\mathrm{Dig}(n)-\mathrm{Dig}(m)+2
. If we write
m=m_{0}+m_{1}p+\cdots
and
n=n_{0}+n_{1}p+\cdots
in their
p
-adic expansions. The definition of
\gamma
implies that
m_{i}=n_{i}
when
i\geq\gamma+1
and
m_{\gamma}\geq n_{\gamma}+1
. We are left to prove that

(A.6.1)

(p-1)\gamma\geq 2+\sum\limits_{i=0}^{\gamma}(n_{i}-m_{i})

Since
n_{i}-m_{i}\leq p-1
for every
i=0,\dots,\gamma-1
and
n_{\gamma}-m_{\gamma}\leq-1
, (
A.6.1
) already holds, except in the worst scenario where all inequalities above holds. Yet in this case, we are forced to have
m-n=1
, which contradicts our assumption. The Lemma is then proved.
∎

Recall from Notation
3.25
, for two nonnegative integers
m,n
, write
m=m_{0}+pm_{1}+\cdots
and
n=n_{0}+pn_{1}+\cdots
for their
p
-adic expansions (so that each
m_{i}
and
n_{i}
belong to
\{0,\dots,p-1\}
).
Let
D(m,n)
denote the number of indices
i\geq 0
such that
n_{i+1}>m_{i}
.

Lemma A.7
.

Let
m,n
be two nonnegative integers.

(1)

We have
D(m+1,n)+1\geq D(m,n)
and
D(m,n)+1\geq D(m,n+c)
for any
c\in\{1,\dots,p\}
.

(2)

Assume that
m\geq\lfloor\frac{n}{p}\rfloor
. Then we have

v_{p}\Big(\binom{m}{m-\lfloor\frac{n}{p}\rfloor}\Big)\geq D(m,n).

(3)

We have an equality

\binom{z}{m}\binom{z}{n}=\sum_{j\geq\max\{m,n\}}^{m+n}\binom{j}{j-m,j-n,m+n-j}\binom{z}{j},

where
\binom{j}{j-m,j-n,m+n-j}
is the generalized binomial coefficient;

(4)

For two nonnegative integers
s
and
t
such that
\max\{s,t\}\leq m\leq s+t
, we have

(A.7.1)

s-m+\Big\lfloor\frac{n}{p}\Big\rfloor+\max\Big\{t+v_{p}\Big(\frac{t!}{n!}\Big),0\Big\}+v_{p}\Big(\binom{m}{m-s,m-t,s+t-m}\Big)\geq D(m,n).

Proof.

(1) Let
m=\sum\limits_{i\geq 0}m_{i}p^{i}
and
m+1=\sum\limits_{i\geq 0}m_{i}^{\prime}p^{i}
be the
p
-adic expansion of
m
and
m+1
respectively. If we set
j=\max\{i\geq 0\,|\,m^{\prime}_{i}\neq 0\}
, we have

m_{i}^{\prime}=\begin{cases}0,&\text{~if~}i<j,\\
m_{j}+1,&\text{~if~}i=j,\\
m_{i},&\text{~if~}i>j.\end{cases}

So we have
m_{i}\geq m_{i}^{\prime}
for all
i\neq j
and hence
D(m+1,n)+1\geq D(m,n)
. The second inequality can be proved by a similar argument by considering the
p
-adic expansions of
n
and
n+c
;

(2) Let
m=\sum\limits_{i\geq 0}m_{i}p^{i}
and
n=\sum\limits_{i\geq 0}n_{i}p^{i}
be the
p
-adic expansions respectively. Then
\lfloor\frac{n}{p}\rfloor=\sum\limits_{i\geq 0}n_{i+1}p^{i}
is the
p
-adic expansion of
\lfloor\frac{n}{p}\rfloor
. The inequality follows from the well-known fact that
v_{p}\big(\binom{m}{m-\lfloor\frac{n}{p}\rfloor}\big)
is equal to the number of carries when adding
m-\lfloor\frac{n}{p}\rfloor
and
\lfloor\frac{n}{p}\rfloor
in base
p
;

(3) Without loss of generality, we can assume
m\geq n
. By a direct computation, we have
\binom{z}{m}\binom{z-m}{n}=\binom{m+n}{n}\binom{z}{m+n}
. Combining with equality
(3.5.3)
in
[
LWX17
]
, we get

\displaystyle\binom{z}{m}\binom{z}{n}=\binom{z}{m}\sum_{i=0}^{n}\binom{z-m}{n-i}\binom{m}{i}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\binom{m}{i}\binom{z}{m}\binom{z-m}{n-i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n}\binom{m}{i}\binom{m+n-i}{m}\binom{z}{m+n-i}{\mathrel{\to}^{j=m+n-i}}\sum_{j\geq\max\{m,n\}}^{m+n}\binom{j}{j-m,j-n,m+n-j}\binom{z}{j};

(4) By Lemma
A.5
(2),

t+v_{p}(\frac{t!}{n!})=0
when
t=\lfloor n/p\rfloor
. Hence

t+v_{p}\Big(\frac{t!}{n!}\Big)\ \begin{cases}\geq 0,&\text{~if~}t\geq\lfloor\frac{n}{p}\rfloor,\\
<0,&\text{~if~}t<\lfloor\frac{n}{p}\rfloor.\end{cases}

This suggests to divide our discussion into two cases:

(a)

When
t\geq\lfloor\frac{n}{p}\rfloor
(and hence
m\geq\lfloor\frac{n}{p}\rfloor
), it suffices to prove that

s+t-m+v_{p}\Big(\frac{t!}{\lfloor n/p\rfloor!}\Big)+v_{p}\Big(\binom{m}{m-s,m-t,s+t-m}\Big)\geq D(m,n).

This follows from the binomial identity

\frac{t!}{\lfloor n/p\rfloor!}\binom{m}{m-s,m-t,s+t-m}=\binom{m}{m-\lfloor n/p\rfloor}\binom{t}{m-s}\cdot\frac{(m-\lfloor n/p\rfloor)!}{(m-t)!},

and the inequalities
v_{p}\big(\binom{m}{m-\lfloor\frac{n}{p}\rfloor}\big)\geq D(m,n)
and
s+t-m\geq 0
;

(b)

When
t<\lfloor\frac{n}{p}\rfloor
, the inequality
(
A.7.1
) is equivalent to

(A.7.2)

s-m+\Big\lfloor\frac{n}{p}\Big\rfloor+v_{p}\Big(\binom{m}{m-s,m-t,s+t-m}\Big)\geq D(m,n).

Set
\ell:=\lfloor\frac{n}{p}\rfloor-t
and
n^{\prime}=n-p\ell
. Then
\lfloor\frac{n^{\prime}}{p}\rfloor=t
and we can apply case
(a)
to
m,n^{\prime},s
and
t
, and get the inequality

s+t-m+v_{p}\Big(\binom{m}{m-s,m-t,s+t-m}\Big)\geq D(m,n^{\prime}).

It then suffices to prove
D(m,n^{\prime})+\ell\geq D(m,n)
. But this follows from (1).∎

For the following, recall some definition from Notation
3.27
. Fix a character
\varepsilon=\omega^{s_{\varepsilon}}\times\omega^{a+s_{\varepsilon}}
.
For a positive integer
\lambda
, write
\deg\mathbf{e}_{\lambda}^{(\varepsilon)}=\lambda_{0}+p\lambda_{1}+\cdots
in its
p
-adic expansion. For a positive integer
n
,
\alpha\in\{0,\dots,p-1\}
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, define

D_{\leq\alpha}^{(\varepsilon)}(\underline{n},j):=\#\{\lambda\in\{1,\dots,n\}\,|\,\lambda_{j}\leq\alpha\}.

When
\alpha=0
, we write
D_{=0}^{(\varepsilon)}(\underline{n},j)
instead.

Lemma A.8
.

Fox a positive integer
n
. Write
\deg\mathbf{e}_{n}^{(\varepsilon)}=\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}p+\cdots
in its
p
-adic expansion.

(1)

For every
j\geq 0
, we have
D^{(\varepsilon)}_{=0}(\underline{n},j)\leq D^{(\varepsilon)}_{=0}(\underline{n},j+1)
.

(2)

If either
\alpha_{j}\neq 0
,
\alpha_{j+1}=p-1
or
\alpha_{j}=\alpha_{j+1}=0
, then
D^{(\varepsilon)}_{=0}(\underline{n},j)=D^{(\varepsilon)}_{=0}(\underline{n},j+1)
.

(3)

Assume
\alpha_{1}=p-1
. For any
\alpha\leq\alpha_{0}
, we have
D^{(\varepsilon)}_{\leq\alpha}(\underline{n},0)=D^{(\varepsilon)}_{\leq\alpha}(\underline{n},1)
.

Proof.

Let
\Omega
denote the set of nonnegative integers which are congruent to
s_{\varepsilon}
or
a+s_{\varepsilon}
modulo
p-1
. For every
j\geq 0
and
\alpha\in\{0,\dots,p-1\}
, we define

\Omega_{\leq\alpha}(j)=\{m\in\Omega\,|\,\text{the~}j\text{th digit in the~}p\text{-adic expansion of~}m\text{~is}\leq\alpha\}

and
\Omega_{\leq\alpha}(\underline{n},j)=\{m\in\Omega_{\leq\alpha}(j)\,|\,m\leq\deg\mathbf{e}_{n}\}
. Then we have
D_{\leq\alpha}(\underline{n},j)=\#\Omega_{\leq\alpha}(\underline{n},j)
. When
\alpha=0
we write
\Omega_{\leq 0}(j)=\Omega_{=0}(j)
and
\Omega_{\leq 0}(\underline{n},j)=\Omega_{=0}(\underline{n},0)
.

We define a bijection
\eta_{j}:\Omega_{=0}(j)\rightarrow\Omega_{=0}(j+1)
as follows. Write an element
m\in\Omega_{=0}(j)
in its
p
-adic expansion
m=m_{0}+m_{1}p+m_{2}p^{2}+\cdots
, define

\eta_{j}(m):=\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}m_{i}p^{i}+m_{j+1}p^{j}+\sum_{i\geq j+2}m_{i}p^{i}=m-m_{j+1}(p^{j+1}-p^{j}).

Since
\eta_{j}(m)\leq m
, the bijection
\eta_{j}
induces an injection
\Omega_{=0}(\underline{n},j)\rightarrow\Omega_{=0}(\underline{n},j+1)
, which implies that
D_{=0}(\underline{n},j)\leq D_{=0}(\underline{n},j+1)
. The equality holds if and only if for any
m\in\Omega_{=0}(j+1)
,
\eta_{j}(m)\leq\deg\mathbf{e}_{n}
implies
m\leq\deg\mathbf{e}_{n}
. The latter implication holds under either assumption of
(2)
. This proves
(1)
and
(2)
.

Under the assumption in
(3)
, it is straightforward to verify that the map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{\leq\alpha}(\underline{n},0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{\leq\alpha}(\underline{n},1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m=m_{0}+pm_{1}+p^{2}m_{2}+\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}:=m_{1}+pm_{0}+p^{2}m_{2}+\cdots}

is a bijection. So we have
D_{\leq\alpha}(\underline{n},0)=D_{\leq\alpha}(\underline{n},1)
.
∎

Appendix B

Errata for
[
LTXZ

24

]

We include an erratum for
[
LTXZ

24

]
here.

In
[
LTXZ

24

, Corollary 5.10]
, the claimed inequality (5.10.1) does not hold for
(\ell,\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime})=(0,1,1)
. We give the corrected statement in Proposition
2.22
of this paper.
